PREFACE 

The organization of social life and our social problems of today are so complicated and demand such expert knowledge, that no Catholic can take his rightful position as an intelligent citizen without pursuing some practical studies of these matters. At one time a liberal arts education was an assurance of adequate preparation for a life of usefulness in extending Christian principles to those around one. In our times, there is need for a much wider knowledge, if we are to understand the affairs of the world and to realize the possibilities of making practical application of Christian principles to the demands of our complex social life. These practical social studies cannot be relegated to future college courses, for this would be to deny necessary knowledge to the great majority of our students who do not complete a college career. Neither can social studies be relegated to schools for boys and young men alone, for now that women have the right to vote at the polls, they, too, must know something of the facts and principles involved in the problems of today.

The needs of the times were clearly realized by the late Pope Pius XI. In his encyclical Divini Redemptoris, published in March, 1937, we read: "It is of the utmost importance to foster in all classes of society an intensive program of social education adapted to the varying degrees of intellectual culture. It is necessary with all care and diligence to procure the widest possible diffusion of the teachings of the Church, even among the working classes."1 

'Pope Pius XI, Atheistic Communism (N.C.W.C. edition), p. 38. (Five Great 

Encyclicals, p. 197.)

vi 

PREFACE 

It is hoped that the present book will enable our Catholic highschool students, and also adults in study clubs and elsewhere, to learn the principles and problems of sociological studies within an adequate Catholic setting. A non-Catholic text is inappropriate for use by Catholic students if we are to carry out the desire expressed in the encyclical Inscrutabili Dei, by Pope Leo XIII, and emphasized by Pope Pius XI, viz.: "Greater stress must be laid .. on bringing into full conformity with the Catholic faith, what is taught in literature, in the sciences, and above all in philosophy."2 

The purpose of SOUND SOCIAL LIVING is not only to acquaint students with Christian social principles, and with an introduction to sociological theory, but also to integrate this theoretical study with a knowledge of actual conditions in the world today. At the end of the course they ought, indeed, to be able to carry out to the full the famous motto of the Jociste youth groups: to see, to judge, so that they may act. They will be accustomed to observe conditions around them. They will be acquainted with the social organization of the United States, as it exists today; they will know the machinery of government; they will be aware of the major practical problems of our times, and how social agencies, both private and public, are trying to deal with them. They will see. 

More than this, they will be accustomed to judge these conditions in the light of the teachings of the Church, and the ethical norms which should be standard in any Christian society. For this purpose full use has been made of the social encyclicals of Pope Leo XIII, of Pope Pius XI, and of our present Holy Father, Pope Pius XII. Gradually during the course, therefore, students will become accustomed to apply these norms, and to form judgments of conditions today. Theories and principles will then become for them part of the actual living of social life. Thus trained in habits of observation and sound judgment, based on a knowledge of Christian social principles, of sociological theory, and of actual

UNIT ONE  

We shall learn in this Unit that we are social beings by nature and that we have developed a human way of living (culture) which is a very important and interesting subject for study. What makes us what we are, and how culture is developed, will be discussed. We shall also learn what elements of culture are fundamental and unchangeable because they fill fundamental human needs. 1

Ewing Galloway

Man is social by nature. He has a natural aptitude for social life-in the family, in the Church, in the State, and in all the other social groups which he forms.

CHAPTER ONE  The purpose of this chapter is: 

1. To realize that man is a social being, and as such forms many groups and societies. 

2. To learn the difference between the deductive and the inductive method. 

3. To learn what sociology is about, and to see that although it makes most use of the inductive method, deduction and the truths of revelation must be taken into account by sociologists. 

4. To realize that every science takes certain facts for granted because they have been proved to be true in other branches of knowledge. 

5. To understand what truths we must, therefore, take for granted in the study of sociology, and why. 

Human beings need the help of others in order to live. 

It does not take much thought to realize that we need the help of others from the moment we are born until we are finally laid to rest in the grave. Let us consider how much we depend upon the help of others in a general way, not only upon the aid we receive from those now living, but also upon what generations past have left us.

As new-born babies, we could do absolutely nothing for ourselves. 

Our parents, or others, fed and clothed us, and attended to all our wants. Later on, had not brothers and sisters or others supplied our need of amusement and playmates, we should have been very lonely. Later still, we felt the need of the companionship of others outside our family. Only a small percentage of people are twins or triplets, and unless we belong to this small group, our brothers and sisters were either older or younger than ourselves, and did not 3

fully understand all our social wants; therefore we found friends among girls or boys of our own age. When we become adults, we shall probably find still other friends, some of whom will be older and some younger than ourselves. We shall probably visit one another in our homes, for conversation and games, take drives or go on hikes together, go to the theater, and in other ways enjoy one another's company.

A little more thought on this subject will convince us that our wants are supplied not only by those around us, but by many people whom we do not know at all but who are indirectly of great importance to us. For instance, our ready-made silk clothes have been secured through the cooperation of a series of groups of people whom we shall probably never know or even meet: the 

Japanese silkworm growers, the people who transported the silk by ship, rail, or truck, the spinners, the weavers and the dyers, the persons who cut out the clothes and those who sewed them together, and others who fixed on the buttons or embroidered them, and finally the wholesale dealers and the retail merchants who sold them. If our clothes are of cotton, it is probably Southern cotton growers who provided the original raw materials. If the clothes were sold to us nicely packaged, cardboard box manufacturers were needed, woodcutters and wood pulp makers, paper manufacturers, printers, string or ribbon makers, and people to prepare the raw material for the string or the ribbon. We can multiply examples from everyday life. Consider how, for instance, when taking a bus, we use among other things, at least to some extent, the steel and all the other goods, such as upholstery and rubber tires, which go to make it; the services of the people who manufactured it, and of the salesmen who sold it, as well as of the man who actually drives it; the gasoline and the oil which it uses; the paper for the tickets issued to passengers, and the services of the printer who printed them and of the makers of the ink he used in the printing.

We all belong to many social groups. 

Because we need the help of others in order to live, we have formed social groups of various kinds. Each of us is a member of a family, to which he is closely bound by ties of relationship and affection, and his feeling of intimate union with the members of his own family is entirely different from his regard for other families. However, we do not remain isolated within the family but we are members of numerous other social groups. We are enrolled in a school or study group, for example, and although this group is entirely distinct from our family circles, we nevertheless feel that we belong to it, too, in a special manner. We have also our own particular group of playmates or companions, toward whom we likewise feel a bond of comradeship, affection, and interest. We are members of various other societies as, for example, a parish sodality, or a corporation for which we work, or a club or association such as the Boy Scouts, or a cooperative society. We all belong to a 

Church, and have a special regard, first for our priests and the members of the parish, and then for the whole Church of which our parish forms but one unit. Again, we feel in a group apart from those who belong to other religious bodies. 

Then, too, each of us lives in a village or in a small town, or in a certain district of a large city. We feel a sense of loyalty toward the other residents in our neighborhood, and we have a pride in our district or a desire to improve it. We have our local town hall or municipal building, our own police court and prison, our local hospital, schools, churches, stores, factories, and workshops. This larger group is called in sociology our civic community or neighborhood group.

Many such groups united make up a local political division, usually called a county or parish and these, in turn, form a state such as California, Illinois, or Pennsylvania. All these states, united, form the United States of America. Our country, with its people, taken as a whole is also called a State, and it is in this general sense that the

word is commonly used in sociology. We have feelings of loyalty for our country; we are anxious to uphold its laws; and we wish others to conform to our ideals.

Now, it must be noted that our State is but one national unit in the world, where there are many States and many peoples, all of whom are essentially like ourselves, whatever be the accidental differences of customs, race, color, or language. Can you name two such states which border on our own, and tell of one custom in each which differs from ours?

Man is social by nature. This fact is proved by the fact that he has a need of social life, a natural inclination toward it and a natural aptitude for it. 

We have just shown that man needs the help of others, and naturally tends to form groups. This tendency toward association with others and dependency upon them is what is meant by man's social nature. Man cannot normally live alone. Common sense as well as history makes this evident. 

We can prove that man is social by nature, therefore, first of all by the fact that he needs social life. A second proof is that we also have an inborn inclination to live in society. To be truly happy we need to associate with others. Normal people enjoy life best when they are with congenial companions, and they are unhappy if left in isolation. Even when circumstances isolate a man, he does not live truly alone, for as a substitute for actual companionship he has the memory of former times spent with men, or he receives the ideas of others through books, the radio, or other means. It should not be difficult to realize that we have indeed a propensity or natural inclination for social life.

Besides our inclination for society and our actual need of social life, a third proof of our social nature is that we have an obvious aptitude for living with others. Evidence of this aptitude lies first in the fact that everyone, even the lowest savage, has the gift of speech, which enables him to exchange his ideas with others: a necessary condition of social life. Second, only through social inter-

Department of Recreation, Sioux City, lowa Man is social by nature. Normal people enjoy life best when they are with congenial companions.

course can man fully develop his intellectual, moral, and physical powers. Indeed, all the finest qualities of man, such as kindness, charity, unselfishness, and true love, are developed by social life. 

Man's social nature is therefore proved by his aptitude for social life, his inclination for it, and his need of it. We might sum up what we have written thus far in the words of the writer and artist Eric Gill, who shows that man is a "social person" as follows: "1. Because physically (here, in this temporary place) there are two kinds of men - men and women. "2. Because the reason of having men and women is children. 

Social means living in society. The first kind of society is the family (father, mother, and children). "3. Because people are not exactly like one another. Therefore some are good at one thing and some at another. Some make good fighters, some make good builders or good farmers, some make good merchants, some make good craftsmen, some make good teachers, some make good entertainers - funny men and artists of all sorts. "Therefore men like to live in groups where there is something of everything."

Sociology is one of the social sciences. 

We have shown by various examples that man lives in society and is a social being by nature. Before we can discuss what sociology itself is, we must secure some notion of what is meant by a social science, for sociology is one of the social sciences. 

There are various methods of attaining truth by human means. 

Broadly speaking, they may be divided into the philosophic method and the scientific method. Philosophers chiefly use the method of deduction, which is called the philosophic method, and they use induction to only a very limited, though, of course, to a necessary extent. By philosophy we chiefly study the causes of things and then 'Eric Gill, Unemployment, p. 3

deduce from the cause what might logically be considered to be the effect of that cause.

The other method is called the scientific or inductive method. 

Experimental scientists chiefly use induction, and make but very limited use of the deductive method. They collect all the data they can find about a thing, describe these facts, analyze and classify them, and from them draw (induce) certain conclusions concerning them. In other words, they classify facts and, in so far as they are able, they go from the effect to the cause. The scientific method is, however, incomplete, and can only reach the cause indirectly. 

Sociology is one of a group of sciences called social sciences. The social sciences all deal with man in his many and complex social relations with others. They include sociology, economics, history, political science (government and international relations), social psychology, anthropology, and demography. They are called sciences because in each of these subjects the scientific method is the chief method used. Definite facts are observed, analyzed, and recorded. 

Yet these social sciences are not of the same exact nature as, for example, mathematics. They deal with man, and one cannot have a complete knowledge of man without using the deductive method to a greater extent than the other sciences, for a more exact knowledge of man's nature. The scientific method can classify man exteriorly, and find many exact facts about him, but there are four reasons why social sciences must use the philosophic method to come to any exact conclusions about man and his nature, and must also take into account revealed facts as distinct from observed facts. 

First, man cannot be wholly observed in the way we can observe chemicals and their reactions. Second, man has a mind and a will (which are powers of his soul). These are nonmaterial (not made of matter), hence their internal action cannot be wholly known. Third, man also has a nonmaterial (supernatural) life of grace which cannot be observed by scientific experiment. He is, therefore, not known solely by his external actions and works. Fourth, man belongs to a fallen race, and therefore some of the social practices which we may

observe are not in accordance with his true nature, as originally intended by God (as we shall shortly explain). Sociology, therefore, being a social science, sociologists must make use of the knowledge gained by philosophy and revelation when they interpret the facts about society which they discover by observation. 

Sociology is the science of society, and studies human society, its customs, institutions, and the processes whereby man has arrived at these.

Sociology is the science of society. It is one of the social sciences and deals specifically with human society, its customs, institutions, and the processes whereby man has arrived at these. By a study of sociology we learn how man has come to form the social groups to which he belongs, the extent to which he is influenced by these groups, and the reasons why he is influenced by them. For example, sociologists have analyzed society and have come to the conclusion that certain groups, such as the family and the State, are essential for man's well-being, whereas others, as, for example, the different kinds of play groups, are useful but not essential. Society or group life in general, and social groups in particular, affect us very considerably, as we shall learn in Chapters Two and Three. Sociology analyzes the various societies to which we belong: the family, the neighborhood, the school, the play group, and others, and it discovers not only the distinguishing characteristics of each of them, but also the manner in which these and all other social groups and social influences affect us. Let us ask ourselves why people wear fashionable clothes, even when these clothes may look ugly or hurt the wearer; or why we have so many social customs of etiquette, such as shaking hands, using certain types of table implements. 

The answer is: Because we have developed certain customs out of habit or imitation.

It is most fascinating to study our social life: to learn about the groups we form because they are essential to our nature and seem to be ordained for us by God, and the groups we form merely out of a desire to live a social life; to discuss our many social customs,

some of which are found all over the world, others only in certain societies; to study the way in which we can affect others, and the way by which others affect us, sometimes for good, and sometimes for evil by leading us to do actions which we would rather not do. 

Sociology is a vital study. In theoretical sociology we study how people lived socially in the past, and how they live in the present, and by taking these facts in conjunction with the truths of religion, philosophy, and other sciences about man, we form theories about social life. In applied or practical sociology we examine the various ways in which social life is unsatisfactory according to the theories studied in theoretical sociology; the causes for the problems which have arisen, and the means of remedying them. In accordance with our social theories.

There are many facts to be learned in sociology, and many theories about society which any intelligent person ought to know. 

Every intelligent person wishes to understand the life around him, to know what is wrong with it, and to study the various means proposed for its improvement. The Catholic especially will be glad to learn how he can understand social life according to God's plan, and how he can improve bad conditions according to the standards of Christian social principles. Christian sociologists not only study the manner in which man actually lives, or has lived in the past, but they compare these factual studies with the standards set by Christianity for social living and form conclusions about man's possible deviations from these standards.

Before beginning a study of sociology, one must take certain truths for granted.

Because sociology is a social science, the most important part of its study is man. Before we can interpret the facts gained from observation about man's social relations and form correct theories regarding them, we must understand certain facts about him. These facts are either self-evident or they have already been satisfactorily proved by philosophy or revelation. They are, therefore, truths and can in no wise be considered mere theories or hypotheses.

The chief postulates of sociology, that is, the chief facts or truths which we must take for granted, are: 

1. God exists and created all things, including man. 

2. Man has a spiritual soul which is immortal; hence he has an eternal destiny, and it is more important that he should attain this than that he should be happy upon earth. 

3. Man is endowed with a free will and hence his conduct is not always predictable. 

4. Man is subject not only to physical laws, but also to the moral law. Whenever man forgets the moral law in his social relations disharmony occurs to a greater or lesser extent, and social problems arise. 

5. Man has certain rights and duties, and these are common to all mankind. By reason of man's nature and his inherent dignity as a creature of God, redeemed by Christ and intended for an eternal destiny in heaven, every man has a right to life, liberty, justice, truth, social life with directive authority, the ownership of property, and the right to perform his duties to his Creator. Every man, in justice, has a duty to respect the same rights in others. More than this, Christians recognize a "law of charity." When we see a person who is unable to secure his natural rights, Christian charity would lead us to supply what is lacking, to the best of our ability, provided that in so doing we do not fail to carry out any of our duties. 

6. Christ, the Son of God, established the Church to which He gave authority to guide men in matters related to their supernatural destiny.

A brief discussion of the reason why Christian sociologists postulate these facts will be found in the Appendix. One cannot understand the true meaning of social life without a consideration of them. Whenever man in his social life fails to live up to the social principles of Christianity, his social life becomes faulty. Whenever we find unsatisfactory social conditions, or whenever any social problem exists, we shall find that one of the major causes of the situation is the disregard of some or all of the persons concerned

for the true social standards of Christianity. No real solution for our social problems can ever be arrived at if we do not take Christian social principles into practical application. 

Can you use correctly the following words? neighborhood group social nature inclination science postulate deduction moral law induction right aptitude nonmaterial duty sociology gregarious social principles social science fact theory philosophy

1. List ten necessities for which you consider that you depend upon others. 

2. If you had to depend upon the local production of goods to supply your material wants, name five items of clothing which you or your companions now wear, which you would have to do without. 

3. Name the different types of workers who cooperated in making this book. 

4. Name the chief social groups to which you belong. 

5. Give three proofs that man is social by nature. 

6. What is meant by the expression: "Man has an aptitude for social life"? Give three examples to prove that the statement is true. 

7. What is the difference between philosophy and a science? 

8. What are the social sciences? How do they differ from natural sciences? 

9. Show how it is difficult to experiment in connection with man's social life. 

10. What is sociology? What is the difference between theoretical and applied sociology? 

11. Why would it be unsatisfactory for a Catholic sociologist merely to observe man's social life and to draw conclusions from these observations? 

12. Why would it be foolish in any scientific study not to take for granted certain truths already proved in other studies? 

13. Do we have to consider every kind of truth already discovered, before we begin any new study? Why or why not?

14. What are the truths about man which the Catholic sociologist takes for granted when discussing man's social life? 

15. Prove by five concrete examples that there are no rights without duties. 

16. Could there be a satisfactory explanation of the pain and suffering endured by some people if man's soul were not immortal? 

1. Prepare a short skit with three characters in it (for presentation to the class after Chapters One, Two, and Three have been studied): 

A: who asserts that he is free to do as he pleases, and that he need obey no one because he has free will.

B: who asserts that man has no free will because he is wholly influenced by habits which come from environment. 

C: who rightfully expresses his opinion, and makes the others see the truth of his statements: that true liberty consists in ordered restraint; that man is subject to the moral law, and to the human positive law (when this is in accord with the moral law), but that nevertheless he is free to make a choice between obeying and disobeying the law. 

2. Begin to prepare a survey of the social organization of your community, and of the social agencies (public and private) which it includes.

First, procure a copy of Colcord, J. C., Your Community (New 

York: Russell Sage Foundation, 1939). Study pages 15, 16, 17, as well as the chapter headings of the whole book. 

Second, compile a list of government departments and both public and private social agencies about which you will need to procure information. Consult the Projects and Activities section of each of the chapters of this book, to secure further ideas about needed information. Make a chart of your city, town, or county government, following page 15 of Colcord as your model, so as to have a clearer picture of the topics to be understood.

Third, limit the departments or agencies about which you desire information, to a reasonable number. Then divide the class into small committee groups making each committee responsible for securing the details about one or more of these topics, for example: 

The organization and work of the local government; the court system; protective services such as police system, fire protection, public

safety; health services; park and playground facilities; museums; libraries; public welfare (care of the poor); public works (for the unemployed); the inspection of weights and measures, factory inspection; the school system, etc.

Fourth, discuss in class the way in which each committee will best secure its information (e.g., by direct visit, by letter, by consulting a local library or chamber of commerce, by asking questions at home). 

Each committee must then have a final consultation with the instructor, who will state exactly what must be done. To avoid difficulties and any unintentional criticism of the school, students must be careful to follow the directions of the instructor in every particular. If a personal visit is to be made, the wishes of the instructor must be followed as regards the person to be seen, the time of the visit, and the manner of approach; if a letter is to be written, the instructor should see it so that it will be free from errors, directed in a correct manner, and that a stamped, self-addressed envelope is enclosed, when approaching a private individual or agency.

If the school authorities permit students to visit outside agencies in groups, the class might also discuss at this time some of the social agencies they would like to visit, if this can be later arranged. 

These initial discussions may be spread over part of the time for several days or weeks. Generally speaking, some of the reports ought to be ready when Chapters Five or Seven are discussed, and after this they should be presented at the time the appropriate chapter is studied. 

3. Let each student begin a scrapbook (or let the class arrange to prepare a joint scrapbook) which will include newspaper and magazine clippings and other suitable articles and pictures to illustrate the information given in each of the chapters of this book. Care must be taken that students do not cut up newspapers, magazines, or other printed material without the permission of their owner; no library copies should ever be cut up without the express permission of the librarian, because usually such copies are needed for reference in future years.

Attractively produced scrapbooks would be valuable library material for students in future years, if they were left in the school for this purpose. Students can help to improve the school facilities in this way.

If a photography club exists in the school, photographs of the work of local agencies may be secured. 

4. Make a list of the agencies which send films for school use, either

free or at a nominal charge. Discuss whether it will be possible to have some of these films shown in your school as the various topics are discussed.

Chapter Two  The purpose of this chapter is: 

1. To learn what we receive from heredity. 

2. To show how our nonmaterial nature cannot be inherited, although it is influenced by inheritance. 

3. To show the important function of the soul and its faculties. 

Man is influenced by a variety of complex factors. 

Since sociology deals with social relations, it is intimately concerned with man's individual character and temperament. After all, man is the most important unit in group life, and his social usefulness, his moral conduct, health, occupations, are all affected by his character and disposition. These latter are, in turn, due to a variety of complex factors. It will be well, therefore, to study man a little in relation to these facts.

All men are equal in the sense that all are composed of a body and soul made to God's own image and likeness, that all are descended from Adam and Eve and have been redeemed by Jesus Christ, and as human beings all have certain equal rights and duties, as mentioned in the previous chapter. In every other respect, however, each individual is different from his fellow men. Probably all of us have wondered at some time or other exactly what it is that makes us what we are! What makes our character and our conduct? Some would explain the differences in human beings chiefly as the results of heredity; while others attribute them to the effect of environment, particularly economic and geographic environment. 

There is much truth in both opinions, and a consideration of 17

them is the subject of this and the following chapter. In general, a sane viewpoint is that which does not unduly stress either one factor, but co-ordinates the whole, recognizing that man is influenced by a mixture of both heredity and environment. 

We inherit our human body with its functions and its instincts. 

Heredity means the potentialities which we receive from our forefathers at birth. It includes many complex factors. We inherit a definite bodily form and function which is common to all the members of the human race. We all have two eyes, set in approximately the same position on our faces; each of us has one nose, not several; two arms, two legs, and other physical characteristics. 

We are always recognizable as human beings, therefore, distinct from the other animal creation - whether we be tall or short, stout or thin, light or dark complexioned, Russian, Irish, French, African, Chinese, German, Canadian, or North American Indian.

With our body we inherit certain functions. We are equipped with the power of locomotion, the ability to walk about. We inherit a nervous system which provides us with a capacity to learn and profit by experience, so that by frequent repetition of the same pleasant and unpleasant sensations very much of our behavior becomes habitual, though to a certain extent only, as we shall later see. We also inherit certain inborn abilities which express themselves in behavior which we do not have to learn, but which comes naturally to us, such as breathing, sucking, blinking, the digestive functions, the taking of food and drink when hungry and thirsty, and similar processes. We inherit, too, the exterior senses of sight, hearing, taste, smell, and touch; and such interior senses as the ability to revive sensations (the imagination), and the memory, which depends upon associations of ideas about the things which come to us through one or other of the senses. 

We inherit also a gregarious instinct and a sex instinct, that is: a desire for the companionship of others and an urge to mate. These instincts are given to us to insure the propagation of the race, the

necessary care of children during their long period of adolescence, and our general social well-being. Then, too, we obtain from our physical inheritance some ductless glands, such as the thyroid and the adrenals. These glands send substances directly into the blood stream when they are aroused, and as the blood stream is pumped up to the brain, the substances affect it, and through the brain they affect our mind (which works through the medium of the brain) and the nervous system, and so produce our emotions. We inherit, therefore, the ability to feel anger, fear, and pleasure. 

We inherit only potentialities, and these potentialities may never develop.

Not everything that we inherit appears at birth. Some of our inherited capacities never appear, and some of them we may lose. 

We may inherit a potentiality to have laughing brown eyes, a Roman nose, and to grow to the height of six feet. For a long time after we are born, however, our eyes may be blue, they may stare rather fixedly, our nose may be a very small buttonlike affair, and we shall certainly be less than six feet tall! Despite our inheritance, our nose may remain buttonlike throughout life, for perhaps we shall fall on it and put it out of joint at a very early age. We may be undernourished in our youth, and never attain our inherited capacity to grow to a height of six feet. We may develop a disagreeable disposition so that our eyes will show a sulky quality and not twinkle in merriment at all. We may inherit an extraordinarily fine sense of touch, or an extremely keen sense of smell, yet these special qualities will not be noticed or even fully developed until we have lived for many years. Again, we may have the misfortune to lose our hands in an accident, so that our superior inheritance of the sense of touch may never be known at all.

Myopia (nearsightedness), albinism, deaf-mutism, and color blindness are inherited defects. We may inherit a good or a bad physique or constitution. Mental deficiency is sometimes caused by an inherited defect, but it may also be due to environment; for

example, a blow or a fall on the head may cause it, or malnutrition.

Diseases are not inherited, although we may inherit a susceptibility to certain diseases, just as we may inherit a special capacity for resisting other infections. A child may inherit weak lungs and a predisposition to tuberculosis because he has tuberculous parents. If he lives with these parents from birth, in all likelihood he himself will develop the disease. On the other hand, if the parents take great care to avoid spreading germs, he may escape the disease, and he might also be prevented from developing tuberculosis if he were separated from his parents rather early. Some people rarely take cold, while others seem to be particularly susceptible to cold germs. 

Some people seem to inherit a bad chemical reaction to certain things (an allergy); for example, they become indisposed if they eat strawberries, or sleep on a feather pillow, whereas other people do not appear to inherit any predispositions to allergic reactions at all.

Some of the things which we inherit never show in us, because biologists have discovered that we have certain recessive (hidden) characteristics. If one parent has pure brown eyes (with no inherited blueness as a recessive) and if the other has blue eyes, biologists have discovered that all the children will have brown eyes, but that about half of the children will have inherited a hidden recessive characteristic for blue eyes. The hidden blue "recessive" will never show in these children, but if one of the brown-eyed children with recessive blue marries a person with blue eyes, instead of all the children having brown eyes, about half of them will have blue eyes. 

The other half will have brown eyes with, however, a recessive for blueness which will be handed down to the next generation. Parents are never sure, therefore, what "recessive" inherited qualities they may be passing on to their children, for some of the capacities which they inherited will not have shown in themselves. 

Take deafness as one final example. Deafness, except that which is due simply to accident or illness, is usually inherited. One might think, therefore, that the children of deaf parents would be deaf;

but often they are not. The explanation is simple. There are many kinds of deafness, and deafness, if inherited, is a "recessive" characteristic. If a person who is deaf through a defect in the middle ear, marries someone who is deaf because of an inherited defect in the eardrum, the children will probably have a good sense of hearing, but they will probably inherit two types of "recessive" characteristics for deafness. If a person with hidden inherited defects of this type marries someone with good hearing, or with inherited deafness of a third type, again the children will probably be of normal hearing, and will not show the inherited defect. Normally speaking, only if such a person marries someone with exactly the same type or types of inherited deafness, actual or "recessive," would their children show the family's defective inheritance. 

Man is dual by nature. That is to say, he has a material nature which he inherits. He also has an immaterial side which could not be inherited, because it is nonmaterial. This nonmaterial side of man forms his vital principle (his soul) with its two faculties of intellect and will.

The inheritance which we have just described is but one part of us. It includes our vegetative and sensitive life, and our ability to move, and it is very similar to that of animals, although it differs somewhat in form. Man, however, is not a mere animal: he is a rational being. That is to say, he has a human soul which is present in every part of his body, and provides him with his strictly human faculties of intellect and will. Each man's soul is an active, spiritual essence which co-ordinates all his functions. Since the soul, with its faculties of mind and will, is nonmaterial by nature, for we cannot see or touch it, it cannot be transmitted by physical inheritance, for physical inheritance is the inheritance of material substance. Like most of man's inherited endowments, this spiritual, noninherited part of man develops its powers gradually, until it reaches its highest capacity in adult life.

III 

​BB AA 2:5 AB AB AA 

AB 

BB AB AA AA AA AA AB An example of Inherited Characteristics: Let BB = deafness; AA= good hearing; AB good hearing with a recessive characteristic for deafness. 

I. Two people with good hearing marry, but one has inherited a defective gene for deafness, although it is not apparent. II. All their children will have good hearing but about half will have a recessive characteristic for deafness hidden in their germ plasm. III. If one of the children with this recessive characteristic marries someone with a similar recessive defect, out of four offspring, generally speaking, one will have perfect hearing and will never pass on a gene deficiency for deafness; two will have good hearing, with the hidden defect; one will be deaf. This result was shown by the monk Mendel (1822-1884) to be a law of nature; now called Mendel's Law. 

Although the soul is not inherited, it works through the body and so is influenced by heredity.

It is important to note that although the mind and the will are. not inherited, these faculties of the soul work through our body and hence are influenced by our bodily inheritance. At birth our minds are blank. We know nothing. Everything that is in our mind comes to us through the senses (which we inherit), that is, we have heard, or seen, or touched, or smelled, or tasted something, and the sensations produced have gone through our nervous system (which is also inherited) into our inherited physical brain, and may be worked upon by our imagination, too, and influenced by our emotions

(which are largely affected by our inherited nervous system and our inherited ductless glands). What comes into our mind, therefore, depends in large measure upon our inherited capabilities. We do not have any normal mental concept of the color red, for instance, if we are born with a defective sense of sight so that we are color blind, and never see the color. Similarly, if we inherit a weak nervous system, our will may be very weak. The predisposition which we all have by reason of original sin, to at least some of the seven "deadly sins," is due either to an inordinate regard for ourselves as persons, or to a defective inheritance of some kind, or to a mixture of the two. For example, an overdeveloped sense of taste may lead us to gluttony; an overdeveloped sex instinct to sins connected with sex; defective adrenal glands to frequent fits of anger. 

With the right type of education and the will to improve, and with the help of grace, we can conquer our bad tendencies to a large extent, or we can develop the bad and stifle our good qualities. St. 

Francis of Sales, for example, is said to have had a strong predisposition to anger, but he fought against it with such success as to become famed for his meekness. Even the worst of criminals had many good qualities before he allowed them to be overcome by his antisocial tendencies.

It is evident, therefore, that before marriage young people should consider the situation seriously, if there seems a danger that they may pass on very bad inherited tendencies to their offspring. It is true, of course, that love, without much consideration of anything else, is the dominant element in the choice of a mate; yet we have been endowed with the intelligence to foresee and forestall possible bad results due to inheritance. If there is a likelihood of passing on a very bad physical defect, or a mental defect caused by physical inheritance, persons who intend to marry should consult their physician. In general, though, it must be said that we still know too little about inheritance to be very accurate in predicting what the children of a married couple will be like.

Chapter Three ENVIRONMENT, CHARACTER, AND CONDUCT The purpose of this chapter is: 

1. To discuss the various types of environment. 

2. To realize the value of forming correct habits. 

3. To come to a true understanding of the respective place of heredity and environment in the making of character and conduct. 

Environment is any external force which influences us as individuals, and it is very important. 

In the preceding chapter we began a consideration of what makes us what we are: what makes our character and our conduct. We showed the important basis which is furnished us by heredity, and we also showed the importance of the working of the nonmaterial faculties of the soul, the mind, and the will. We said, however, that there is nothing in our mind which does not come to us first by environment, by means of one or other of the five senses. Moreover, practically everything we do is the result of imitating our environment or having been trained by individuals around us, who also constitute part of our environment.

Environment is any external force which influences us as individuals. It is the whole of our surroundings: the persons, objects, ideas, and all other external impressions which affect us from the very moment of our birth. There are three types of environment: 

First, there is physical environment, which includes geographical features, such as beautiful scenery or ugly views, and which also includes buildings, furniture, color schemes, and the like. The food, drink, and drugs we may take are also parts of physical environment. Part of this environment is supplied by nature, and part of it 27

is made by man and pertains to the group in which we live. Second, there is psychosocial environment, which is the name given to the influence of all the people around us. Finally, there is psychic environment, the environment of expressed ideas, sounds, and other psychic things which affect our mind and nervous system. 

To a very large extent, all types of environment influence us unconsciously. The imitative capacity of a small child is very marked. 

For this reason children who have adequate attention paid to them from birth, who are played with and helped in their attempts at imitation, are usually more intelligent than children who are left too much alone for their inherited intellectual endowment to be developed to the full. Rather rapidly the child either trains itself by imitation, or is trained by others, in a surprising number of skills. It learns by unpleasant sense experiences what to avoid, and by pleasant sensations what things are enjoyable. Within a few years it can talk, walk, feed, and dress itself - by habit. 

Geographic environment is important, but less important than environment of other kinds.

There is a school of thought which places undue emphasis on the influence of geographic environment (a part of our physical environment). Adherents of this school of thought are called geographic determinists. They say that man's social life is almost entirely determined by his geographical environment, and that human energy and achievement, sex relations, religion, occupations, thought, art, crime, character, are all profoundly affected by locality, climate, and other geographical factors. There is, of course, some truth in their statements. People living in hot climates tend to be lethargic and noninventive, for nature provides them with an easy livelihood. Very cold climates give their inhabitants a hard struggle for existence and little time or ambition for cultural pursuits. Those living in isolated districts, in deserts, or on mountainous slopes, lack the incentive of social intercourse with others, and are rarely known for great achievements. Temperate climates are most favorable to

man's development, for he has to work hard to earn a livelihood, and yet not so hard that he has no leisure for other things. Climate affects our moods and nerves. Great heat is irritating and enervating; a dull day may make us sad and discontented, a bright one fills us with joy. Geographical environment, however, in no way dominates human life, for a well-trained character can easily enable us to rise above it, at least to a large extent. Temperature certainly does not appear to have the influence often imputed to it, for despite the torrid climate of their country, we have ample proofs of the high degree of civilization attained by the early Sumerians, Egyptians, Indians, and others; nor does climate appear to affect the achievements of those who inhabit cities in the United States with high summer temperatures and great humidity. Moreover, geographical influence is being rapidly decreased by improvements in room heating and cooling, and in transportation and communication - by railroads, airplanes, wireless, ocean vessels, newspapers, and the spread of literature.

Economic environment has an influence on man, but this influence is not insurmountable.

Some people are brought up in an atmosphere of luxury. Others belong to good, middle-class homes of varying degrees of comfort or luxury. Others, again, come from decent working-class homes where there are few comforts, but where, nevertheless, there is a sufficiency of food, clothing, and recreation, and where the neighborhood is clean and decent. But many people, even in these civilized times, live in dirty tenements and hovels, with bad sanitation, lack of fresh air, and under crowded conditions which lend themselves easily to immorality and other vices, as well as to ill-health and general feelings of despondency. 

The ideal economic environment is undoubtedly that of the middle-class household living in the country or in the suburbs of a city, where there is good air and adequate sanitation, food, clothing, recreational and educational facilities, and sufficient room space in

Ewing Galloway

An Eskimo family at dinner in their home. The diet of the Eskimo is made up largely of meat and fat to provide warmth against the climate. His clothes, too, are suited to the intense cold of the north. the home. Children living under such conditions generally lead wholesome, healthful lives, and develop good vitality and physique and alertness of mind. On the other hand, the children of the rich are sometimes pampered, and may become selfish, lacking in restraint, and given to excessive indulgence in pleasure in an attempt to overcome the boredom resulting from lack of useful occupation. 

Those brought up in an extremely poor economic environment are also likely to be affected by it. Malnutrition and lack of adequate health facilities often result in physical ills. The depressing effect of destitution may lead a child to such evils as stealing, lying, envy, hatred, and lack of ambition. As a result of insufficient economic resources, a person may never have the opportunity to rise out of his condition, and may always have to remain in poorly paid positions even though his character and mental capacity may be well fitted for a much better type of work. 

The socialists and communists have recognized the influence of

economic environment, but they have exaggerated its significance. 

They attribute all the world's happenings, and all social institutions, even religious and moral, to the struggle to earn a living, and they say that material well-being, that is, having plenty of money and a good economic environment, is of the greatest moment in life, to the exclusion of all other factors. Of course, this idea considers material goods more important than nonmaterial and spiritual things, and this is false. Their theory, that all events and social institutions have their rise in economic causes is called economic determinism or the materialistic conception of history. It is inaccurate because although economic environment does indeed affect people, as we have just shown, yet poor environment is not always harmful, nor are affluent circumstances always beneficial. Man's own nature, his good and his bad qualities, external factors such as overpopulation and famine, and especially man's attitudes and ideals gained from his philosophy of life, have been more frequent causes of historical events and the development of our social life than any economic factor.

Poverty is no disgrace, of course, if it is not due to laziness or other character defect. A poor family in which the children are brought up with parental love and care, will be a greater asset to a nation than a careless, pleasure-loving family. Poverty may act as a powerful incentive to the development of one's capacities, gifts, and virtues. In general, however, any civilized community wishes to do everything possible so to organize social life that the majority of its members have at least a moderate amount of economic resources. 

Psychosocial environment has an enormous influence upon man. 

Psychosocial environment is the social environment of all the people around us our family, our neighborhood friends, the people we meet in school, at play, in offices, workshops, stores. 

These people influence our senses, our nervous system, our emotions, our thoughts, attitudes, and ideals. We imitate their manner of walk, their dress, their actions, mannerisms, and affectations.

GS Acme A very poor section in one of our large cities. The dirty tenements and crowded, filthy streets are breeders of vice and ill-health. 

Often we are consciously trained by them to certain types of habitual action. This environment is called psychosocial because it is both the people around us and their attitudes, ideals, and ideas which affect us, and they affect our mentality as well as tend toward inducing in us physical habits of various types. 

As most people spend the greater part of their days, and often the whole of their waking hours, in the company of others, it must be obvious that we are greatly influenced by the social groups with which we are associated. As the proverbs put it: "Tell me your companions, and I shall tell you what manner of man you are," and "Birds of a feather flock together." If a person's companions are socialists, he will probably come to regard their teachings as true, even though at the outset he disagreed with them. If a person's companions regard gay parties and the latest fashions as essential to happiness, ignoring such higher human activities as intelligent reading and discussion, it is very probable that he will come to act

Acme A middle-class section in Salem, Massachusetts. Children living in neighborhoods such as this have opportunity to live wholesome, healthful lives. and think as they do. Innumerable examples of the influence of psychosocial environment might be cited. Man is imitative by nature, and psychosocial environment is so important that he usually tends to conform his conduct to that of the social environment in which he lives.

Psychic environment also has an important influence upon man, especially in forming attitudes and ideals. 

Psychic environment is the influence exerted on our mind and will, not only by the attitudes, ideals, and ideas of people around us, by public opinion and convention, but also by the literature we read, the music we hear, the radio, theatrical performances, art, religious ceremonies and all the other indefinable and often unnoticed forces which play constantly upon our mind. 

The ideals and attitudes set up by psychic environment are espe-

cially important. Ideals are types of excellence, good or bad, which we hope to attain. They are undoubtedly of great influence as the motive power behind our thoughts and habits. Attitudes are certain ways of viewing things, gained from our environment, but changed by the working of our mind and our imagination. 

Not all the social groups to which a person belongs have the same ideals and attitudes. In adult life, a person may usually choose his surroundings and the groups in which he wishes to move; or, if he be forced to live in an environment with ideals and attitudes different from the ones he has already formed by long-established association and habit, he may not be greatly influenced by it. A younger person may have serious conflicts when the psychic environment of groups to which he belongs differs. He may have moral conflicts, when his impulses and emotions are not in accord with his conscience or the standards of his environment, and other conflicts may arise from the differing social groups of which he is a member. The habits which his parents try to instill in him and the psychic environment of his home may be quite different from the habits and ideals of his companions at play or in the school. If he has good parents, they are not likely to permit him to have the wrong kind of companions, literature, or pastimes; but not all parents are as careful in these matters as they should be, or their children may hide from them certain facts about their companions which the parents should know. Some parents may themselves have the wrong attitudes and ideals. Again, parents who have been brought up in an environment different from that to which the children belong outside the home, may be unable to appreciate the good qualities of the latter or to understand changed modern conditions. This situation was quite common a few years ago, when large numbers of non- English-speaking immigrants came to America from countries whose way of living differed radically from our own. Conflicts of this type often have a harmful effect on younger persons, and may lead to delinquency and crime.

. 

Character is individual and peculiar to each person. It is expressed in conduct which is either automatic (habitual) or willed. 

Character is the expression of ourselves as a complete entity. It is the sum total of our heredity, of the informing presence of the soul with its faculties in every part of our being, and of all the changing roles of environment to which we have been subjected from birth onwards.

Character expresses itself in conduct, and human conduct is of two kinds: willed, and automatic or habitual. Much of our conduct is due to habit and is performed without reflection, first, because of the tendency of human beings to imitate those around them; second, because of habits formed either by the training which we have given to ourselves, or that which others have effected in us. There is much value in this automatic conduct, for by it the mind is released for more important decisions, and for abstract and constructive thinking.

For example, if every time we put on our socks or stockings, we had to think: "First I must take up my sock, now I must raise my right foot, now I must put my toes in the opening at the top, and carefully draw the sock up my leg . . ." it would certainly take a much longer time to dress ourselves than we actually spend in the operation (because we have formed a habit of dressing almost without giving a thought to it). Moreover, had we to concentrate on every movement we could not think the thoughts we might now indulge in while performing all our intricate dressing operations in an habitual manner. 

Yet although habit is a very important factor in our conduct, it by no means rules all our thoughts and actions. We are not merely cleverly trained animals! Our mind is continually working, continually taking in new impressions from the senses, and in this way new thoughts are acquired, we try new skills, and changed attitudes and ideals are formed in our mind. Some of these new ideas and skills, in their turn become habitual. A little reflection will show,

however, that there is indeed a second type of conduct which is more specifically human than imitative or habitual thought and action; this is conduct which, after thought, we determine by the use of our will power to perform. It is this fact, that man is capable of willed conduct quite contrary to the conduct of people around him, quite contrary even to his own usual habits, which we must clearly understand if we are to realize fully that man is more than an automaton more than an animal which may be entirely influenced by its surroundings. 

Although everything in our mind comes from outside environment, man's rational nature makes him superior to animals, who depend solely on outside impressions. 

If man were entirely subject to the impressions received through his senses from outside environment, then he would be no more than an animal. But once the impressions have been received through the senses and the nervous system, into the brain, the nonmaterial mind and will of man seem to be able to do what the animal brain cannot do. We, as human beings, can think about the impressions we have received from our senses, we can form abstract thoughts about them, and reason about them. An example will illustrate this difficult consideration.

It is obvious that at birth we have never seen nor tasted spinach. 

We are not born with a liking for spinach, or with a dislike for it. 

Suppose that we later taste some spinach and do not like it. Let us say that whenever we see any green vegetable that resembles spinach, our sense of sight takes the impression to our brain via the nervous system, and thence our imagination revives the initial sensation of taste. We remember the taste of spinach, shudder at this remembrance, and seem to taste the spinach once again. Any animal might do the same, for animals have a sense of sight and of taste, an animal imagination and memory. We, with our human mind and will can, however, go much farther than the animal. We could sit down and think abstract thoughts about taste in general, and how

strange it is that some people like one thing and some another. Our thoughts might continue on this higher mental level, and we might think: "I do not like spinach, but it cannot possibly harm me, and it is supposed to be good for me."

This would be abstract thought, truly human thought, conducted by the nonmaterial, noninherited intellect which, however, received the original matter for the thought from outside environment, through the inherited sense of smell. 

We might then shrug our shoulders, smile at our musings, and turn our mind to other things. Or we might use the second nonmaterial, noninherited faculty of the human soul: the will. We might think some further abstract thoughts and come to a decision to act upon them. Under the influence of the will, we might decide: "I ought to do some small acts of mortification, because mortification is an essential part of the life of a true Christian. Therefore I ought not to shudder when I see spinach. Spinach cannot possibly harm me. Therefore I will, deliberately, secure some of this spinach and eat a little once a week, despite my dislike for it, despite the possible nausea I shall experience. I shall do this with a smiling face, as a compensation for sometimes overindulging my desire for nice things to eat and drink."

These faculties of mind and will, whose operations we have just described, are the distinctly human faculties of man. They distinguish him from the animal. They enable him to think abstract thoughts, and through their exercise he may rise above the animal part of his nature. If we keep our resolutions, through strength of will, then we are showing the mastery of our human nature over our animal nature. If our will be weak, because we have not strengthened it by repeated acts of the will, by making ourselves at times, do things which we do not like, and by forcing ourselves to keep our resolutions, then, in so far as our senses are not ruled by our intelligence and our will, we are nearer the animal and just that much less dignified as human beings.

The companionship and pastimes which a young person has will influence his character. Healthful recreation in good surroundings will influence it for good. 

Individual character and conduct depends upon an interplay of heredity and environment, plus the operation of our mind and will.

Heredity, plus our mind and will, provides us with the basis of our physical characteristics and of our character development, and hence of our conduct. How we develop, physically as well as in every other way, depends upon the environment to which we are subjected. Since we belong to a fallen race, we have all inherited certain tendencies toward evil. However, we all have also some good qualities which can be developed. If we are trained in good habits, good attitudes, and ideals when we are young, we shall probably develop into worthy citizens. If we are trained to use our mind and our will in a good way, then we shall be persons of "strong" character, intelligent and noble. If our mind be trained, but if we are ruled by habits to too great an extent, and our will is weak, then we shall be less useful people: too frequently we shall do things which we would have preferred not to do, had our intelligence ruled our actions. If we are so unfortunate as to have a bad environment of

Ewing Galloway 

An alley dice game a school for crime. Lack of parental supervision, bad companions, and poor neighborhood surroundings are responsible for many of our youthful criminals. many kinds, then we shall probably develop into good citizens only if the special environment provided by grace is very strong. The importance of this added environment of grace is usually realized to the fullest extent only by Catholics. An increase of grace by means of the Sacraments, indulgenced prayers, and the like, can help most powerfully to overcome a bad inheritance or the effects of a faulty environment. 

Does heredity have more to do with what makes us what we are than environment, including grace, and the influence of our soul (the mind and will)? We shall never be able to answer this question with any exactitude, for no one can tell exactly what comes to us from one or the other influence. On the whole, however, it may be said that although heredity provides us with the base, it is not the most important influence in our lives. Hence, in order to develop into worth-while Christian citizens, it is necessary to realize fully the importance of a good home, a good school, desirable companions,

healthy amusements, good reading, and the intelligent use of the intellect and the will.

BIBLIOGRAPHY Allers, R., Self Improvement (New York: Benziger Bros., 1939). 

Ross, E. J., Fundamental Sociology (Milwaukee: Bruce Publishing Co., 1939), Chap. 3. 

Schwesinger, G. C., Heredity and Environment (New York: Appleton-
