Chapter Four  The purpose of this chapter is: 

1. To learn the meaning of the sociological term culture, how culture is developed, and how we are affected by it. 

2. To distinguish between fundamental social institutions and culture which is merely relative, and to know the meaning of the term primary group. 

3. To distinguish between social life in general, and a society, and to realize that the formation of societies and the building up of culture are essentially human activities not to be found among animals. 

Culture is the name used by sociologists for the way of life of a group of people. The culture of different groups varies, sometimes considerably.

We have just been discussing the factors that go to make our own individual character and conduct. We needed to know about individual heredity and how we acquire knowledge and habits because, since the world is made up of individuals, we could not study social life profitably without knowing what makes us, as individuals, what we are. As sociologists, however, we are interested in social organization and not in individual psychology. Our next problem, therefore, is to discuss social organization in general; to discover why some social groups act in one manner, and some in another; to discover the types of social groups to be found wherever human beings live, and to learn which of these are merely an expression of man's social nature in limited localities, and which of them seem to be essential to man.

Sociologists refer to man-made environment of all kinds, by the word culture, or civilization. By this they mean not what is ordi- 43

narily meant by the word, but the way of life of a people. Culture in this specialized sociological sense includes all the social institutions, customs, and ways of living which are peculiar to any group of men, and all the material goods which any group may make use of. It is a highly complex concept, including not only objects, but specialized group activities and thought of every kind. It does not include geographical environment nor the people themselves, although, of course, both geography and the people who live in any particular geographic locality influence the culture of the group. 

Geography provides limiting factors. Certain things can be done in one climate or locality and not in another. One cannot live chiefly on fish, for example, in the middle of a desert, nor ski in tropical plains. As we said in the preceding chapter, however, geographical limitations are being overcome to an ever greater extent by modern methods of communication and transportation. It is men of the past who made the culture of our different groups, and, of course, it is men who take it up and who change and adapt it with the passing of years, but they themselves are not part of culture, in the sociological sense, although they express it and pass it on. 

Culture, therefore, is both material and nonmaterial. It differs in all social groups. All groups have their own peculiar use of material objects, and their own peculiar habits, customs, and reactions. Think how normal we in America consider a ball game to be, or ice cream for dessert in wintertime. In England, our cheering and shouting at a ball game would be considered very queer, and we, in turn, think the English behavior at a cricket match rather odd and slow. The 

English rarely eat ice cream in the winter, for they consider it only a hot-weather dessert. We are considered impolite if we do not shield our mouth when we sneeze; but when a Hopi Indian sneezes, he puts his tongue out! Girls and women in America change the style of their dresses at least every year; Chinese ladies, on the contrary, prefer to use only one style which they have retained for almost countless generations.

The culture of different groups differs in many particulars from the culture of similar groups in the past. 

Our present culture differs in many particulars from that of past ages. For instance, any book about the early American pioneers will show us the great difference between their manner of living and ours. The styles of clothing, for example, popular in 1850, or 1900, or even ten years ago, seem very odd, perhaps laughable. Again, we are accustomed to thinking of our civilization as of a very high order. Materially this is so. Never before in the history of the world have people enjoyed so many material goods. From the moral point of view, however, one cannot be so sure. People of a few generations ago in the United States and in Europe worshiped God in church as a matter of course. Many people of today, however, prefer to stay in bed on Sunday mornings, or to enjoy themselves instead of going to church. If we compare these situations, we shall be forced to decide that whole groups of people today are not so good as those of former times.

In every nation we find some people who are foolish enough to consider that the whole nation, or certain groups within it, is superior to all others. Let us consider a few facts. Many people consider that Orientals are inferior. If we look far enough into the past, however, we shall discover that all the peoples of Europe were barbarians when the Egyptians, the peoples of Asia Minor, and others in the Orient were highly civilized. We can trace from history how the 

Greeks were the first of the western peoples to become civilized. 

Then the Romans perfected a Roman culture and carried this civilization practically all over Europe, with the spread of their Empire - although they did not reach so far as Ireland, where a more primitive material culture prevailed for many centuries longer. Then we shall learn that it was the barbarians from northern Europe, in about the locality of present-day Germany, who overran Italy and the rest of Europe, broke up the civilization of the Roman Empire (although retaining much that was worth while in the culture of

the people they had conquered) and finally developed the Feudal System. About this time, when Europe became less civilized than before, the Arabs developed a civilization of a higher order. Later, in Europe, the guild system developed, and, after many other changes, our modern capitalistic civilization, which is an economic system that differs from Bolshevism in Russia, and totalitarianism in Germany, Italy, and some other countries. 

We in the United States think that most of our culture came from Europe, especially from England. So it did. Yet it was the North 

American Indians who gave us, and Europeans too, the use of tobacco; and it was they who introduced the use of corn, the potato, tomatoes, and many other things we accept as our own. We, in our turn, invented a number of material items of culture and the methods of using them, which are now employed by Europeans. For example, an American Negro invented shoe-manufacturing machinery; Edison invented electric-light bulbs; the scientific management of modern industry was perfected by Americans. 

We receive culture as a social heritage from the past, and adapt it to our needs. Culture remains relatively stable, however, because in transmitting its culture to us, society exercises an important social control.

Culture does not come to us by biological inheritance. We are not born with ideas about democracy, or with the notion that white people are superior to colored, or that it is correct form for a man to raise his hat when meeting a lady; neither are we born with the ability to play the piano, write on a typewriter by the "touch" method, or put on lipstick and rouge in a becoming manner! We say that we "inherit” culture, but this inheritance is social, not biological. It is a legacy, as it were. Ideas, inventions, ways of living, are passed on from one generation to the next as a social heritage, by means of social groups and the education which these groups give us either through formal instruction or through our conformance, voluntary or unconscious to the ways of those around us. Either we are forced to conform to the group, or else we usually want to do so, because

otherwise we are considered "queer" or "odd." Sociologists use the term social control to express the way society directly or indirectly influences us to act and think in accordance with its customs and standards.

Some of our thoughts and some of the things made by our present-day society are exactly like those of the past. Other aspects of culture have been gradually changed to suit modern needs and ideas, or because people saw better ways of doing certain things and gradually changed accepted opinion. For instance, we took the idea of locomotion by means of a horse and buggy, and developed the automobile, which we perfect yearly. Improved methods of transportation have, in turn, resulted in considerable changes in many of our ways of life, and in certain aspects of our social and economic culture. Democracy is a part of our culture, yet we submit to greater social control by government than in former times: the change came about gradually, and because we saw the need of it, we submitted to the additional control. 

Culture has developed differently in different parts of the world. 

We may well ask the question: How did culture originate? All men are, of course, descended from Adam and Eve. This means that culture, no matter how it may differ in different parts of the world, began with the culture developed by the first members of the human race, for only man has developed culture. Before the Fall, the moral culture of Adam and Eve must have been very high, but their material culture was probably primitive indeed. They used as best they could the goods supplied to them by their geographical environment: their first dress was made of leaves! Apart from the brief account contained in the first books of the Bible, we have no record of the manner of life of the earliest period. The exact history of mankind does not go back very far, for although this earth has been inhabited by men for tens of thousands of years (possibly even for hundreds of thousands of years), history takes us back only a few thousands. Archeological discoveries, however, seem to show

Tea drinking in the Orient. Compare this scene with a modern American soda fountain. that, in general, the farther away from our own times we get, the more primitive were the remains left to us. The earliest times of which we have any remains at all have left for us only a few flints and ash-heaps; whereas we have a growing amount of remains of later periods of the far prehistoric past. 

Although we know nothing definite about earliest migrations and culture, other than the very brief Biblical account, probably when the district around the Garden of Eden became too thickly populated, groups of men wandered off to other geographical localities. 

They would take with them the ideas they had developed at home, the use of material objects and the like, and would adapt these in the new locality, making use of the new objects they found there. 

Then travelers spread from one group to another the new ideas and

inventions which had been made. Thus culture developed. Each generation would receive the culture of former generations, change and develop it before passing it on to the next. So different cultures grew up in different parts of the world: the culture of the peoples of the East, of the Arabs, of different European countries, of North and South America. With the more rapid development of transportation and communication the spread of new cultural ideas would be more speedy, so that today a new idea conceived in one part of the world may be quickly transmitted to us by letter, telephone, or telegraph, or even by a personal visit. On the other hand, the culture of people living in isolated areas-distant islands, in forests far from more advanced civilizations, and similar relatively inaccessible spots - has sometimes remained very primitive. Of course, these more primitive people have developed the original culture of early man, changing it to meet their own geographical features and limitations. There has been some further development among them because of the original ideas of certain of their members, but their cultural advancement has not been that of the people who lived nearer to centers of communication. 

Man is social by nature and lives in community or society. Some of the social groups men form are mere chance associations, others are societies. A society is a relatively stable union of two or more persons who unite to secure a common end by collective action.

Because man is social by nature, he lives a group life. General group life is called the community, or society in general. Within this general life, however, he forms special groups. These special groups may be merely chance associations, such as a crowd at a theater or at a ball game, or the people in a streetcar or a train. On the other hand, these groups may be formed for a particular purpose, and then the group becomes a society, such as a family, a boy-scout group, a school, a parish club.

A society of this latter type has certain characteristics: It is a rela tively stable union of two or more persons who unite to obtain a

common end by collective action. In other words, one cannot have a society of less than two persons! Usually there are more than two. 

There must be some reason for its existence, that is, some sort of social bond between the persons. There must be a certain permanency about the association. Because no group of persons can remain harmoniously in a group founded to carry out a specific purpose without rules or someone to direct them, there must be some sort of directive authority for every society. Thus the elements of a true society, as distinct from community or society in general, might be summed up as follows: plurality of persons, community of aim for the common good, stability of bond, cooperation of effort, and authority.

Many aspects of culture are relative; other aspects arise out of the very nature of man and must remain stable. Social institutions have been built around some of man's needs. 

In a previous paragraph the ease with which man changes many items of his culture was shown. As was said there, a number of our ways of life have changed considerably within recent years. For example, fashions in dancing change almost from year to year, and certain customs built around dancing have changed considerably from one generation to the next. Compare, for instance, the elaborate ceremony involved in asking a young lady for the privilege of a dance, once in vogue in America, with the now commonly accepted custom of "cutting in." (On the other hand, certain dance customs have varied but little for generations, for example the Highland fling or the Irish reel.) Again, it was not so long ago that most men had beards; today, on the contrary, almost all men are clean-shaven.

Certain aspects of culture, however, arise out of man's very nature, and must be maintained if man is adequately to fulfill his purpose in life. This fact must be clearly known and understood. For example, as discussed in Chapter One, all men are born to serve God, their creator, and have a right to life, liberty, justice, truth, and limited ownership to private property. Our social organization

and our culture must, therefore, give man an opportunity to maintain his rights. It must also allow man to perform his duties, which are chiefly to serve God, to see that the rights of others are respected, and to love his neighbor: a duty which Christ Himself said was a duty second only to the commandment of loving God.¹

Around both relative and essential culture we have built social institutions. A social institution is a fixed form of a phase of social life which has become habitual to man, either because it is built around a fundamental need, or because it at least satisfies a need of widespread importance. Some social institutions, therefore, are considered natural to man. They are to be found in all types of culture and must have been designed by God. Others are important but not essential. A social institution may be a set of customs or it may be a society.

The fundamental social institutions considered essentially natural to man are six in number.

The family is the most important natural institution or society in any community. Man has a sex instinct and a social instinct which lead him to desire to mate. If the majority of people did not marry, the human race would long since have died out. Children need a much longer training than animals to develop to their highest potentialities, and therefore a more or less permanent union is required, and the monogamous family seems best suited to effect the purpose of the family, hence it must be natural. 

A second natural institution or society is called the State. Just as the individual cannot live in isolation, neither can the family. Individuals and families need the cooperation of others, and it is natural for them to group together for the special purpose of protection and development of the common good of the group, under an authority which is called the government. All the special societies of the group then function within the State, which is sometimes given the name of Civil Society. 'Cf. Mark 12:31; Matt. 25:31-46.

1 

Ewing Galloway

A well-to-do Chinese family at dinner. Compare their manner of eating with that of an American family.

A third fundamental social institution, which is supernatural rather than natural, is the Church. Everything, including man, has been created by God, hence we owe Him worship. Since we are social beings, we owe Him collective worship in a society which we call the Church. Of course, there are many other proofs that the 

Church is a supernatural society. For example, man's nature is never fully developed here on earth, and this presupposes a continued existence: God revealed the existence of His Church to us through our Lord Jesus Christ and the Scriptures.

Economic society, or the work group, is a fourth natural society. 

It is obvious that man can live better by dividing up labor so that some men specialize in one thing, and some in another, and then

exchange goods they do not need for those which they do. Men seem to have been aware of this fact from time immemorial, for we find the work group existing, with the division of labor, all during the times of history, and among all people at the present day. The work group, therefore, seems to be a natural society. 

Organized international society would seem to be a fifth natural society, even though it does not now exist. Just as the division of labor within a local community helps men to procure a greater variety of goods than they could produce for themselves, so harmonious international relations enable the people of one region to communicate with those in other regions by trade, and permit the general exchange of culture by travel and other means of communication. For this reason, international society seems to be a natural society which man, with his intelligence, should be able to organize successfully.

A sixth natural social institution is private property. This institution is not a society! A certain amount of private property ownership enables a man to develop to the best of his capacity and to provide for all his needs and the needs of his family. Unlimited private property ownership is bad, of course, for this leads to some people having too much wealth and others having too little. As shown in Appendix II, property ownership is a stewardship which the individual must administer wisely so that the rights of others are not impeded.

The basic elements of all these fundamental social institutions should not be changed, because they are necessary to man by reason of his very nature. Incidental customs which have grown around them, however, may be changed at will. For example, the monogamous family is fundamental in itself, but whether the father be called "Sir," or "Pop," or whether everyone works at home, or the father or some other member of the family goes outside the home to work for others, does not necessarily affect the fundamental character of the family. Divorce, on the other hand, effects a change in a basic element of the monogamous family.

Society is also divided into primary and secondary groups. 

We have just discussed social institutions as those which are natural and fundamental, and those which are not. Society in general may also be differentiated as primary and secondary groups. 

Primary groups are those which are of greatest importance to man as an individual, because he comes into personal contact with them daily or frequently over a long period of time. These are: the family, the neighborhood, the church, the school, the play group, and the work group. Some of these primary groups are natural societies too;

Ewing Galloway 

A swarm of young bees going into a new hive. Animals are gregarious, but not social. They lack intelligence and free will. others are not. All other social groups may be called secondary associations. This classification of society will become more clear when we have discussed social life at greater detail in later chapters. 

Only man is a social being, and the subject of sociological study. 

Animals are gregarious, but not sociable. They cannot consciously form societies or hand down culture because they lack reason and are dependent upon instinct and mere imitation. 

Certain animals are sometimes spoken of as social beings, because some of them remain true to their mates, as do many birds, and because others, as wolves, bees, ants, beavers, go together in groups or help each other. These facts show that animals have a gregarious instinct, and some of them a very highly developed instinct to adapt themselves to needs and circumstances. However, animals never

form societies, because they do not have the intelligence to work together for a definite prearranged purpose. For example, at a certain definite time bees swarm, whether or not preparations have been made for the swarming. They cannot decide not to swarm; they cannot refuse to obey their instinct to swarm. In other words, animals lack the intelligence, which human beings have, to distinguish between ends and means; moreover, they lack a free will. 

Animals differ from human beings in two other important respects. 

First, unlike human beings, they do not form a culture which they can hand down from generation to generation. Secondly, they have not developed a language, so as to transmit ideas and hand down written records to posterity. For example, a parrot may be taught to imitate a few sounds and say a few words, or a dog may be trained to do some tricks, but they cannot teach their offspring the same achievements. No animal has ever been found that learned to make tools or other artifacts (items of material culture), and certainly not one that could hand down such cultural achievements to its offspring by training, by verbal tradition, or by a written record.

2. Give a short talk on the need of a certain amount of social control in order to conserve true freedom. 

3. Secure an interview with someone who has lived in a different cultural group from the present one, and ask him to make a list of the differences in culture which he noted. If possible, try to discover what changes annoyed him and what he merely found amusing or different. 

Discuss whether or not the annoyance was due to prejudices, or whether it was justified and the present environment might well be changed to another type. (It is not necessary to meet a person from a foreign country to answer this question: any member of a city who formerly lived in the country or vice versa; or any person who formerly lived in a different section of the country or who belonged to a different church group will be able to help students to make this activity productive of worthwhile results.) 

4. Draw up a list of arguments on the blackboard, pro and con, for the slogan: "Manners Makyth Man," using as examples such habits not belonging to current "good manners" as the use of bad grammar and of careless pronunciation, the chewing of gum, lack of gentlemanly politeness to women in crowded streetcars or busses, etc. 

5. Divide the class into two groups. Have one group draw up a list of reasons why they might think prejudice to be a lack of true understanding of the nature of the relativity of culture. Let the other group draw up a list of examples and reasons to prove that it is sometimes unintelligent to be "unprejudiced" or "neutral" and that in certain situations one may appear to be "prejudiced" because one must uphold certain unchanging standards or "norms." 

6. Have some member of the class define and illustrate the term "stereotype." Discuss some popular stereotypes, for example those

about government employees, Negroes, Americans, the Irish, the English, the Russians, a modern American high-school girl. Then divide the class into groups and have each group prepare a short description of one of the stereotypes discussed, with cartoons or other illustrations, and a brief opinion as to the advisability of judging any individual according to such stereotypes. 

7. Have some members of the class prepare two-minute talks on the following topics: 

A definition of propaganda. 

How propaganda is disseminated. 

The influence of propaganda. 

The greater influence of propaganda today than in former times. 

Why we should beware of being influenced by propaganda. 

The necessity of propaganda in certain instances. 

How the intelligent person can discriminate between propaganda which should be listened to, that which should be discarded, and that which should be heeded only to a limited extent. 

General summary of the topic. 

8. Arrange a class discussion about the topic: Material environment affects the city dwellers to a greater degree than those who live in the country. 

9. Show by an exhibit of newspapers of different views on politics or other topics, how readers may be influenced by the attitudes and ideals expressed in headlines, editorials, cartoons, pictures, and other features. Note the presentation of biased news, and the difference between the presentation of facts and opinions. 

10. Visit a museum which has exhibits of the culture of other peoples, for example, North American Indians, any foreign groups, any primitive groups, reconstructions of the culture of prehistoric times. Make a written report of the chief items of culture which you saw which differ from those used in your own social group.

Chapter Five  The purpose of this chapter is: 

1. To know why the State is a fundamental, natural society, and to learn its functions, its rights, and its duties. 

2. To distinguish between the government and the State. 

3. To learn what is meant by a democracy, and to know the civic organization of the United States. 

4. To know what are the rights and duties of citizens, and when civil war may be justified. 

We need the cooperation of others for our economic and social welfare. States came into being to protect the rights of individuals and to promote their welfare in organized society. 

At the beginnings of the race, the family was sufficient to care for human needs. As the population increased and separate families were formed, communities developed. These local neighborhood groups may be called primary groups because people came into frequent face-to-face contact with them. History is silent as to when people first realized that mere living together in social groups is insufficient: and that man needs to form a definite organized society if he is to live an ordered life as a human being, to develop his special gifts and aptitudes, and to secure the goods and services which other people might be able to give him because of their special gifts and skills. It must soon have been obvious, that if each family had to be self-supporting and provide for its own requirements, there would be little time left for those with abilities in craftsmanship or the arts or sciences to follow their true avocation. 

Nor could the single family provide the means of communication, 61

roads, mail service, education, trade facilities, and many other worth-while things of life without the assistance of others in similar need. The general community also needed some form of organization for protection against hostile tribes. Individuals and families within the group needed protection in their rights. Very soon after mankind began to live on this earth, therefore, people must have realized the advantages of organizing a definite society for protection and self-defense and the maintenance of peace, as well as for the promotion of the general economic and social good. "Man is a political animal," as Aristotle said. The society organized for this purpose is called the State. 

Although the rise of the earliest state-forms cannot be traced, history provides us with information about the establishment of later forms. The Old Testament records the development of the 

State among the Jews, from a patriarchial system within the tribe, to a theocracy or government by the priests under God, and later to a monarchy which the people themselves asked for when they wanted a king to rule them. Within comparatively modern times various new States have been formed out of old ones. Belgium came into being in 1830. After centuries of domination by Germany on the one hand and Russia on the other, the State of Poland emerged at the end of the Great War of 1914-18. In 1776 part of the continent of North America and the people there emerged as a State known as the United States, whereas before it had been colonized by British, Dutch, French, Spaniards, and others. By the Statute of Westminster of 1931, several countries attained dominion status and the British Empire was changed into a "commonwealth of Nations." 

The State is the whole people of an area, organized for government, with the authority necessary for the orderly conduct of this organization.

A State is made up of four elements. (1) There must be a given population or community, that is, an association of individuals and families, and all the municipalities, and other local groups

to which they belong. (2) The people must live within the boundaries of a well-defined territory. (3) They must be free from foreign control. (4) They must have a government which exercises real authority: an agency through which the common good of the people is realized.

We have already explained that the reason for forming a State (sometimes called a civil society) is for the necessary twofold function of protection and the development of the common good. 

Generally speaking, a State is usually characterized by a certain degree of homogeneity in race, language, social and cultural ideals, which distinguish it from other States. 

Although the words state and nation are frequently used synonymously, actually they have not the same meaning. The word nation, derived from the Latin nascor, natus, meaning birth, refers primarily to the common origin of a people. We keep this original sense when we speak of one's nationality, the group of persons to which one is connected by birth. The word state, on the other hand, from the Latin status, meaning something fixed and established, refers primarily to the organization and government of a people.

The State is a natural institution: a fundamental natural society. It is also a perfect society. 

The State is a natural society because in it is fully expressed man's aptitude and propensity for group life, for organization under authority; and in it is also found the full expression of the universal, fundamental, human need to gather into groups for mutual protection and for the promotion of the social welfare, that is, the common good. It is only by living within an organized State that we can attain our full material, intellectual, and moral development, further our general interests, and protect our rights and our liberty from the encroachment of others. 

It is necessary for man not only to found families, but also to live within a State. As Pope Pius XI expressed it, following the

teachings of theologians and philosophers: "Civil society is a perfect society, having in itself all the means for its peculiar end, which is the temporal well-being of the community.' 991 The family is founded especially for the propagation of the race and the education of children, and is so fundamentally necessary that it, too, is a natural society. It is, indeed, the most important society upon earth for without it mankind would die out from the earth. As Pope Pius XI said in the same encyclical: "The family has priority of nature and therefore of rights over civil society. 

Nevertheless, the family is an imperfect society, since it has not in itself all the means for its own complete development." He continues by showing that in view of the common good, the State "has pre-eminence over the family, which finds its own suitable temporal perfection precisely in civil society." The only other perfect society is the Church. Because the State's aim is the material and temporal welfare of its members, it belongs to a sphere inferior to that of the Church, whose aim is the spiritual and eternal welfare of her members. Nevertheless, in its sphere, the State is sovereign. 

The Church has no authority over the State as such, but only over the individual members who form the State. Both Church and 

State have relations with their members as members of other societies, and with these societies as a whole, to which their members may belong. All other societies are, in some measure, dependent either upon the State or upon the Church. 

No state can exist without authority exercised by the ruler or government.

Men are social beings, and since no group of men can pursue a common end harmoniously and with advantage for any length of time without a directing hand, authority is absolutely necessary in any society. Of its very nature, then, authority is essential to 

¹Pope Pius XI, Divini illius magistri, The Christian Education of Youth (Five 

Great Encyclicals, p. 40), p. 6. 'Ibid., p. 6 (Five Great Encyclicals, p. 40). 'Ibid., p. 6 (Five Great Encyclicals, p. 40).

a social group, and must come from God. "There is no power but from God." For this reason, we have already stated that authority is one of the four essential elements of the State: we call the organization of this authority by the term government. 

It is natural for man to live in organized civil society under a duly constituted government, because he has a propensity, aptitude, and need for more extensive social cooperation than that provided by the family, and a need for living in ordered society. Yet the type of government to be arranged is not determined by divine law. It is left to men to decide that, either consciously, or by the arrangement of providence. The opinion most widely accepted by political scientists and philosophers about the method in which the government of a State receives its initial authority is this: that the authority necessary for the right conduct of the State (civil authority) is first vested by God immediately in the people. The people, however, cannot all exercise it, for chaos would result. 

They are therefore bound by nature to organize into a State, and in forming civil society (the State) they transfer their authority to the ruler or rulers to whom they entrust the reins of government, according to the form decided upon and willingly agreed to. 

The government of a nation thus derives its power from God, through the people. This authority or power is conferred on the ruler or rulers when a particular form of government is established. 

A ruler or government may procure this power or authority through inheritance, through election by the people, or in any other fashion decided on by the people in establishing a definite State, or acquiesced in by the people when a new form is imposed upon them by force. 

'Rom. 13:1. 

'Some philosophers, however, are of the opinion that authority comes directly from God to those who are designated to exercise it, and not through the people and their natural organization into civil society. Thus for the infliction of the death sentence (exercised by a judge, etc.) they claim authority must come directly from God, for He alone has power over life, saying that no man or group of men possesses such po-ver.

Gathering of a Witan, an early Saxon tribal court. From the first, individuals and families have needed protection in their rights. 

The power of the State is limited by the rights of individuals and of lawful societies.

Although the State is sovereign in its own sphere, and has the power to enact laws and to enforce their observance, even where necessary by force, the authority of the State is not unlimited. 

First, the State is limited by the measure of the power conferred on it by the people, by long habit or by a direct constitution. 

Second, the State itself is merely a unified body of the persons who compose it, and since all men are moral beings, with free will and subject to duties, the State is a moral being as well, and hence is subject to the moral law. It cannot, therefore, make laws which are contrary to the natural law and the common good. 

Third, the State exists solely for the use of man, because man has need of it to live a full and complete human life. It is there-

fore limited in its power by the natural rights of individuals and of groups of individuals, and by the rights of the Church. The individual and the family both existed before any State came into being, and it is the State's duty not to usurp their rights, but to safeguard and promote them. Similarly, since man has a natural right to social life, he has a right to form any social group or society that he may choose, provided it is not dangerous or contrary to the natural law. The State must therefore preserve and protect the right of any lawfully constituted society, however small. 

Fourth, the State must not interfere with the Church's authority in matters pertaining to faith and morals, but must protect the rights of the Church, which has a right to exist as a lawful, fundamental, and perfect society. This is true also because man's supernatural interests have been entrusted exclusively to the Church, and the State, existing for the good of man, must not hinder his eternal welfare, for this is of more importance than anything else in the world.

As no State should do more than is necessary for the performance of its functions, citizens are safeguarded against tyranny in a properly conducted State. 

Provided the two functions of the State are carried out satisfactorily, the government may take any legitimate form. 

Provided a State is duly constituted and the government works reasonably well for the protection and the common good of the people within the State, the government may be of any form. 

A strong central government, such as exists under a monarchy or other form of authoritarian State, e.g., modern Portugal, is by no means necessarily bad. The common good of the people may be more truly promoted by the ruler than it would be under another type of government. The difficulty in such a form of government is that the power may be inherited by an unwise or an unjust ruler, to the detriment of the citizens at that time. A government having power that is limited by a constitutional document seems

to be better than an absolute monarchy or other authoritarian government. Generally speaking, a democracy seems to be the best type of government although it, too, has its limitations. 

An authoritarian form of government of the totalitarian kind is definitely bad, because the philosophy which gives rise to it is that the people exist for the State. This false view fails to realize that the State is not a mystic entity, but an organization of the very people themselves. Totalitarian States and their governments do not recognize sufficiently the limitations of the State's power. 

The government of a totalitarian State, such as Russia, Germany, and Italy, is in the hands of a party which sees few or no limits to its powers, whereas in actual fact the State should exercise only the power necessary to carry out its twofold function and nothing more. Russia, of course, claims to be a democracy, but the voter has not much choice: he can only vote for one party!

The government of the United States is a democracy. 

A democracy is a type of government where the people rule, either directly or indirectly. A direct democracy is one in which all citizens vote on each governmental issue. The indirect democracy, however, is the only existing form at the present time. This is the form in which the people elect a body of representatives to form a government, delegating to them all or nearly all their power. 

An indirect democracy may take several forms. The form prevailing in the United States is considered to be one of the best, although even here many problems arise because of the size of the country. Actually, our Federal Government is, as the word implies, a federation of smaller ones. The division of our government is as follows: 

First, city or local township government. Second, county government. Third, state government. The state government is very important, because it is under it that city and county governments hold their charters. Each state has its own constitution, and the state political representatives must follow the laws laid down in

the constitution of their state, or be subject to legal procedure. 

Under the United States Constitution, each of the states has very wide powers of self-government. 

Fourth, there is the Federal Government, whose work is to supervise interstate organization, to supply whatever the individual states cannot do for their population's needs, to co-ordinate the political organization of the whole, and to represent the nation as a whole in international life. 

The Federal Government is subject to legislative control, because the Constitution limits considerably the power of the Federal Government. It is also subject to control by other groups which form a system of checks and balances. Thus, the United States has legislative, judicial, and executive departments, each with limited powers. The Supreme Court, which is above and beyond all political parties, has as its duty to see that the laws and practices of the government conform to just principles and the Constitution of the 

United States. 

Democracy is the best form of government for Americans, provided they watch for weaknesses and continuously try to maintain high standards.

Since a democracy seems to us to be the best form of government, a consideration of its defects and of the means by which these defects may be eliminated is of considerable importance to all intelligent citizens of the United States. 

One of the difficulties connected with democratic government is that, although many of our politicians are men of sterling integrity and ability, anxious to rule the country well, popularity rather than ability is too often the deciding factor in elections. Then, too, the people, although they do not have a direct vote, can influence the vote of the representatives whom they elect to rule for them. 

Now, the majority of the people have neither the education nor the specialized knowledge to form a correct judgment on governmental issues. They are easily swayed by propaganda or the biased opinions of badly informed persons with popular emotional appeal,

and hence they may seriously hamper the government activities of their representatives. Where a government issue concerns foreign affairs, or affairs of a technical character such as banking, the majority of the people and many of their representatives may lack the experience or knowledge to form an intelligent opinion. As a result the general governmental body may be led to oppose a course of action which competent authorities within the government, with their more expert knowledge, honestly and rightly consider to be necessary. Similarly, the delay entailed by required governmental procedure according to the constitution or longestablished custom sometimes seriously interferes with prompt action on affairs of vital importance. On the other hand, although an absolute monarch or the ruler of an authoritarian State is not hampered as in a democracy and can make prompt decisions and insist on any lines of action which he decides upon, democracy, for all its weaknesses, definitely seems to provide greater safeguards for the good of the majority of the citizens. 

The function of the State is to protect the rights of the individuals who compose it, and to promote their well-being. It is the duty of the State to do whatever is necessary to carry out these functions.

The State's whole reason for existence is that it can provide more completely than the individual or smaller groups for man's needs, and can foster the temporal happiness of its members. 

1. The State must protect the rights of individuals and groups which compose it, and in doing this it must protect the rights of everyone, not merely those of a majority or of the rich or influential. 

2. The State must promote the well-being of its members. The 

State should not, for example, undertake any work that is not primarily an affair of government and which can be satisfactorily accomplished by private initiative and smaller societies. If the State undertakes unnecessary tasks, it is not promoting social well-being. 

Man should be free to undertake what he thinks to be best,

Keystone View 

Officers of the Food and Drug Administration inspecting grain. This is one means taken by our government to protect the quality of our food supply. provided it be lawful; and if the State leaves smaller groups to do what they can, it will then have greater freedom to fulfill its other duties. Certain functions, of course, may be carried out advantageously by the State. Such, for example, would be the delivery of the mail with all that it entails, and even, as in some countries, the ownership and operation of railroads, telephone companies, and the like. Government ownership and operation of certain types of business are not socialism, provided that in acquiring the businesses the government compensate the former owners. 

Most States provide such protective services as an army, a navy, an air force, a police force, and a judicial service. Most States also protect their citizens from fire hazards, ill-health, and disease, by

the provision of laws for fire fighting and prevention, for street cleaning, sewage disposal, clean handling of food, nonadulteration of goods, inspection of factories and workshops, and the like. They protect the morality of citizens by legislation against immoral literature, plays, and vice in general. Since man's eternal welfare is of prime importance, a good State gives legal protection to religion. 

The promotional services of the average State are quite extensive. 

Most States provide for the construction and upkeep of necessary roads, bridges, canals, public buildings, and waterworks, and they also preserve beauty spots, undertake reforestation schemes, and similar services. The State should also foster education and supply the defects of private educational systems. States usually establish universities, museums, libraries, art galleries, zoological gardens, and other educational facilities. The duty of the State, however, is merely to supply the deficiencies of the family and private endeavor, and to exercise part of the disciplinary powers formerly exercised by the parents alone.

The State has a duty, too, to provide laws for the regulation of social and economic life, and to perform those social and economic services which are not adequately carried on by private associations. For example, when the capitalistic system failed to provide adequately for the unemployed, the aged, the dependent poor, those in ill-health, it was the duty of the State to remedy the situation either by promoting adequate private measures, or by undertaking such services as the promotion of social security legislation.

The State likewise has a duty to provide municipal and governmental administrative departments, and also a diplomatic corps for its dealings with other States. 

If a State fails to carry out its duties, civil war may be just, within certain limits.

The citizens of a State can justifiably overthrow an established government only when they are legitimately dissatisfied with the

existing form of government, and cannot by legal processes substitute another. A revolt against existing authority, to bring about a needed change of government, is morally allowable only on the following conditions: 

1. If all legal processes to effect a change have been tried unsuccessfully. 

2. If there is reasonable hope of success for the rebellion. 

3. If the evils resulting from the rebellion are not expected to be disproportionate to the gravity of the present government disorder, and are certainly less than the benefits which it is hoped to attain by the rebellion. 

4. If the international common good is adequately safeguarded. 

Since the State is a natural society, and hence perpetual by nature, a change of government does not mean a change in the State itself, and the new form of government must always undertake to pay the obligations of its predecessor. 

A State may be established by illegal means, but if afterward the government rules justly and well, with the general consent of the people, there is no justification for a civil war. 

The State has the right to demand anything which is necessary for it to carry out its functions in an efficient manner. 

In order to prevent abuse on the part of the officials who form the government, and who represent the State in carrying out its functions, the rights of the rulers of the State must be limited by the authority committed to them by the legal enactments of the State, by long custom, or by a definitely written constitution. Within these limitations, however, the State as a whole has the following rights: 

1. The right to do anything necessary for the common good. 

2. The right to expect its citizens to cooperate with it fully in the exercise of its duties for the common good, for this end is the very reason for the State's existence. 

3. The right to expect its citizens to obey its laws; and to punish transgressions for the common good.

Courtesy National Bureau of Standards 

An official of the National Bureau of Standards in his laboratory. This bureau determines standards for many items in commerce and industry, thus protecting the public against inferior materials. 

4. The right to conscript men for military service, for the defense of the country. 

5. The right to exact taxes, to secure the requisite finances to carry on its work.

The first right gives the State wide powers, within the limits of the moral law. For example, legislation usually enables the State to limit the individual's natural right to the ownership of private property, when this is necessary for the common good, and provided due recompense is made to the owner of the property concerned. This right, called the right of eminent domain, is exercised, for example, when it is necessary to build a road over someone's land.

Citizens need the cooperation of the State to protect their rights and to promote their well-being, but they, in turn, must cooperate with the State in its efforts to fulfill its duties to other citizens. 

Since rights involve corresponding duties; and since man is a social being who must cooperate in any effort to help his fellow men; since also the State is a natural society, deriving its authority from God, the citizen has the following duties to perform toward the 

State: 

1. He must comply with all civil laws which are in conformity with reason and not against the moral law. 

2. He must respect public authority, both public officials and the laws they enact, for "There is no power but from God." 

3. He must be patriotic and loyal to his country, cooperating sincerely with the State in its work for the common good and contributing to its security and advancement. a) A citizen must have a natural love for his country: this is true patriotism, a noble virtue. However, if his regard for his own country is such as to exclude a charitable understanding for other countries and their citizens, who all form part of the human race and the Mystical Body of Christ, then his attitude is perverted, and is called nationalism, chauvinism, or jingoism. Excessive nationalism is an unnatural emotion. It takes its root in national pride and egotism, and breeds international injustice, hatred, and war. b) A citizen must use his vote, if he has one, and so must take the greater or less share in government that has been accorded to him, in order to avoid a majority vote at the hands of those who have possibly less correct notions of justice than he has himself. 

Every citizen should try to form intelligent opinions on all public matters, and if he has a vote, he should use it. If he can take an active part in civic affairs, he ought to consider seriously whether or not he should do so. c) A citizen must pay the taxes justly demanded of him. The • Rom. 13:1.

State needs money to carry out its functions for the benefit of the citizens. Each citizen, therefore, should pay his fair share of the costs of this burden, in accordance with his ability to pay. A tax is each man's social obligation in this matter, expressed in terms of money.

1. What is the difference between a neighborhood group and the State? 

2. What is the difference between the terms, State and government? 

3. Is the State different from the people who compose it? Why or why not? 

4. What is a State, and what are its elements? 

5. What is the difference between the terms, State and nation? 

6. Why is the State a natural social institution? 

7. What is meant by the statement: "the State is a perfect society”? 

8. What is authority? 

9. Why does true authority within a society come from God? 

10. Why must people usually exercise the necessary authority for organized civil society through a government? 

11. Would the people of a State be really free if no authority were exercised through a government? Discuss. 

12. What is the limitation of the power of the State, as exercised through the government? 

13. Why is it possible for the government to take several forms, provided the State carries out its two functions satisfactorily?

14. Why is democracy not the only form of good government? Why is it a very good form? 

15. Why is a totalitarian government wrong? 

16. Why is it correct to say that we have a democratic government in the United States, even though the government does many more things for citizens today than in former times? 

17. Can you give an important reason why it may be necessary for the government to do more for us than it did in former years, even though we remain a democracy? 

18. Why would it be impossible for a large nation such as ours to exercise direct authority on all national issues? 

19. What are the three divisions of government in the United States? 

20. What are the weaknesses of democracy? How can we lessen these, and still remain a democracy? 

21. Under what conditions is a civil war justified? 

22. What are the rights of the State? Discuss briefly. 

23. What are the duties of a citizen? Discuss briefly. 

24. Why should everyone who possesses a vote use his right during an election? 

25. Why should good citizens watch public affairs closely, and enter as much as possible into civic life? 

26. Why is it particularly important for an American Catholic citizen to become acquainted with political issues and to take part in civic affairs? 

27. What is the connection between a good democratic government and a good public educational system?

1. Give a short talk to show that citizens of any State must be sure to see that any new proposed form of government will not lead to a totalitarian government. 

2. Give a short talk on the services which ambassadors and other diplomats render to the State. 

3. Give a short talk on the work of consular offices abroad. 

4. Show by diagrams on the blackboard the chief divisions of the legislative, executive, and judicial branches of the following governments: a) Your local village, town, or city; b) Your county;

c) The Federal Government. 

5. With the permission of your teacher, write to your local government for information on the following departments, so as to prepare short talks to describe their civic functions: a) The Department of Sanitation; 

b) The Department of Public Health; 

c) The Department of Public Welfare. 

6. List on the blackboard some of the fields of human activity for which you think there should be little or no legal control over the individual. Discuss this. 

7. Make a list on the blackboard of the various ways in which private property is limited or restricted by government control. Why is this control necessary? 

8. Make a list of all the public buildings which you think your city should have. Discuss. 

9. Debate. Resolved: that the government should maintain a control over the radio system of the country.

Unit Two  

This Unit will show us that the family is the most important social group from the natural point of view. We shall learn its important functions in life, and why social life becomes disorganized and contrary to God's plan if families are not well-knit social groups. We shall also learn the importance of the Church as a social group, and its influence on society. 81
