A home is much more than a mere house. Home includes a spirit of affection and comradeship. It is the inner sanctuary of the family.

Chapter Six  The purpose of this chapter is: 

1. To realize the great social importance of marriage and the family as social institutions. 

2. To study fully the social functions of the family. 

3. To understand the true purpose of monogamous marriage and the family, and why no other forms are satisfactory. 

Marriage is a fundamental social institution, necessary to maintain the family and the home. Christian marriage is a Sacrament. 

God has given human beings two important instincts—the sex instinct and a social instinct, which usually lead them to desire to marry and to found a family and a home. He also gave human beings an affectionate nature which would lead them to want a more intimate companionship and love than that afforded by ordinary social life. Parents might weary of each other, and find parenthood and the rearing of children too arduous, without the love which makes continued companionship desirable. 

Men soon realized that these gifts were so great that they needed to be safeguarded, and they saw that they would serve their purpose well only if they were well regulated. Social institutions called marriage and the family were therefore established. They are called fundamental social institutions because they are fixed forms of certain phases of social life built around a need which is rooted in man's very nature. Indeed, if it were not for marriage and the family, the human race would have died out long ago. A regulated institution of marriage is also needed to ensure a stable family and home life. 

Let us be sure that we know the meaning of the terms we are 83

going to employ. Do we realize how important it is to know the true meaning of marriage?

Marriage is the permanent union of two persons, formed under a contract, for the purpose of mutual help and friendship and the founding of a family. When made in accordance with the laws of the Church, the marriage contract becomes also a Sacrament. 

Wherever there are groups of men we find that families are first united by some form of marriage ceremony. In other words, we find that a specific marriage contract has been established by all peoples as a social institution. Indeed, marriage is a fundamentally necessary social institution because it is essential to the well-being of the natural society of the family. If people married without the social control of the rules and regulations which exist in connection with every important social institution, too many social problems would arise: there would be an especial danger that the children would not be properly cared for, and that the finer forms of social living would be missed.

Because marriage is necessary to perpetuate the human race, God has given most people a vocation to be married.¹ Since marriage is the vocation of most people, and since married life and the rearing of children involve many difficulties, Christ raised the social institution of marriage to the high dignity of a Sacrament. Marriage is important, therefore, not only as a social institution, but also as a divinely instituted religious contract and Sacrament. 

The family is a natural (fundamental) society, with a definite purpose or end. It forms a primary face-to-face group within the home, and is the most important agent of social control. 

A family is a domestic society which originates in marriage. It includes parents and their children, and sometimes other relatives ¹There are, of course, three vocations: to religion; to the married state; to the single state in the world. The highest vocation is that to follow the counsels of perfection, which includes remaining unmarried (cf. Matt. 19:12, 21; I Cor. 

7:25-28; 32-34; 37–38). Nevertheless, if one's vocation is to marry, that is the highest vocation for the person concerned, and many saints have been married.

also. In a few countries we find that permanent servants are considered members of the family for which they work because of the assistance which they give to the family in carrying out its functions. This latter situation is not usual in the United States. 

The primary purpose and function of marriage and the family is the procreation and the education of children. Unlike animals, children cannot care for themselves at an early age. They need many years of training and care before they can earn their own livelihood, and only where there is permanent union of husband and wife is this care assured. For the upbringing of children, therefore, and hence for the continuance of society in the world (the chief purpose of marriage) the family is essential. The family is, indeed, the most important agent of social control. 

Within the family the chief purpose of married life is to provide for the mutual development and perfection of husband and wife and their children. Happy family life is the basis of stable social life in general.

No other society on earth provides such an intimate face-to-face association as does the family within the home. The family is not only a fundamental social institution and natural society, therefore, but also a primary group. 

Do we realize the importance of the home?

A home is a place where the members of a family live together. 

It is much more than a mere house: four walls, space to move about in, furniture, and ornaments. Home includes a spirit of affection and comradeship, as well as physical surroundings. It is the inner sanctuary of the family, and it is the spirit of the members, rather than its physical features, which makes it such. A true home offers the most comfortable and satisfying of all social relationships for parents and adult members, and the happiest of all places for children. Some rich people with a very favorable external environment for family living have little home life at all, and their family is a failure as a well-knit social organization. Some very poor people, with a miserable physical abode, have a very excellent family home life.

Dwelling prepared for a married couple in the Bismarck Archipelago. Marriage customs vary among different peoples, but among all it is subject to some social control.

There are three forms of marriage and family life: monogamy, polyandry, and polygyny. 

The form of marriage and family life to which we are accustomed is known as monogamy and is found in all social groups. Two other forms prevail, however, and it is useful to know about them. 

First, let us describe monogamy. Strict monogamy exists where a family is formed by the marriage of but one man and one woman at the same time, and where marriage of one of the partners with a second person is allowed only on the death of the first partner. 

Where the majority of families in a community are monogamous but where, nevertheless, marriage of one of the partners with a second person while the first partner still lives is not considered offensive to good morals, then dominant monogamy prevails. In other words, dominant monogamy exists where married people can legally divorce their partner and marry another, as is allowed in most countries today.

The two other types of marriage and the family are expressed by the general term polygamy, meaning plural marriage, which includes both polyandry and polygyny. 

Polyandry is a form of marriage where one woman marries several men, often brothers, at the same time. This type of marriage and the family which results, is comparatively rare, and where it exists it does so usually because one man alone could not afford the upkeep of a family, and needs to share the expense with others; or because there is a desire to preserve property undivided, as formerly in Ceylon and among the ancient Spartans; or because women are scarce for one reason or another. Where polyandry exists, usually the richer classes are monogamous. 

Polygyny is the marriage of one husband to several wives, as is, for example, sanctioned under the Mohammedan law. 

The family as a natural social institution should be monogamous. 

Sociologists are generally agreed that the best form of family is that which results from a monogamous marriage. To carry out the

primary functions of marriage, a continuing relationship is essential, because children need the care of parents for many years before they become able to take their place in the world with developed characters, possessing the knowledge and skills essential for earning a living. Since more than one child is usually born to husband and wife, this fact prolongs the need of their staying together as husband and wife. Strict monogamy, which involves lifelong partnership, is the ideal for the satisfaction of true affection, for economic security, for the proper upbringing of children, who need the influence of both parents in their training, and for the protection of the mother during her childbearing years.2

In general, this ideal family social unit which results from a monogamous marriage would not be so widespread if polyandry or polygyny prevailed. A consideration of polygamous family life leads us to the definite conclusion for natural reasons alone, that God must have intended human beings to found monogamous families. 

Let us examine the situation in greater detail. 

Polyandry is so comparatively rare that it is obviously not a natural situation. Besides, the sexes are born more or less in equal numbers, and if polyandry prevailed, large numbers of women would be forced to remain unmarried, and there would not be enough children to maintain, much less to increase, the population. 

Polygyny, which is much more widespread, does not interfere with the primary end of marriage; that is, with the begetting of children and rearing them in the family. Yet, polygyny could not be a universal natural institution for several reasons. First of all, the polygynous family is not usually so well knit as the monogamous household: the bond of mutual affection between parents and children is usually somewhat lessened when a husband has several wives, and it is certainly not so great as in a happy monogamous family. In the second place, whenever the sexes are more or less equal in number, as they usually are, large numbers of men would 2 Apart from sociological studies, of course, we have clear Scriptural authority that God intends the family to be monogamous (cf. Gen. 2:24; Matt. 19:4-9).

be obliged to remain unmarried, and this is not a normal situation; similarly, if a woman could get a husband for herself, she would not usually wish to become a second or third wife of a man already married. Finally, polygyny presupposes that the man have a certain amount of wealth, to be able to maintain several wives and their children, unless the economic organization of the group includes a preponderance of woman and child labor, in which case, of course, a large number of wives and children would be an asset. 

The monogamous family is a basic social institution, and provides many important functions: biologic, social and spiritual, educative, protective and affectional, and economic.

The continuance of the human race and its culture is the basic fact behind the need of permanent marriage relations within the monogamous family. The transmission of physical life from one generation to the next is, of course, a biologic function. But marriage and the family have many other important functions as well. The family is especially important in bringing up children, educating and training them. As we said before, the family is the most important agent of social control within society. It also provides protection for a mother and her children. It provides all the members of the family-husband, wife, and children—with the stability of affection which we need. It provides a primary face-to-face group for recreation, in addition to the important work of education and training. It forms an economic unit, too.

If the citizens of a nation belong to happy, stable families, in which they have learned the meaning of the give and take of life in society, and have become grounded in high ideals and the culture of the group by means of family social control, then will the nation as a whole be sound and stable, composed of law-abiding, worthy citizens, willing to contribute to the social good because they hold a true affection for the land in which their home is situated. Sound family life, therefore, provides a spiritual function not only of vital importance to the individuals who compose it, but also of very great value to the whole people of a territory, organized as a State.

In earlier times the family was the sole factor in the upbringing of the future citizen. There was the patriarchal family, where the father was the head not only of his immediate family, but also of the families of his grown-up sons and daughters. Abraham and other early Biblical characters provide us with an illustration of the power of the patriarchal family head. The father of this extended family was responsible for everything, and members received from him directly, or from other members at the father's command, all that was necessary for their spiritual, mental, moral, and physical growth. He was the early ancestral priest, and the older members of the family cooperated for the education and all the other needs of the younger members. Although the family is no longer such a selfcontained unit, and although it now delegates part of its functions to the Church, the State, and the school, nevertheless, it is in the family alone that the child receives his care and training in his very earliest and most important years. The family, then, is still the basic social institution; and although it may delegate some of its functions, the child who is reared entirely outside the family circle is sorely handicapped in later life, in competition with those who have had the advantage of good parents and adequate home care. Let us, therefore, examine the functions of the family a little more closely. 

The family is the most important group which educates and trains the child. It is a religious and a moral school. 

The work of the parents does not end with the birth of the child. 

Children need many years of loving care and training before they can take their place in the world as well-integrated adult citizens, able to support themselves. To bring up children in an adequate human manner involves education and training. In the family the child receives its initiation into the culture of the group, and it is through the family that much of the social heritage of the past is preserved and handed on from one generation to the next. The child's character needs to be formed, so that the undesirable predispositions, which he has received by inheritance, may be eradicated

Dome C 

Ewing Galloway

The family is the most important agent of social control. Happy family life is the basis of stable social life in general. and his good points developed. He needs to be taught good habits, to acquire emotional stability, and to develop a sense of responsibility as a member of society. In the family the child first learns the demands of social life; here he comes to realize that living in society involves giving as well as receiving, and that in addition to rights, one has also duties toward others. From his earliest years, his mental ability and manual dexterity can also be trained within the family. 

As regards the most important instruction of all — that in religion and moral conduct - although the Church is the society founded to occupy itself officially with the teachings and truths of religion and right conduct, the family is, nevertheless, the first religious school to which the child belongs. Even though the school and the Church see

to it that in later life a child is taught his spiritual and moral obligations, if parents misguide a child, either positively in their teaching or indirectly by bad example, the child will usually adopt their attitude rather than follow the precepts taught outside the home. Children whose parents do not worship God, and who have not been taught by them to say their prayers and to act rightly from supernatural motives, do not usually grow up to have a true recognition of their duties to God and their moral duties toward their neighbor. 

The child is under the environmental influence of the home for a longer period than under that of any other environment. Hence the family exercises the greatest influence in shaping the child in attitudes, ideals, and conduct, and the child's interests, ambitions, hopes, fears, prejudices are all strongly influenced by those with whom he is most closely associated — the members of his family. 

The family is an important group for the protection and security of its members, and for the true development of human affection. 

All the members of the family, both parents and children, need the security which can be found within the family. A good family will provide a home which ensures the well-being of all the members: a place where they will meet true affection and sympathy, and will secure the peace, rest, and recreation which will give them the physical and mental health and strength needed to cope with any difficult situations which they may meet in social relationships outside the home. The close union of parents who love one another, and their children, leads to a development of family affection which is one of the best and most satisfying joys which man can experience. 

Happy family life is indeed a social ideal. If the family is well knit by ties of mutual affection, intimate social help, and happy memories of pleasant times spent together, the members will be happy, well-developed social people, with a true social anchor in their home. 

The family is the primary economic unit. 

Man must work in order to live, and to live adequately not only must he obtain food, clothing, and shelter, but the food must be

prepared, the clothing must be made and kept in repair, and the home must be maintained in a clean, orderly condition. The members of the family also need special attention in illness, childhood, and old age. From the earliest times, man divided labor among the various members of the family. The wife took care of the home and the cooking and looked after the children. Sometimes she also tilled the soil and wove the clothing from wool or flax. The husband hunted, or occupied himself with agriculture, or gained a livelihood in other ways. Even up to comparatively recent times, industry was carried on in the home, so that the family was united in its mutual gaining of a livelihood and in caring for its needs. However, although in present times the husband usually works away from the home, the family is still the most important economic unit of society, because it is the family which creates the demand for the goods made in industry; and likewise because it is for the upkeep of the family that the majority of men perform their daily work. There are few persons in this world who earn their livelihood for themselves alone and not as part of a family unit. 

Relations between members of the family should be clearly defined and harmonious. 

The home is the inner sanctuary of all social life, and true home life should be one of mutual peace and harmony, and of love and charity between parents and children.

As every society needs within it an authority, to make final decisions on important controversial issues, the authority in the family is usually conceded to the father. This is both the Christian and the 

Jewish viewpoint. St. Paul expressed this clearly: "Let women be subject to their husbands, as to the Lord," he said. The reason for the advisability of this is clear. A man is not handicapped physically and emotionally by childbearing; his greater physical strength enables him to protect the wife and child; generally speaking, the man has a greater opportunity of weighing all sides to an issue. 'Eph. 5:22.

because usually the man travels more widely outside the home than the woman.

On the other hand, it is neither the Christian nor the modern viewpoint that the man should exercise his authority in a selfish manner. Woman's position is an important and a dignified one. St. 

Paul did not teach that a woman was to be subject to her husband as a servant, but as a companion. Immediately following the above quotation, he added: "Husbands, love your wives, as Christ also loved the Church, and delivered Himself up for it. . . even thus ought husbands to love their wives as their own bodies. He that loveth his own wife loveth himself." 994

Family life involves relationships not only between husband and wife, but also between parents and children. Obviously, since it is the parents' duty to educate the children, the latter must obey them and trust in their superior wisdom. This duty of love and reverence of children for their parents does not cease with adult life, although when grave injustice is done to them, children are, of course, released from their duty of obedience even when underage. "Honor thy father and thy mother" is one of the Ten Commandments.

On the other hand, good parents strive to maintain an intelligent parent-child relationship. They bring up their children in a wise discipline, remembering that their children have personalities as well as they, and that their natural rights should be respected. St. 

Paul clearly expresses the Christian ideal: "Children, obey in the 

Lord your parents, for this is just. . . . And ye fathers, provoke not your children to anger but rear them in the discipline and admonitions of the Lord." ...

Only when parents and children recognize the mutual rights and duties of each member are family relations truly harmonious. In so far as members of a family fail to fulfill their duties in this regard, the family will fail in its true purpose, the members will be worth 'Eph. 5:25, 28.

Helping mother. From earliest times man divided labor among the various members of the family. Children, today, who are encouraged to help about the house, will take more interest in the home. less as social beings, as citizens of their State, and the State itself will be that much less stable and secure. 

To ensure harmonious family life, people ought to be very careful in their choice of a life partner. 

At one time, parents chose the partner for their children usually according to their common-sense appraisal of the candidate considered as suitable. Generally speaking, good parents chose as a partner for their child, a man or woman whose family was congenial to theirs, and who had approximately the same cultural background. 

Today, this is changed, and young people prefer to find their own mate in life, according to the dictates of what is called "romantic

love." This method has many points in its favor, but it has one great drawback. Often people who fall in love with each other fail to realize that marriage involves lifelong partnership, and that much more than the ability to "make love" in a romantic fashion is needed for a wife to manage a household satisfactorily, and for a man to carry out his duties as husband and father. One cannot take out all one's stakes in the past, and live without any reference to one's former character and social setting. Young people who consider marriage must be much more intelligent than was necessary when parents did the choosing for their children. They must appraise each other and the cultural background of each as objectively as possible, to determine whether or not happiness would result from their living together and founding a family. Catholics will want to take to heart the advice of Pope Pius XI, who wrote a letter for them on marriage, and said these wise words about the necessity of prudence and foresight in the choice of a life partner :6 "To the proximate preparation of a good married life belongs very specially the care in choosing a partner. . . . And so that they may not deplore for the rest of their lives the sorrows arising from an indiscreet marriage, those about to enter into wedlock should carefully deliberate in choosing the person with whom henceforward they must live continually: they should, in so deliberating, keep before their minds the thought first of God and of true religion of Christ, then of themselves, of their partner, of the children to come, as also of human and civil society, for which wedlock is a fountainhead. Let them diligently pray for divine help, so that they make their choice in accordance with Christian prudence, not indeed led by the blind and unrestrained impulse of lust, nor by any desire of riches or other base influence, but by a true and noble love and by a sincere affection for the future partner; and let them strive in their married life for those ends for which marriage was constituted by 

God. Lastly, let them not omit to ask the prudent advice of their "Pope Pius XI, Casti Connubii, On Christian Marriage, pp. 41-42. (Five Great 

Encyclicals, pp. 112-113.)

parents with regard to the partner, and let them regard this advice in no light manner, in order that by their mature knowledge and experience of human affairs they may guard against a disastrous choice and, on the threshold of matrimony may receive more abundantly the divine blessing of the Fourth Commandment."

Chapter Seven  The purpose of this chapter is: 

1. To realize that family life in America today is often far from ideal. 

2. To learn about the defects in our social organization for family living, and to discuss the remedies for them. 

3. To understand the natural as well as the moral reasons why divorce is a great evil. 

Considerable changes have developed in family life within recent years.

Many changes have occurred in family and home life within recent years. Some of these changes have been useful and satisfactory and we should not wish to go back to former ways of living. 

Many others, however, seem to be detrimental to family life rather than beneficial to it. First, let us consider improvements which have been made. In general, these are all of a material type, and chiefly benefit women and children. For example, women are much less household drudges than formerly, and have much more time for intellectual development and for recreation. The complicated housework required by heavy furnishings in the homes, a multiplicity of ornaments, complicated clothing, and the like, which used to characterize our American living, has been eliminated. Modern houses are built to involve a minimum of housework. Many inventions have been made in laborsaving devices for the home: electricity or gas usually takes the place of kerosine lamps, and one can make use of such appliances as electric vacuum cleaners, washing machines, toasters, mixers, coffee percolators, and the like. The average woman of today is much better educated than in former times, her voting 100

right often gives her an interest in current affairs, and her interest and skill in sports often make her much more capable of being a true companion to her husband. The average worker's home usually has more material comforts in the rooms themselves (though a garden is generally lacking). Children have little or no work to do, either in the household or to earn their own living: this is to their advantage in some respects, but in others it is a handicap to their development as we shall later explain. 

In general, present-day conditions of family life are not ideal. 

Despite the benefits just named, family life within the home has been injured rather than improved by the many drastic moral, economic, and social changes which have taken place. Many families lack the former unity and affectionate cohesion which should be found in a family group. There are fewer children, or sometimes none at all, to cement the parents' love and to provide a bond of family unity that will hold through all the misunderstandings that inevitably occur in any social group. Economic and social changes have taken place which have adversely affected the family. 

Generalizations about family life are difficult to make, for family relations are, of their nature, such that outside appearances do not always reveal the true situation. Nevertheless, many cases of desertion, nonsupport, separation, and divorce take place. Desertion means a prolonged absence from home without the consent of the marriage partner, and is usually confined to low-income families who cannot afford the costs of securing a legal divorce. Nonsupport is a technical term which usually means that though the husband is at home, he either cannot or will not make economic provision for his family. Separation is a legal arrangement under which a husband and wife agree to live apart with the sanction of the court, without the right to marry again. Divorce is a legal permission for husband and wife to consider their marriage contract to be broken, so that they can marry again. Divorce is permitted by civil law but not by the divine law.

Divorce statistics show that many families have been completely disrupted by divorce in the United States, and a comparison of these statistics with those in other countries seems to show that family life in the United States is less united than in other countries. It is clear that we must give very serious consideration to the problems of family life in America, to see what can be done to improve the situation as rapidly as possible.

Changes in religious ideals have led to less ideal family relationships.

With the growth of a materialistic outlook upon life—that is, an undue regard for material riches and for "having a good time" 2

Computed by Strachey, R., "Current Social Statistics: Marriage and Divorce,"

religion has occupied a less important place in the lives of many people. This situation has led to a forgetfulness of religious ideals, to a lack of true understanding of the holiness of family life and of marriage as a life calling and to a disregard of the mutual duties and obligations of parents and children.

Family life entails many sacrifices, and with the waning of religious ideals has come a decline in the spirit of unselfishness, so necessary on the part of all members of a family for the maintenance of family unity and love. Disregard for the teachings of religion has led to an increase of selfishness, a decline in filial respect, a diminution in parents of the sense of responsibility toward their children as charges for whom they must answer to God, and to a lack of reverence toward the sacredness of family life.

Many people who would like to be good citizens forget that the family is the primary unit of society, and that if it fails to maintain high ideals, the nation will inevitably suffer. Children who are not brought up in a spirit of reverence toward their parents, and are not taught to understand the necessity of the right type of social control, are not inclined to obey authority in other quarters, either civil or social. Children and parents who do not exercise a sense of social responsibility and cooperation within the home, cannot be expected to be good and useful members of society, reverencing and recognizing the social rights of others, and fulfilling their social obligations in an unselfish manner. Most cities now have special courts to deal with problems of domestic relations, family quarrels and dissensions, and cases of a man's nonsupport of wife and children.

We must not fail to note, however, that the good qualities of our American family life are all founded upon the Christian ideals on the subject which were brought to us from European countries by past generations, and firmly established in our social organization and in our laws. Monogamy is still the ideal form of marriage and the family; separation, divorce, and desertion are still considered as

#12 

Courtesy Wisconsin Electric Power Co. 

Some of the time- and laborsaving devices in use in many American homes. 

In 1920 there were 1,800,000 electrical appliances (exclusive of radios and refrigerators) in use in the country. In 1941 there were 117,000,000 of them in use. deviations from the ideal norm. We still insist that marriage should be a public contract, duly attested and registered. Births and marriages are always registered by the State, and children born to parents out of wedlock are subject to certain legal disabilities. 

Economic changes have made family living conditions less ideal from the social viewpoint. 

A comparison of family life in the United States today with family life some years ago, shows that economic changes have had a considerable influence upon life within the home. Just prior to the growth of capitalistic economic organization, all the members of a family cooperated much more closely in earning a family livelihood, in what was known as the domestic system of economic life. The majority of people were not herded together in cities, as now, but lived by the cultivation of the soil. Even those who were engaged in industry carried on their occupation in or about the home. Thus, all day long, the family was united; parents and children worked and played together. There were, to be sure, many defects in the system; children usually worked at too early an age, and the working conditions of the home were often not up to our modern sanitary standards. Nevertheless, the economic and social interest of all

the members of the family were identical. Mutual helpfulness, cooperation, and trust were the results. 

The industrial revolution effected a change from family production to production in workshops and factories. As a result, families congregated in towns, to be nearer the father's business. This, in turn, not only led to overcrowded housing conditions and an unnatural family life, but it broke up the interdependence and unity of the family circle. The average father, at the present time, is obliged to spend the greater part of the day outside the home in order to gain a livelihood. When he returns after his day's work, he is too tired to take full advantage of the enjoyment he might derive from the society of his family. The wife, also, frequently seeks remunerative employment outside the home, leaving the children for long periods to their own devices. It is very difficult for a woman to be occupationally employed outside the home, and still make her home a comfortable haven, retaining her true position as the spiritual pivot, as it were, of the family life. Often working wives and mothers fail in this respect. Yet, of course, it is also true that sometimes, when a wife works outside the home, the members of the family help each other even more than they otherwise would the husband taking his share of the housework, with the children helping too.

Married women sometimes work because home life is irksome to them, but usually they do so because the husband does not earn a living wage. People who live in the city often find that the wife cannot contribute as much to the family earnings as would be possible in the country. Instead of being able to supplement her husband's earnings by making the family's clothing, soap, canned provisions, and many other household supplies, many city wives find that their household accommodation is too limited to permit of such activities. A family living wage for city people, therefore, may conceivably need to be very much more in actual money payment than a living wage for a family in the country. There is no question that in our modern capitalistic system a married man's wage for a

Courtesy Wisconsin Electric Power Co. 

In 1920 there were 10,000 electric refrigerators in the country; in 1941, 14,240,000 of them are in use. full day's work should be sufficient to maintain a family in reasonable comfort. In Chapter Ten, on Conditions in Industry, this question is discussed at greater length. 

Another economic feature of home life at the present time is the use of mechanized appliances in conjunction with smaller-sized living quarters. The result is that children have fewer tasks to perform around the house, to aid the mother, and therefore the spirit of cooperation which would be fostered by such duties is often not developed.

Housing conditions are important for the development of happy family life and the preservation of health and morality. 

The living quarters of the family, its immediate physical environment, provide the place where members may meet in privacy, enjoy each other's company, and develop a normal family social life. 

Good housing was defined by housing experts as follows: "Social welfare demands for every family a safe and sanitary home; healthful surroundings; ample and pure running water inside the house; modern and sanitary toilet conveniences for its exclusive use, located inside the building; adequate sunlight and ventilation; reasonable fire protection; privacy; rooms of sufficient size and number to decently house the members of the family; freedom from dampness; prompt, adequate collection of all waste materials. These fundamental requirements for normal living should be obtainable by every family, reasonably accessible from place of employment, at a rental not exceeding twenty per cent of the family income."³ 

Members of a family are usually considered to be "decently housed" when there are at least the same number of rooms in the house or apartment as there are members of the family, with separate sleeping accommodation for unmarried members of the opposite sex. Of course, this does not mean that everyone must have a private room. One of the rooms will probably be a kitchen, and another room may well be used exclusively as a sitting room or dining room. Adequate housing does not necessarily mean a private house with a garden, but it does at least include the provision of near-by open spaces, with supervised recreation grounds for small children. In most cities, indeed, better housing facilities exist where space has been saved by the erection of apartment houses, if there are adequate parks and playing fields near by, than where onefamily dwellings with small gardens or none at all take up all the available space. 'Proceedings of the National Conference of Charities and Correction, Cleveland, 1912, pp. 391-392.

Good housing also includes the provision of adequately wide streets and sidewalks, with sufficient lighting; it includes transportation facilities near at hand; protection where level railroad crossings exist; and an absence of dangerously unsafe, abandoned buildings in the neighborhood where children might play or vagrants gather. 

The effects of bad housing conditions upon the social life of the family are very many. Although physical environment does not make the home, it is the shell, as it were, within which the home functions. Physical surroundings have an important psychological influence on almost everyone. It is difficult not to feel depressed in drab surroundings, particularly in slum areas where the streets are dreary, and where almost incessant noise comes from crowded streets and neighboring rooms. Frayed nerves and quarrels are the almost inevitable result, leading to unhappy family relations and often to the breakup of the home.

Morality frequently suffers under bad housing conditions. It is difficult for parents to teach children good habits and high standards of cleanliness, tidiness, and respect for the property of other members of the family, in overcrowded and badly equipped rooms. 

Even if parents succeed in making the home joyful and happy, the children will be obliged to go elsewhere for the recreation facilities which must necessarily be lacking in their own home surroundings. 

Many children, with no joyful home to attract them, with the street as their playground and bad companions as their playmates, grow up undisciplined, and this inevitably leads to a lack of true harmonious family living. Adults may foolishly turn to drink, gambling, and the use of narcotics as a temporary distraction from their surroundings, and all such vices lead to character difficulties and unhappy family situations.

Many families in the United States live under substandard housing conditions.

Students from modern residential districts of our cities may think that every American family lives in a house with hot and cold water

butonus folged silo

Good housing is very important for health and morality. This picture shows housing in one of our large American cities before and after a slum clearance project. Note the great difference in general surroundings. supply, with one or more bathrooms, with electric light, near wellcleaned, well-lighted, and paved streets, and with all the other amenities which are listed under good housing needs. Actually, this is not the real situation at all. 

In many cities of the United States there are families living in rooms which have no heating or plumbing, where sunlight never penetrates because they are inside rooms without windows or because the windows are too close to tall adjoining buildings. Often each room has to serve an entire family for the triple function of kitchen, parlor, and bedroom. The word slum is the term given to

an area where the majority of the homes are below minimum standards for decent living. Frequently public authorities add injustice to the lot of slum dwellers by bad provision for street lighting, street cleaning, and garbage disposal. Refuse odors are therefore added to the air already polluted by smoke and gasoline fumes from house chimneys, factories, and automobiles.

Sometimes conditions in single-family houses in the country may be almost as bad as those we have just described. The houses may be mere tumble-down shacks, with ill-fitting windows and middle rooms with no windows at all; with no heating except from a stove or delapidated open fireplace, and the damp cold air of winter coming through many an airhole in the wall or floor; with no indoor toilet facilities, and water obtainable only from a distant well. In the country, however, the human spirit is not so stultified as in the city, for there is fresh air to breathe and often beautiful surroundings and the other advantages of country life. 

Recently the Federal Government has co-operated with local and state authorities in an extensive housing program, including rural housing improvements through the Farm Security Administration. 

City conditions have had a detrimental effect on family life. 

We have already stated that where men work in cities, away from the family circle, the unity of the family is affected. We have also shown the detrimental effect of bad housing conditions whether in the city or in the country. City life is especially harmful to the unity and harmony of the family unless the members of the family take great care to overcome difficulties. For example, recreation is essential for man's well-being, and yet in city slums, and even in the apartment houses of the well to do, facilities for healthful recreation are limited, if not wholly absent. Members of the family, therefore, must spend their leisure hours largely outside the home. For the satisfaction of their recreational needs they are, indeed, for the most part at the mercy of commercial undertakings. Not only do morals frequently suffer as a consequence, but the interdependence

SANTE CHEESE 

Acme The kitchen of a little better type of slum dwelling. Notice the bathtub in the corner of the room. Many of our citizens actually live under conditions like this. of the various members of the family, already affected by the economic system, decreases still more.

The provision of commercialized recreation outside the home and the lack of normal family life, have also led to great restlessness among the population. People crave for ever increasing pleasure and enjoyment, and the mother and other members of the family become less and less attached to the home. Family life and the moral training of the children necessarily suffer. 

City life has led to other evils, too. Social competition has brought about the fact that a person's worth is judged more often by the wealth he possesses or appears to possess, than by his moral quali-

ties. Thrift and frugality have become almost forgotten virtues in many city homes, and thus the family becomes less and less united, and dissensions and disputes may be more frequent. 

Of course, it must be noted too, that country people may be equally imbued with the desire for commercialized recreation and the acquisition of material goods, and young country people frequently disrupt their home life by going to live in the city, hoping thereby to secure greater material satisfaction in life. Those who are interested in improving social life in the country are trying to remedy this situation by improving the school curricula, by showing country people the greater solid advantages of country living for true happiness and contentment as well as usefulness in this world, and also by providing local recreational facilities, libraries, and other attractions which many country districts lack. 

Insufficient preparation and training for married life, and insufficient prudence and foresight in choosing a partner are also frequent causes of domestic disunion. 

Economic life, at present lived mostly outside the family circle, in factory, workshop, office, or store, has had an extremely harmful effect on the preparation of children by parents for marriage and child rearing. Away at work for the greater part of the day adolescent boys and girls do not learn the requisites for homemaking. The family certainly has many more chances of success if the man can turn his hand to household matters when occasion demands. But this training is essential for a woman, on whom homemaking more naturally devolves. An adequate knowledge of how to manage a house and care for husband and children is not easily learned, and the learning requires more time than is normally possible under present-day industrial conditions. Yet in some aspects, modern housekeeping requires more training than formerly, for in place of the more or less trustworthy local dealers, the modern woman must deal with an almost bewildering number of labeled brands of goods whose real worth is often obscured by misleading advertising matter. 

Again, although every mother needs time for recreation, never-

theless, where she is occupied in the home for the greater part of the day, she provides a family bond and earns an appreciation from her husband and offspring which normally will not lead to a disrupted home. It is to be noted, however, that even where a girl does not go out to earn her livelihood before marriage, but stays at home, the cramped living quarters of many modern homes, especially in cities, the laborsaving devices, and other amenities of modern life obviate any thorough training in domestic duties. This means the probable elimination of the spirit of cooperation which such duties foster. It means, too, that women have more leisure time at their disposal which, to be pleasantly employed, is often spent outside the home. 

Such a situation weakens another bond of family union. 

Our present school system is also not wholly blameless in the matter of insufficient domestic training in early years. Although the extension of education for all is highly desirable, nevertheless family unity was much more stable and enduring when the child was at home during a great part of the day. His presence at home from his earliest years also insured an early training in domestic activities. 

A remedy, of course, lies in the school giving more attention to studies which develop homemaking qualities, both moral and manual. 

The need of prudence and foresight in choosing a life partner was discussed in the preceding chapter. If young people paid more attention to this need, many possibly unsuccessful marriages might be averted.

Smaller families have sometimes led to domestic disunion. 

The decline in the birth rate in the United States has been very noticeable within recent years. There are now fewer children in the average family than formerly, as the statistics given show. Children cement parental love, and oftentimes preserve unity in a family when the parents may temporarily disagree. Divorces are not so lightly undertaken if there are children to be thought of, as they are if there is only one child to arrange for, or none at all.

SIZE OF AVERAGE FAMILY IN THE UNITED STATES¹ Number of Persons per Family Year 

1860 1870 1880 1890 1900 1910 1920 1930 1940 (including Parents) 5.3 5.1 5.0 4.93 4.69 4.54 4.34 

The reasons for the decline in the birth rate are many. It is partly due to the fact that women marry later today than in former times, and so will naturally have fewer children. For some biological reason, perhaps, women will have fewer children than before the sports they go in for and their generally more active life may well lead to such a situation. However, some people have small families consciously, and use restraint in marital relations so as to have fewer children. Their reasons are many: sometimes it is to give children a better education, which is costly; sometimes it is because they feel that they cannot afford more children. This latter reason is often given. At one time, many children went to work at a very early age, ten or even less, and they might even help in lucrative homework or in the fields earlier than this. They therefore added to the family income rather soon, and the more children a family had, the better off it was. Today, in many states, children must stay in school until they are 18 years of age, often they cannot find employment when they have left school, and so they are an expense to the family instead of an economic asset. Some people have fewer children because they are selfish, and find children a handicap to their freedom. If parents use ordinary restraint, there is nothing 

"Fifteenth Census of the U. S. Population," Vol. VI, U. S. Dept. of Commerce, 

Bureau of the Census, 1933, p. 10. 1940 figure an official estimate issued March 

22, 1941.

necessarily wrong in their not wanting larger families. Wrong methods of restraint, however, are immoral. Of course, we must not think that every married couple who have few or no children do not want more. Some people do not seem intended to have children, or seem destined by nature to have very few. 

The number of divorces has increased greatly in modern times and has led to many social problems. 

Sometimes people make errors in their choice of a life partner, and for various reasons it may be advisable for husband and wife to separate. Separation is not divorce. As we explained earlier in the chapter, divorce is a permission granted by civil law to married people to break their marriage contract and to marry again if they wish to do so.

Although divorce is contrary to the moral law, it is permitted in the United States, and some states such as Nevada allow divorce very easily. We showed earlier in the chapter that there are more divorces in proportion to marriages in the United States than in any other country in the world.

The causes of this greater divorce rate are many. Chief among them seems to be selfishness and a lack of training in emotional control and general stability; a lack, too, of ideals of family stability brought about by a change in public opinion on the subject of broken homes and by the growing neglect of church attendance. 

The fact that women work outside the home is also a cause of divorce in some cases. Their greater financial independence leads them to disregard reasons which should keep them at home, and sometimes they crave excitement to take away the fatigue of work. 

Insufficient training for married life leads to some divorces: many a girl who has worked outside the home previous to her marriage, has never learned the intricacies of good housekeeping. Frictions resulting from too cramped housing quarters may also lead to divorce. Another cause is lack of prudence and foresight in choosing a partner: arranged marriages seem to be proportionately more suc-

cessful than when people marry of their own free choice! Childless families or families with only one or a few children seem to be subject to divorce, for the parents do not feel so "tied" as they would if they had the welfare of a greater number of persons to think of. For example, of the number of people divorced in 1932, 55.4 per cent had no children, 21.7 per cent had one child, and a further 9.9 per cent had only two children.5

Unhappy family life may also be the direct result of lax divorce laws and marriage legislation. Some states do not make sufficiently strict legislation to control marriage. Unhappy marriages are much more frequent when the legal ceremony is very easily arranged, without the safeguards which are afforded by the publication of banns, by requiring parental consent for those under age, and by a certain minimum time of residence in the district. Similarly, when divorces are easily obtained, husband and wife may be tempted to give rein to their emotions and to seek divorce on very inadequate pretexts. If it is difficult to secure a divorce, there is much more likelihood that quarrels and dissensions will be patched up when time has softened the misunderstanding. 

The effects of divorce are chiefly emotional, so far as husband and wife are concerned, though this may lead to serious mental as well as moral disorders. There is certainly much greater peace and security in marriage and family life if neither party has to fear that one will be unfaithful, secure a divorce, and leave the other unsupported in old age. The chief sufferers from divorce are the children, for through it they are deprived of the loving care and help of normal family life. The influence of both parents is ordinarily needed for the complete development of the child: the mother's influence is, perhaps, the stronger in early childhood, and the father's during the adolescent period, but the influence of both with their differing outlooks and temperaments, is needed for full development. Some children of broken homes survive the difficulty 

"Marriage and Divorce," Eleventh Annual Report, 1932, U. S. Dept. of 

Commerce, 1934, p. 6.

well enough, but many others entirely fail to receive the necessary training for useful social and economic life. Still others, unable to respect their parents, develop a disrespect for the law. Only too often the cause of juvenile delinquency, crime, and poverty may be traced to the lack of normal home life in early years. 

Besides being contrary to the moral law, divorce is, therefore, to be deplored because of the social disunity and social problems which it brings about. The former fact, however, must not be forgotten. 

Marriage is not a private contract to be left to the consent and good pleasure of the contracting parties, and to be dissolved for any reason whatsoever. It is a social obligation freely undertaken by the parties because of the need which God has implanted in human beings for married life and for domestic unity and love. It was shown in the preceding chapter that the family was intended by God to be monogamous, and that polygamy is never best for society as a whole. Divorce is merely a legal permission for a form of polygamy, and is thus contrary to the natural law of monogamy. 

The present permission given by the State for divorce is, therefore, contrary to man's natural welfare. 

The Church does not sanction divorce," and it is claimed that her attitude inflicts a severe hardship on those who wish to break the marriage bond. It is true that it may seem unjust that a marriage contract cannot be dissolved, if afterward the parties find their characters to be unsuited for the life partnership of marriage. 

Nevertheless, the Church cannot, for the sake of benefit to a few, change a precept which is grounded in the natural law, in the wel- Sometimes people do not realize the difference between the meaning of the terms divorce and annulment. In general, an annulment is a declaration by a 

Church court that the parties are not, and have not been, married. Thus if two persons thought that they were married, and later discovered or suspected that they were not, then the Church would examine their case, and if they actually had not been married, would declare the marriage null. Very few decrees of annulment are granted. The Church never allows a divorce, except as a legal convenience in the eyes of the State, and then it is considered by the Church simply as a separation and not as a permission to remarry.

fare of society, and on the solemn words of Christ Himself." In certain cases, to prevent a worse evil, the Church allows married people to separate, that is, to live apart from each other; she may even allow them to secure a legal divorce, but neither one can remarry during the lifetime of the other partner, even though this would be allowed by the State. 

The family is so important a part of harmonious social life that there is an urgent need for a re-establishment of higher ideals regarding the family.

It will be evident from the foregoing discussion of some of the causes of present-day family problems, that a re-establisment of higher ideals regarding the family is essential to the true progress of society. We might summarize by saying that the first requisite for reform is the restoration of religious ideals and a true comprehension of the dignity of successful married life and of parenthood. 

When this dignity is realized once more, preparation for marriage will become more adequate, greater prudence will be used in selecting a partner, divorce will not be considered as an honorable way out of a domestic difficulty, parents and children will come to regard the home as a home in the true sense of the word, and will be less inclined to seek companionship and amusement elsewhere. 

To attain all this the sure remedy would be the restoration of religious principles. Not only would such a remedy affect man's aspirations and his outlook, but his family life would be improved in other ways. Other factors needed for bringing about more satisfactory family life are the payment of a family living wage to every married man, sufficient to maintain his wife and children in the home; the improvement of housing conditions; education in how to make the home attractive to its members by the provision of recreation and family hobbies and other joint activities. 1. Discuss some of the changes which have taken place in family life since the advent of apartment houses. 

2. What is the meaning of the term, desertion, nonsupport, separation, divorce? 

3. How has a lessened interest in religion led to less ideal family relationships? 

4. How, if at all, has capitalism affected family life? 

5. Why are housing conditions and family life so intimately connected? 

6. What is meant by "good housing"? 

7. Discuss the ways by which family life may be benefited by the situation of the home in a rural area. 

8. If it is true to say that city conditions have had a detrimental effect upon family life, why would it be incorrect to say that all city conditions have this effect?

How would this be a good preparation for successful married life later?

12. Is there any social advantage to the observance of "Mother's day" and "Father's day"? Discuss. 

13. Why is education for family life essential in modern times? 

14. Why would it be incorrect to say that all families with one child or none at all would lack Christian harmony? 

15. What are the social problems caused by divorce?

18. Why does the Church sometimes allow separation? 

19. What is meant by the term, annulment? How does this differ from divorce?

21. Why do some classes of people resort to separation rather than to divorce? To desertion rather than divorce? 

22. Why should a young couple just starting married life be very careful in their choice of area of residence? 

23. Write an essay on the social advantages and disadvantages of the "career woman."

Chapter Eight  The purpose of this chapter is: 

1. To realize man's need to form a religious society to worship God, and to realize the essential importance of the Catholic Church. 

2. To realize the important social function which is performed by religious groups. 

3. To understand something of the organization of religious societies in the United States. 

4. To learn the organization of the Catholic Church and some of its major functions in the United States today. 

Religion is the expression of man's duty to his Creator. It is a social duty most fully expressed through the Church. 

Religious society, or the church group, is a society formed for the social worship of the Creator. It is to be found everywhere among men, even though some of them may have lost hold of essential truth as regards the Creator and the best way of worshiping Him, and may have a perverted notion of religion. 

The word religion is derived from the Latin religare, to fasten or bind, and implies the bond between God and His creatures. Religion is the external and social expression of this bond by means of social worship, and includes adoration, in acknowledgment of God's supreme dominion; thanksgiving, in acknowledgment of His gifts; atonement, in satisfaction for neglect of duties; and petition which is an acknowledgment of dependence upon Him for all that is needed. Morality or ethics, which is an observance of the law of nature, is included in religion, because the obligation to give homage and praise to God carries with it the need to obey God's 122

laws, both those that are explicit and those that are implied by our need of an intelligent use of our nature and of the things which God has created for our use.

Because man is an intelligent being, and because he is a social being, he recognizes that he owes social worship to God, and wherever groups of men are found upon the earth they have some form of organized religion as a social function, with some notions of morality as part of this function. 

God Himself ordained that man should belong to a religious society, since religion is one of man's fundamental needs. Man's life on earth is merely preparatory to life eternal, and religious society exists to help man to work out his salvation in a human, that is, in a social manner. Religious society is, therefore, a natural group. Had God not founded a specific religious society to fill man's religious needs, then the State would have been obliged to determine the means of organization of religious society best calculated to enable men to carry out the social worship of their Creator. However, God did found a specific religious society for this purpose. 

Catholic sociologists take it for granted, when studying religious society and man's social relations with it, that God founded first the tribal religious organization of the Israelites, which Christ His Son later replaced by the world organization of the Catholic Church, saying: "Thou art Peter; and upon this rock I will build My

Church and the gates of hell shall not prevail against it." "Going, therefore, teach ye all nations . . . and behold I am with you all days, even to the consummation of the world."2

The late English artist and writer, Eric Gill, expressed a right idea of the Church in his own inimitable fashion: "The Church: that is the society of all men united 

¹ Matt. 16:18. 

2 Matt. 28:19-20. not as fathers and mothers not as neighbors

not as a nation not as workers or traders but as passengers. "Passengers on a ship or a railway train are united not as men and women, not because they are in the same cabin or compartment or deck (first class, etc.), not because they are officers or deck hands or engineers, not because they speak the same language in the dining saloon, but because they are all going the same way. . . "The Christian, and by that I mean the Catholic Church which has its visible head in Rome (I mean the Catholic Church because it is the only one which knows its own mind for certain and the only one which is not a national society or a society of special people; it is a society which contains all kinds of men, rich and poor, silly and wise, bad and good, white, black, yellow, and red, French, English,

Hottentot, and Chinese. The Church of England is a very good 

Church, I am sure, but it is the Church of England — that is, it is mostly just English and people in English colonies; and English people are not the only passengers in the ship we call the world. 

The Catholic Church is called the Church of Rome, but only because its visible headquarters are in Rome - not because it is Italian). Well, the Catholic Church is a society of men who think everybody is going to be found, found in heaven, found in the presence of God- unless they would rather not. 

"I say, 'unless they would rather not.' For according to our first principle: man is a complete person. He has free will."3 

All church groups are primary societies. The Catholic Church is a supernatural and perfect society. 

All church groups are primary societies, because through them people come into frequent face-to-face contact with one another, and the religious society to which one belongs has a very important influence on one's life and conduct. Sociologists consider a study of 'Eric Gill, Unemployment, pp. 4-5.

the social influence of church groups to be very important, for they realize that religious society has had in the past, and still has, a great influence upon the trend of social life. 

It is the Catholic belief that membership in the Catholic Church is best for us, not only that we may fulfill our social nature by worshiping God in common with other men, but also because God has made the Catholic Church the depository of truth and the official means of salvation. Whereas the ideas of individual men are often incorrect and men often misinterpret the dictates of their conscience, Catholics believe that by strict adherence to the teachings of the divinely founded Church all men can be assured of attaining the purpose of their creation: eternal happiness in heaven. Because of its high purpose, the Church may be said to be a supernatural, rather than a fundamentally natural, group. 

The Church is, therefore, a primary and supernatural society. 

More than this, it is a perfect society because, like the State, it is sovereign in its own sphere and does not depend on any power other than God. Yet, as was said in Chapter Five, the State, whose aim is the material and temporal welfare of its members, belongs to a sphere inferior and subordinate to that of the Church or religious society, whose aim is the spiritual and eternal welfare of her members. Because the State exists for the good of its citizens, and because our supernatural interests have been entrusted exclusively to the Church, the State must not interfere with Church authority in matters pertaining to faith and morals, for our eternal welfare is more important than anything else in the world. Hence the State should not interfere with the Church in her own sphere, and should protect her rights as a lawful, supernatural, and perfect society. 

Religious society is a dominant factor in social life. 

That religious society is a social institution of major importance in our organization of society must be obvious. Much of its influence may be intangible, but the thoughtful observer of society cannot fail to note its presence and its effect.

First, it satisfies the spiritual urge in man to worship his Creator. 

By so doing it brings man into touch with his Creator, and makes him zealous to do His Will. If men fulfill their most important duty upon earth, that of worshiping the Creator and doing His Will, they cannot fail to be happy; they cannot fail to have a well-organized social life; and their happiness will have its influence on the harmonious working of society. 

Secondly, religious society aims at teaching truth, and its importance as an exponent of truth is very great. It becomes a dominant factor in determining man's attitudes and ideals regarding social life, and in maintaining those ideals. Thus it encourages men to do what is right, and by being somewhat exacting in its requirements, it trains men's wills. In this way it helps men to overcome that part of their nature which tends to lawlessness. When a true religious spirit flourishes, therefore, social life is more in harmony with true ideals of justice. Moreover, true religion teaches man his correct place in the universe, and the Christian Church in particular teaches the value of those specific Christian virtues of charity, including mercy and forgiveness. By thus teaching truth and tolerance, justice and charity, the Church provides a great unifying factor in social life, and it has a great influence on the ideals of governments and of all social organizations, and hence on men's actions within these organizations in all spheres of social life. 

Where true notions of religion and morality are absent, social life becomes disorganized, until, to the extent that God and His law is forgotten, it may even become intolerable. Pope Pius XII wrote a pastoral letter especially to the Catholics of America at the end of 1939. In this encyclical, entitled Sertum Laetitiae, he stated very clearly the fact which we have just noted showing wherein is the root of our modern evils, and what happens when man forgets the important truths of religion. He wrote: "This, according to the admission of all reasonable men, is everywhere the bitter and prolific root of evils: the refusal to recognize the Divine Majesty, the neglect of the moral law, the origin of

which is from heaven, or that regrettable inconstancy which makes its victims waver between the lawful and the forbidden, between justice and iniquity. "Thence," continued the Holy Father, "arise immoderate and blind egoists, the thirst for pleasure, the vice of drunkenness, immodest and costly styles in dress, the prevalence of crime even among minors, the lust for power, neglect of the poor, base craving for ill-gotten wealth, the flight from the land, levity in entering into marriage, divorce, the break-up of the family, the cooling of mutual affection between parents and children, birth control, the enfeeblement of the race, the weakening of respect for authority, or obsequiousness or rebellion, neglect of duty towards one's country and towards mankind."

These are serious thoughts, and inasmuch as many people in our country have forgotten the claims of religion, we can see the bad effects already among us.

This is no cause for despair, however, for many people in the United States do sincerely worship God. If we wish our American way of life to flourish along correct lines, we must surely strive to support religion, and to increase its influence. 

Although religion has been largely ousted from our public school system, it is still basic to our American culture. The Declaration of 

Independence acknowledges God, even though it is a regrettable fact that the Constitution of the United States nowhere shows that the nation hoped to be under the guidance and patronage of God. 

Because of the differences of opinion of the early American citizens, the first amendment to the Constitution specifically forbids Congress to establish any religion by law. There is, therefore, no official worship of God in the United States within an organized body, but religion is nevertheless given its place in our lives as an organized group of citizens. Every session of the United States Congress and of the state legislatures opens with prayer. Court oaths end with the words: *Pope Pius XII, encyclical, Sertum Laetitiae, To the Church in the United 

States, p. 10.

"So help me God." Chaplains of various religious denominations are provided at public expense for the army, the navy, prisons, and other public institutions. Church property used directly for religious purposes is not taxed. Freedom of worship and religion is basic to a true democracy. But only if our people renew their religious ideals, and carry them into practice will true democracy continue to be ours. Because people have a voice in government and in social living in a country with a democratic government, it is all the more important that they be religious people, with knowledge of true values, and with high ideals which will enable them to ensure that political and social measures are true and right according to God's plan.

The percentage of the various religions in the world. 

After 19 centuries only 19 per cent of the whole world is Catholic.

In several countries which were formerly Christian, the political organization is no longer of a type to foster religion. Ever since the 

Bolshevistic regime in Russia, religion has been more or less condemned. For a number of years party members were compelled to profess atheism; many churches were destroyed and others were sometimes used as museums of antireligious propaganda; societies organized for religious purposes were prohibited. There is less open antagonism to religion in Russia today, but religion is not encouraged nor is religious propaganda allowed. In Germany, the religious worship is hampered in many ways and ministers of religion are not allowed to speak to their congregations the truth as

they see it. Many Christians and many Jews have suffered severe injustices for their faith. As in Russia, the government is totalitarian, and one cannot worship a dictator, or an impersonal State, and at the same time give God His due.

Religious society of the western world has many specific achievements to its credit, in addition to its great, intangible influence. 

True religion results in action: in the apostolate. Throughout the ages it is evident that, after giving due time to the worship of God, religious people spend themselves in carrying their ideals into practice in the service to their fellow men. In all modern societies where religion is allowed its true place, therefore, we find religious people conducting schools and in other ways instructing others in truth.5 

We find them practicing charity by caring for the unfortunate, in conducting hospitals, orphanages, homes for the aged, or in visiting the poor and the ill in their own homes. Because religious society is a primary group we find churches providing relaxation and enjoyment for their members, after the first duty to God has been performed. The church is a true social center in many a community, especially in small rural groups. History will give us many examples to prove the truth of these statements.

Let us consider what the Roosevelt Commission on Country Life reported in 1910 about the social importance of the church in rural America: we can adapt these remarks in such a way as to see the active social place which the church can also take in urban communities: "It is especially important that the country church recognize that "Pope Leo XIII, Pope Pius XI, and Pope Pius XII have been especially noteworthy for the publication of encyclicals setting forth their authoritative views on the social problems of the day. An encyclical is a circular letter sent by the pope to the bishops, to be read to the faithful. Encyclicals treat of any matter concerning which His Holiness may wish to instruct us. The chief social encyclicals are those on the Christian Constitution of States, and the Condition of the Working Classes, by Pope Leo XIII; on the Christian Education of Youth, Christian

Marriage, on the Reconstruction of the Social Order, and Atheistic Communism, by Pope Pius XI; and on the Function of the State in the Modern World, by 

Pope Pius XII.

MRYTY MERCATION CENTER Courtesy Church Property Administration and Architects A. F. Herman and H. T. Simons 

The recreation center of Nativity Parish, Detroit, Michigan. The building provides facilities for several kinds of athletic games, plays, meetings, and the like. More parishes, particularly in rural areas, need centers like this to provide places of meeting for their people. it has a social responsibility to the entire community as well as a religious responsibility to its own group of peoples. "Any consideration of the problem of rural life that leaves out of account the function and possibilities of the church and of related institutions, would be grossly inadequate. This is not only because in the last analysis the country-life problem is a moral problem, or that in the best development of the individual the great motives and results are religious and spiritual, but because from the pure sociological point of view the church is fundamentally a necessary institution in country life. In a peculiar way the church is intimately related to the agricultural industry. The work and the life of the farm are closely bound together, and the institutions of the country react on that life and on one another more intimately than they do in the city. This gives the rural church a position of peculiar difficulty and one of unequalled opportunity. The time has arrived

when the church must take larger leadership both as an institution and through its pastors, in the social reorganization of rural life. . . The rural church must be more completely than now a social center. This means not so much a place for holding social gatherings, although this is legitimate and desirable, but a place whence constantly emanate influences that go to build up the moral and spiritual tone of the whole community. The country church of the future is to be held responsible for the great ideals of community life as well as of personal character. The country pastor must be a community leader."

The churches of the United States. 

There have been many flourishing church organizations in the United States ever since the advent of the early colonists to our shores. The French and Spanish colonists were, of course, Roman 

Catholics, and we have evidence of the flourishing condition of the Catholic Church in those times in the Spanish mission churches of the West and Southwest, and in the churches and institutions of the French national groups which still continue in the Northeast and the South. The state of Maryland was founded by an English Catholic, but, of course, most of the English colonists were members of the Church of England or other Protestant sects, for England has been largely Protestant since the time of Henry VIII. For example, the followers of William Penn, who founded Pennsylvania, were Quakers.

As the United States developed, American members of the Church of England formed a separate church called the Episcopal Church. 

In the course of time, the Reformed Church was set up by immigrants from Holland. The Swedes brought the Lutheran Church into their settlements in Delaware, and this church flourished with 

Swedish, German, Norwegian, and Danish members. The Presbyterian Church was established by the early Scottish settlers. The 

6 Report of the Roosevelt Commission on Country Life, 1910.
