THE OLD MISSION 

The Old Mission at Santa Barbara, California. This is one of the most beautiful of the Spanish mission churches in the country. 

Jews established many synagogues. Irish, Italian, and other Catholics set up numerous Catholic churches throughout the land. Other religious sects brought from Europe include the Mennonites, Dunkers, Universalists, and Schwenkfelders. 

Then Americans themselves founded new sects. Unitarianism grew, especially in New England. The Church of Jesus Christ of 

Latter Day Saints (the Mormon Church) was established first in New York and then after brief sojourns in Ohio, Missouri, and Illinois, finally settled with its headquarters at Salt Lake City, but with branches all over the country. Mary Baker Eddy established "Christian Science." Many semireligious interdenominational groups were also founded, such as the Young Men's Christian Association (the Y.M.C.A.), and the Young Women's Christian Association (the 

Y.W.C.A.). A new trend today is the formation of interdenominational groups, such as the National Conference of Christians and Jews, for the fostering of understanding and tolerance among members of different religious sects.

The Catholic Church in America is particularly flourishing. 

The Catholic Church in America has more members than any one other religious denomination, although there are many fewer Catholics than the combined group of Protestants. The number of 

Catholic churches, schools, colleges, universities and seminaries, hospitals and social service organizations in the country is very impressive.

The parish, the essential unit of the Catholic Church, is composed of the people under their parish priest. The different parishes in a district are linked together in what is called a diocese, under a bishop. Various bishoprics form a province, the chief diocese in the province is the archdiocese ruled by the archbishop, who also exercises jurisdiction in some few matters over the whole province. 

Above the archbishops in dignity are the cardinals, of whom there are three in the United States at the present time. The central organization of the Church is in Rome where the Pope (elected for life by the cardinals from among their number) rules the whole. 

Bishops, archbishops, and cardinals report regularly to him. Various committees (called Sacred Congregations) in Rome are concerned with different matters of importance to the Church as a whole. 

Within the Church there are numerous organizations for religious and social purposes. First, there are religious congregations, which are societies of men or women formed for the purpose of enabling them to carry out the Counsels of Perfection more easily than is possible in the world, and usually also for some specific external apostolic work, such as teaching, nursing, or caring for the poor. The most ancient of these societies are called Orders; such are the 

Augustinians, the Benedictines, the Carmelites, the Dominicans, and the Franciscans. Other societies or congregations, founded later, perform an invaluable service to the Church also, for example, the 

Society of Jesus (Jesuits), Sulpicians, Christian Brothers, and all the other numerous religious societies of men and women to whom 

American Catholics owe so much.

St. Joseph's Pro-Cathedral, Bardstown, Kentucky. Fairly typical of the church buildings in sections of the country where the greater number of Catholics were of English stock.

Secondly, there are many religious societies formed for one or other purpose, to which lay people may belong. Among these we may note the Third Orders of the different religious Orders, the Sodality of Our Lady, the Society of St. Vincent de Paul, the 

Society for the Propagation of the Faith, the Confraternity of Christian Doctrine, the Legion of Mary, and all the different parochial, diocesan, or national Catholic groups. 

Thirdly, there are societies formed for Catholic Action, to which we shall later devote a few paragraphs. 

Because of the vast size of the United States, and the number of Catholics in the country, the American Hierarchy (cardinals, archbishops, and bishops) have established the National Catholic Welfare Conference, usually referred to as the N.C.W.C., with headquarters in Washington. The function of the N.C.W.C. is to render service to the bishops, in uniting Catholic lay organizations throughout the country, and in acting as a center for advice, for the dissemination of information, and for the performance of needed religious and social work not undertaken by other groups in the country. It is divided into eight departments, as follows: 

1. The Executive Department, which supervises and co-ordinates the work of the other departments, and has under its direct jurisdiction a Bureau of Immigration, the Confraternity of Christian Doctrine, and the publication of the monthly magazine, Catholic Action. 

2. The Social Action Department, which deals with studies, programs, and publications connected with industry, rural and family life, and also the Catholic Association for International Peace. 

3. The Education Department, which furnishes educational statistics and information, maintains a teachers' employment bureau, and publishes the Catholic Educational Review. 

4. The Press Department, whose news service provides the majority of Catholic papers in this country with reliable news and pictures of Catholics and Catholic events at home and abroad. 

5. The Legal Department, which collects and classifies needed legal information of service to Catholics.

6. The Lay Organizations Department, including the National 

Council of Catholic Men (N.C.C.M.) and the National Council of 

Catholic Women (N.C.C.W.), with their many local branches throughout the country. The Men's Council has been responsible for the development of the Catholic Hour radio program. 

7. The Catholic Action Study Department, whose work is to assist and promote Catholic Action groups throughout the country. 

8. The Department of Youth, formed in 1940, to promote and assist the activities of youth groups throughout the country. 

In addition to these departments, various Episcopal committees have been formed by the Executive Board of the N.C.W.C., such as the Committee on Motion Pictures and the National Legion of 

Decency, the Committee on Clean Literature, the Committee for

Catholic Refugees, the Committee on the Propagation of the Faith, and others. The National Catholic Community Service is a member agency of the USO (United Service Organization of the government). 

Catholic Action is the official organization of the Catholic laity for collaboration in the apostolate of the hierarchy. 

Of recent years there has been a reawakening to the fact that since lay people can reach persons with whom the hierarchy, priests, and members of religious communities would never come into contact, the Church has a positive need of lay people to carry out its work. The chief functions of the hierarchy within the Church are three: first, to teach the doctrine of Christ; second to work for the sanctification of souls; and third, to govern the Church. They have been assisted in their first two functions by priests and by members of religious communities. In the past, lay people also have played a part in this work, such as lay teachers in Catholic schools, and others doing specific apostolic work, for example, the members of the Society of St. Vincent de Paul, founded by the layman 

Frédéric Ozanam, and Pauline Jaricot, who founded the Society for the Propagation of the Faith. On the whole, however, the laity has played a passive role until late years. But within recent times, the

place of lay people in the Church has been clearly defined, and their work has been officially entitled: Catholic Action. Catholic 

Action is the official organization of the Catholic laity to enable them to collaborate in the apostolate of the hierarchy. Pope Pius X first defined the term in his motu proprio of 1903, and he laid down specific information of the role to be played by Catholic Action in Italy in the encyclical Il fermo proposito, 1905. Pope Pius XI extended this work of Catholic Action to the whole world in the Mens 

Nostra encyclical of December 20, 1929. 

Let us consider the terms of the definition of Catholic Action. The word laity means lay people; therefore priests and religious do not take part in the work of Catholic Action, although, of course, they can be auxiliaries to it. Their place in the Church has already been defined. The word apostolate is important, for the idea of the restoration of all the activities in this world in Christ is of the essence of Catholic Action. Purely spiritual societies, such as sodalities and 

Third Orders, aiming solely at the self-sanctification of members, are not part of Catholic Action, important auxiliaries though they may be, for the apostolate is not their principal aim. Neither can societies of purely professional, social, economic, or political interest be a part of Catholic Action, even though composed entirely of Catholics, because in their object the spiritual aspect of the apostolate is lacking. Official organization is necessary. Apostolic work along private lines is not Catholic Action, because an essential feature of the organization of Catholic Action is an express commission to do certain apostolic work, that is, a commission to do active work to help the hierarchy to spread the Kingdom of Christ, and this is usually performed under the direction of the parish priest, or other priests delegated by the bishop of the diocese. 

Unless they have other work of a spiritual nature to perform, all Catholics ought to wish to take part in Catholic Action. There are one or more branches of Catholic Action societies in most parishes. 

If there are no such societies in our parish, or if they do not suit our gifts and aptitudes, we can always inquire of our parish priest, or of

the secretary of the bishop of the diocese as to what we can do, or we can make application at the headquarters of the N.C.W.C. for information about existing societies which we might join. To take part in Catholic Action is not a participation to be regarded lightly. 

To spread the Kingdom of Christ involves a study of religion and of the ideals of Christianity; it involves a personal spiritual preparation, and it probably involves, too, some specific study along special lines. Those who help to spread the Kingdom of Christ in the apostolate of Catholic Action will also be helping to promote the welfare of the State in the best possible manner. For one who tries to carry out his duties to God and to his neighbor in a more perfect manner, cannot fail to become a better citizen, working for social harmony and for intelligent civic organization within the State, and within the family and all the other social groups to which he belongs. If the work of Catholic Action spreads in the United States, the Catholic Church will fill its place more than ever before as a great means of improving our social life.

 1. Write to the National Catholic Welfare Conference, 1312 Massa chusetts Avenue, N.W., Washington, D. C., for a list of its departments and brief description of its activities. 

2. If possible, arrange a visit to the headquarters of the N.C.W.C. 

If this is impossible, arrange for a well-planned talk on the organization and work of the N.C.W.C. 

3. Read some articles on the actual organization of some Catholic 

Action youth activities, and have some members of the class report on two or three of them. What are the special distinguishing features which make these organizations belong to Catholic Action? 

4. With your teacher's permission, divide the class into committees to visit the different churches in your neighborhood to find out the church attendance and the chief social services which they perform. Prepare reports to show the percentage of people in the neighborhood who belong to a church, and the constructive work which they accomplish. 

5. Arrange for a talk to show in a practical fashion how American social life would be improved if all Catholics were serious in the practice of their religion: observing so far as is humanly possible the precepts of charity and justice in their dealings with their family, their friends, their business relationships, their fellow citizens, their government. 

6. If you are convinced of the truth of the teachings of Catholicism, and of the social value of the serious practice of Catholicism, discuss what you, as a class, and as individuals, can do to show your conviction in a practical manner (a) now, while you are in school; (b) when you have left school.

7. Outline on the blackboard the chief work done by your own parish for the social betterment of the community (for example, the work of the Society of St. Vincent de Paul, a parish Credit Union, etc.). 

8. Have different members of the class prepare brief reports on the history, organization and work of such Catholic organizations as the following: 

a) The Society of St. Vincent de Paul; b) The Knights of Columbus; 

c) The Holy Name Society; 

d) The Legion of Mary; e) The Sodality of Our Lady; f) The Confraternity of Christian Doctrine; 

g) The Catholic Evidence Guild. 

9. Have all students read pages 131-134 of Michel, Rev. V., The 

Christian in the World. Let one student give a report to the class on the social importance of parish life, based on the points made by the author. Follow this report by a class discussion to see if the author's ideas can be applied to your own local parish. If possible, add to the ways by which a parish can assist in the sound social organization of a neighborhood.

Unit Three ECONOMIC LIFE 

We have now discussed the three most important social groups formed by men: the family, the Church, and the State. In this Unit we shall learn how people have organized social groups for another important function: to earn a living. We shall be especially interested in the organization of this economic life in our American capitalistic system, and shall learn its advantages, its disadvantages, and some of the proposals made to improve it. 143

The greater proportion of our population is engaged in the manufacturing and the mechanical industries. 

In the past few years, particularly since the beginning of World War II, the airplane industry has developed greatly.

Chapter Nine  The purpose of this chapter is: 

1. To realize that the basis of economic organization is the fact that man has a fundamental need of material goods, and procures these best by cooperating with others in the division of labor and organizing the four factors of production: land, labor, capital, and organization. 

2. To discover the essential features of capitalism and of corporate organization, and to learn the advantages and disadvantages of largescale production, and to realize the overindustrialization of our present 

American way of life. 

3. To realize the difficulties of a good organization of agricultural economic life in the United States today. 

Man has many material and other needs which can best be satisfied by the division of labor in the production of goods or services.

Man has many needs of all kinds, and he makes better provisions for his material wants by a mutual division of labor- that is, by cooperating with others in the necessary work than by trying to produce for the requirements of himself and his family by his own labor. Where life is simple the division of labor is relatively simple, too. The more highly developed material culture is, the more complicated is the organization of work groups within the State and in international society.

The division of labor has several advantages. First and foremost, men have varying gifts and aptitudes for different kinds of work, and only by cooperating with each other can they more fully develop them. Some people are fitted for leadership and for position of responsibility and authority; others prefer to work in a capacity 145

ECONOMIC LIFE 

which calls for no independent action on their part. Some men are happiest as artists, others as engineers, tailors, blacksmiths, or in domestic service. A man contributes more to the world's wealth if he confines himself to the work he does best, than if he performs many varied duties. In exchange for his specialized services he can receive either goods, or money which can be exchanged for the goods he needs. A second important advantage of the division of labor is that the people of certain countries, and even of certain restricted districts, can produce certain kinds of goods better than others, and it is best that they should specialize in these products and exchange their surplus goods for other commodities. However, too great specialization leads to many undesirable conditions: frequently the worker loses versatility and interest in his work; regional unemployment and exhaustion of natural resources may result.

Economic cooperation has led to various economic systems which may be called occupational societies. The work groups within these economic systems are so necessary for man that they are natural societies. They are also face-to-face groups for those who work.

As far back as we have records in history, man has organized groups in order to earn a better living by the division of labor. There have been many types of these economic systems or work groups in the past, and there are a number of different systems existing at the present time. In the United States and many other countries capitalism is the leading feature of our economic organization. Some form of organization of economic life into occupational or work groups is a necessity for man if he is to develop to the best of his ability in a material way, and even in many nonmaterial ways. Economic society or the work group is therefore a natural society, even though it is fundamentally not so important as the family or the State. It functions within the State, just as do the family, religious society, and all other social groups. Since most people spend a large portion of their day working within a work group, meeting other people

Bettmann Archive 

Division of labor in an early sixteenth-century goldsmith's shop. Notice that each man is doing a particular type of work. with whom they must come in contact in connection with their work to earn a living for themselves and their dependents, the work group is also a primary group - one in which a large portion of the population has almost daily face-to-face contact. 

The working population of the United States is predominantly urban, and is largely engaged in industry and trade. 

According to the 1940 census, 45,166,083 persons were gainfully employed, whereas 48,829,920 were employed in 1930. Many millions then out of work have since joined the armed services or are engaged in defense industries. Many women have also been added to the labor force, so the total number of workers has been greatly increased since 1940. Because the greater proportion of our population now works in industries other than agricultural, we shall confine our discussion in this and the succeeding chapters mainly to industrial problems.

Four factors are needed in production: land, labor, capital, and organization. Where capitalism prevails, as in the United States, capital is considered the most important factor. 

Four things are needed to produce goods. First, the raw materials supplied by nature, which economists designate by the general term land, even though they mean by this anything which comes from the land, or from rivers and seas. Second, accumulated material goods from the past are needed to transform the raw materials into the finished product; for example, factories or other buildings are needed, and such equipment as tools or machinery; also the wherewithal to pay for the making of the goods before they are sold. This is what is called capital. Third, people are needed to supply labor to transform the products supplied by nature. Fourth, persons who can co-ordinate the other three factors of production are needed, to supply the organization, so that business activity functions smoothly. 

In the middle of the eighteenth century a new system of economic organization developed in England, and was soon established in the United States and all the other Western countries. This system was called capitalism. Under capitalism, goods are chiefly made by machinery in large-scale production, and they are made for an "unascertained market," in other words, they are produced before the organizer is sure that he will be able to sell them. Large amounts of money are needed to buy the tools, machinery, and so forth (called capital goods) and also to pay for the raw materials, transportation, and wages of workers before the goods are sold to the consumer. 

Capital is therefore needed in production, and capitalism is a system where capital is considered the most important factor in production.

ECONOMIC LIFE 

Before we consider the other features of capitalism, let us see how capital is procured in our modern capitalistic organization. As we have just said, for large-scale production of goods made in advance of orders from the consumer, large amounts of capital are needed. 

The large-scale producer is usually unable to provide capital for himself or by direct borrowing from a bank, and so the present system of investment banking and the stock exchange arose. Under this system, if a business needs money, the organizers usually go to an investment bank. An investment bank is one which specializes in financing large-scale corporations and is quite different from the commercial bank with which most persons are acquainted.* 

If the investment bank finds the business to be reasonably sound and with reasonable prospects of making profits, it appeals to the public for the necessary funds. In other words, it secures the hundreds of thousands or even millions of dollars needed for the enterprise by inducing investors with relatively small amounts of money to become bondholders or stockholders. 

Bondholders are persons who lend money at a fixed rate of interest, on the security of land, buildings, or other assets. They receive certificates called bonds, which are usually repayable at the end of a certain period. Stockholders are persons who become part owners of the business, sharing in the profits yearly by receiving dividends in accordance with the amount of their investment. They receive certificates called stocks.

When the investment bank has sold sufficient stocks or bonds, or both, to the investing public, it hands over the money received, less a commission, to the corporation concerned. The corporation then has the money needed to buy capital goods and to provide for the other expenses of large-scale production. The stock- and bondholders have their certificates, which they can sell to other buyers at any time on a special market called the stock exchange, through dealers who are called stockbrokers. If a holder wishes to sell all or 

4 'The chief function of the commercial bank is the receiving of deposits, paying them out by means of the checkbook system, and lending money to its customers.

Acme The New York curb market. Here stocks are bought and sold. part of his holdings, he puts them up for sale on the stock exchange through his banker or broker. If a person wishes to buy some stocks or bonds, he purchases them on the stock exchange in the same manner. The price of these securities depends not only on the earnings of the corporation concerned, but also on how many people wish to buy or sell. If a corporation makes large profits, its dividend payments per share of stock may become very high, which means that the stocks are much sought after, and the prices rise. This situation makes the bonds rise somewhat in price as well, for the security behind them will generally be all the greater. Speculation as to supply and demand and future dividend yields (for example, ideas that profits will rise or fall because of war or because of a new use found for a product) leads to great fluctuation in prices of stocks and bonds, and many fortunes are made and lost in stock-exchange operations, which are matters quite apart from the business itself. 

This speculative trading is often called "stock gambling," and, in fact, it does not differ greatly from gambling on races or in games of chance.

ECONOMIC LIFE 

Capitalism is a system which has good and bad features.

Under capitalism any type of business may flourish, and we have one-man businesses, partnerships, and corporations. On the whole, however, most business is done through the corporation, whose organization we have just briefly described. Capitalism has essentially grown within political systems which were founded on a philosophy of individual freedom and man's natural right to own private property. Unfortunately, the idea of freedom became debased and the theory of laissez faire developed the notion that one should let people alone, and that the best will always come out on top under free competition, with a very minimum of governmental restraint. Man's natural right to private property ownership also became distorted in the theory of capitalism, and people did not take into sufficient account the social obligations which ownership entails: the idea of stewardship and of restrictions sometimes necessary for the common good. This liberal idea of unrestrained individualism has led to many abuses, for frequently rich, powerful, or fortunate persons have gained control of economic life to the detriment of the well-being of the masses. 

Under the worst features of this new capitalistic system, organizers and workers alike have often lost all idea of their own welfare and that of society. Production has been undertaken for profits to be gained, rather than for the use-value of the articles made. Workers have often come to look upon their work as a mere "job" rather than as an avocation. Employers have often regarded their workers as "labor" or "hands," and consider their business a profit-making concern rather than a social responsibility. Neither employer nor worker, nor stock- and bondholder, take much account of the consumer's side of economic activity- the side of the people for whom the goods were produced. The consumer is usually charged "what the market will bear," that is, the highest price which is compatible with the greatest number of sales at the largest aggregate profits. 

Since profits are considered the most important aim of business organization, consumers are often induced to buy useless or even

Buick Motor Division 

Four steps in the manufacture of a car. These are only a few of the many processes involved, each of which often demands skilled workmanship. The automobile industry is one of the largest of the nation's industries.

ECONOMIC LIFE 

harmful goods by clever advertising and salesmen's wiles. Generally speaking, most corporations make use of large-scale organization, producing ahead of demand for an unascertained market. 

Large-scale production has many advantages. 

1. More economic use can be made of the division of labor, with an increase of efficiency and reduction of costs. 

2. Large-scale purchasing and shipment of goods effects economies in purchasing and transportation costs. 

3. Expensive experiments can be afforded, and many inventions have been the outcome of the monetary resources and the specialization of work in large-scale industry. 

4. By-products can be utilized for profit, which could not be done in small-scale industry in the same manner: for example, the large meat-packing houses can utilize animal hair in mattress making and upholstery; bone and horn can be made into buttons, glue, and the like; pharmaceutical supplies are produced; and other animal products supply fertilizers, cattle and poultry feeds, soap, gelatine, and many other consumer goods. 

5. High-grade experts can be attracted to a large-scale corporation which can afford to pay high salaries. 

6. Large-scale industry can also settle in a certain locality which will give many advantages: there will always be a supply of specialized workers; banks, lawyers, transportation, and other needed services will also specialize in the industry and therefore can give more efficient service and help to reduce costs or to make added profits,

On the other hand, there are disadvantages in large-scale capitalistic organization: 

1. Large units are not always more efficient than small ones. The organization may become so large as to be unwieldy, and management may not always be efficient. Workers and employers do not understand each other, and workers will often waste goods which could be better supervised in a smaller concern. Eventually the law

CAND 

ORGANIZATION LABOR CAPITAL 

Land, labor, capital, and organization are the four essential factors of production in modern times. of diminishing returns will apply to the business, and manufacturing and selling costs will rise rather than fall. 

2. Large-scale business often overproduces for the market in any one year, thus bringing about unemployment, and the impersonal character of the organization often leads a corporation to discharge workers indiscriminately without thought of their welfare. 

3. Where there is localization of industry, unemployment in one part of the industry will lead to unemployment in other sections also, so that the whole district will suffer intensely. 

This unemployment is a serious matter. Where machines are widely used, workers tend to be of secondary consideration - automatons or "hands" who must keep pace with the machines or be dismissed as incompetent, or perhaps "too old at forty." 

4. The complex division of labor and the localization of industry have also led to a multiplicity of middlemen, so that a product changes hands too many times before it finally reaches the consumer, who must pay a price which includes remuneration to all these middlemen. 

5. The worst feature of the corporation type of organization is the divorce of ownership of capital from the control. The directors do not always work in the best interests of stockholders, especially the small stockholders. On the other hand, directors are always inclined to favor financial interests rather than the interests of workers and consumers. 

6. Production is for profit to stockholders and steady interest rates on bonds, rather than for use to the consumer and social benefit to capitalist, worker, consumer, and the general good of the community. 

7. Large corporation profits are also taxed more heavily than

ECONOMIC LIFE 

profits on a smaller scale, and sometimes smaller partnerships or single enterprises gain by their greater flexibility, since corporations can engage only in the enterprises specified in their charter of incorporation.

Indeed, the social abuses which have arisen through large-scale industry are such that reform is seriously needed, even though few people would deny the manifold advantages we enjoy as a result of the complex division of labor and large-scale industry and would not wish them to disappear. With planning and regulation, capitalism could surely develop into an economic system beneficial to all engaged in industry, as well as to the public at large. Already conditions have been greatly improved, and risks have been arranged for more or less satisfactorily, as we shall show in the next few chapters. 

These changes have been due partly to more adequate social legislation brought about by the agitation of social reformers and of the workers themselves, who formed labor unions to help improve their conditions. They are partly due, also, to the fact that employers have come to realize that good working conditions improve the output and good will of their workers, and hence increase their profits.

The organization of farming involves situations and problems which differ from other economic activities. 

The agricultural industry of America must also claim our attention, because although our country is no longer predominantly agricultural, nevertheless, as we showed earlier in the chapter, in 1940, 43.5 per cent of our total population lived in rural areas; and nearly one fifth (18.8 per cent) of our working population were directly engaged in agriculture, forestry, and fishing. 

Most farms are still small units worked by the owner or tenant farmer and his laborers. Life on the farm is certainly more natural than spending the larger portion of one's day in a noisy workshop or in an office, and the farmer and his family get the benefit of fresh air and good food. Some people, particularly the Distributists, who

BESISTORS

Courtesy The Glenn L. Martin Co. 

A highly skilled worker testing vibration machinery. are anxious to have our economic organization less artificial than it now is, hope that more families will "return to the land," grow their own food, and sell any surplus to obtain money to pay for their other needs. Whether or not it is possible for many cityfolk to make such a change is by no means certain. City life provides many attractions in the form of greater facilities for social intercourse, for recreation, education, and a varied choice of occupations. On the other hand, rural life has been greatly improved by village social activities, by traveling libraries, better travel facilities, betterplanned consolidated schools, and other modern changes. 

Of recent years Catholics have been doing much to improve the condition of Catholic rural people through the Catholic Rural Life Bureau, which is a part of the National Catholic Welfare Confer-

ECONOMIC LIFE 

ence, discussed in Chapter Eight, and through the Catholic Rural Life Conference. The latter organization holds an annual convention and summer schools for rural priests, conducts an information bureau and a speaker's bureau, and publishes an excellent quarterly en titled Land and Home (formerly The Catholic Rural Life Bulletin). 

Agriculture is "industry's poor relation." Although, as we said, many small farms still exist, tenancy has increased considerably during the past sixty years. In 1880 only 25.6 per cent of American farmers did not own their own farms; in 1940, 38.7 per cent of our farmers were tenants. Since that time farm products have been greatly needed because of the war, and the situation may have changed.

Farm tenancy has increased for several reasons. First, such high prices were obtainable for agricultural produce during the 1914–18 war that there was a demand for land, which led to higher land prices. City dwellers, therefore, bought farms for speculation and investment and leased them out to farmers; and tenant farmers themselves were unable to purchase land on mortgage because of the higher price. Some of these investors in land were large-scale corporations, so that certain corporations today own vast tracts of farm land. Other owners had property only on a small scale. A second reason for the increase in tenancy has been the high price of agricultural produce. Farmers sometimes retired and went to live in cities, leaving their farms to be worked by tenants. A third reason has been the mechanization of farming, which has made the initial cost of equipment too high for the small farmer, who often could not compete in price for farm produce if he worked by less efficient methods than larger farmers.

Tenant farming and absentee ownership is not always connected with adverse conditions for the tenant farmer, yet it has led to serious abuses in certain sections of the country, and especially in the cotton states. Tenants rarely receive more than a few hundred dollars in cash as their share of the proceeds of the crops and often

Ewing Galloway

An Oklahoma family driven from their farm by drought. This family is in search of work, like many others from the so-called "Dust-Bowl Area." this meager income is immediately absorbed by the local store, or by the store run by the owner of the land, who gave goods on credit to the tenants prior to the crop yield. 

In recent times many persons connected with agriculture have become homeless because the submarginal land used during the period of high prices could not yield a livelihood when prices had decreased; and because thoughtless use of the land has taken the value out of the soil or exposed it to drought because of the lack of trees and irrigation. These migratory workers swelled the number of farm workers who eke out a bare existence for themselves and their families. Indeed, the greatest migratory movement in the history of the United States took place between the years 1935 and 1938, out

ECONOMIC LIFE 

of the "Dust-Bowl Area." In 1940 it is estimated that there were more than 2 million rural people who were without any fixed abode.5 

This is a very serious situation. 

The industrial tariff policy of the United States in afterwar days also seriously affected the farmer, because other countries retaliated with tariffs on imports of American goods, including farm produce. 

Many improvements in the economic condition of the farmer have been made.

Farmers in different sections of the country, such as the fruitgrowers of California and the dairy farmers of Wisconsin, have helped to improve their economic situation by the formation of marketing and production cooperatives. They have clubbed together for the purchase of expensive dairy equipment and other farm necessities and have saved money in this way. They have also organized to provide marketing facilities for their products so that they could receive the profits which formerly went to the middleman, or could pass these savings to the consumer and secure greater sales because of lower prices.

The Federal Government has been attempting to help the farmer for a number of years. We can cite a few of these measures here. The 

Federal Intermediate Credit Act of 1923 extended to the farmer credit facilities of the type best suited to him. President Hoover attempted to help the farmer by the Agricultural Marketing Act of 1929, which set up a Federal Farm Board and a Grain Stabilization Corporation. The latter institution bought up surplus grain and cotton in an endeavor to maintain higher prices, but is now considered a failure because these purchases were later put on the market, which merely added to the crops of the current year and depressed prices very considerably, while at the same time the government lost $350 millions. 

Of the Roosevelt measures designed to help the farmer, the Soil Conservation and Domestic Allotment Act of 1936, is important. It 

"Cf. Social Work Year Book 1941, p. 491.

authorized government expenditure of $500 millions yearly, through the Agricultural Adjustment Administration, for the purpose of conserving and improving the soil of distressed areas, including payments to farmers who divert the use of their land from present crops to those which are of greater use in building up the soil. 

The Farm Security Administration was founded as the Resettle ment Administration in 1935, its present name being adopted in 1937. Its chief present work is the administration of the Bankhead 

Jones Farm Tenant Act of 1937. The aim of this Act and of the 

Farm Security Administration is to enable subsistence homesteads to be settled by families now on relief, and to make loans to farm tenants, farm laborers, share-croppers, and others for necessary repairs and improvements, so that they can raise their own food instead of buying it from stores. Within the past few years more than 

800,000 farm families have been helped by loans, the average loan being $300.

The Rural Electrification Administration finances the electrifica tion of farms under the Rural Electrification Act of 1936. Only 10.9 per cent of American farms in 1935 had electricity not supplied by individually owned electrical plants, compared with almost 100 per cent in Holland and about 90 per cent in France, Germany, and Japan, in the same year. Undoubtedly the size of our country had something to do with this situation here, but the extension of electricity among the farm population in the United States will assist the farmer in his work, increase the comfort of his family, and save the time and energy of his wife and other homeworkers. Rural highways, consolidated schools, rural free delivery, the telephone, and other devices are also becoming increasingly available to the farmer and are helping to improve his economic situation. 

A reorganization of the whole agricultural industry seems greatly needed, but just what form that reorganization should take is at present difficult to say. Certainly it appears that farmers might 'Cf. Yearbook of Agriculture, 1940, p. 791.

162. 

ECONOMIC LIFE 

benefit by more widespread cooperative producing and marketing, such as that discussed in Chapter Thirteen. Cooperation might help the smaller holder to have the use of machinery now available only to those who have large amounts of capital at their disposal; and better facilities for money credit might enable more people to settle happily on the soil. On the other hand, large-scale one-crop farming is widespread in many areas, and other measures would be needed. to combat the difficulties connected with this form of agricultural organization.

Present-day occupational groups provide many social relations. 

In providing for man's material needs of food, clothing, shelter, and various services, a new set of social relations comes into being, which may be termed economic relations. Since we learn from ethics that social intercourse implies duties and obligations, on the one side, and rights on the other, the satisfaction of man's material life in organized economic society leads to many complicated social and hence ethical situations. In his economic life a man may be associated with others in any of the following ways: 

1. As a consumer, he has relations with persons or organizations which supply him directly with his economic needs; in connection with the goods' supplied by stores, farmers, land- or houseowners, railroads and other transportation agencies, servants, doctors, tailors, teachers, plumbers-to cite but a few examples. Every man alive is, of course, a consumer: he needs goods, and satisfies his needs by using them (called "consuming" them, in economics), and in this capacity he has a duty not to cheat the seller in any way, but to pay him a fair price for what he has received, whether the goods "consumed" have been commodities or services. 

2. If a man supplies economic goods or services to consumers, one must regard his economic relations from a different angle. As a producer he has the duty to provide the purchaser with satisfactory 'The term goods in economic language signifies all commodities and services which satisfy human wants.

commodities or services, not to charge him more than a just price, and to adhere by the terms of any just bargain which has been made. 

3. The worker has relations with his employer. His duty is to be loyal to him and to work faithfully and to the best of his ability. 

4. The employer, on the other hand, has duties toward his employees. He must treat his workers with dignity and justice. He must give them a just, living wage, provide good working conditions, and see that their working hours are not excessive, but that they have time for adequate recreation, Sunday rest, and religious observance. He must also refrain from employing women and children in unsuitable occupations. He must not imitate present injustices, such as the paying of salaries to domestic help which are wholly inadequate to current standards of life, and to the quantity and responsible nature of the services required; the payment of less than a living wage to women or colored people, whereas the wage paid to men or white people doing the same work is a living wage, or at least falls less short of it than theirs. 

5. The worker also has relations with his fellow workmen, toward whom he has duties of justice and charity, and for whom he must help make working conditions more pleasurable. 

6. The stockholder and the bondholder must do all in his power to insure that he is not unfairly taking more profits from industry than are his due, and that the workers in the corporations in which he has a financial interest are being justly treated. 

7. The business organizer, or entrepreneur as economists often call him, must see to it that all the interests of industry receive their fair portion of its products. He is entitled to a just remuneration for his services as organizer, and he must insure just treatment to the worker, to the capitalist, and to the consumer. 

8. All those concerned with economic life in any way have a duty to see that the welfare of the whole State is promoted, in so far as they can work for its promotion. 

These ideal economic relations are far from being realized at the present time. How to organize industry so that a more ideal situa-

ECONOMIC LIFE 

tion is brought into being is a problem for economists and sociologists to consider. Some of the plans proposed are discussed or outlined in a later chapter.

Chapter Ten  The purpose of this chapter is: 

1. To realize that working conditions are not wholly satisfactory within the United States, although they have been greatly improved within recent years because of labor union agitation, the action of private employers, and legislation. 

2. To discuss the wage situation in the United States, to understand the need of a family wage and to consider ways and means of installing such a wage in the United States. 

3. To realize the necessity of effective legislation regarding hygiene and dangerous occupations. 

4. To discuss child and woman labor in the United States, and to realize the bad social effects of excessive woman and child labor. 

5. To learn about sweated labor conditions and the need of further action to eliminate them.

As the majority of workers depend for their livelihood on their daily work, it is important that working conditions be favorable. 

The worker's material happiness naturally depends greatly on the conditions under which he is employed. Most men depend on others for the conditions under which they spend most of their working hours and for the extent of remuneration which they receive for their work. Upon these two considerations, working conditions and wages, depend the health and material well-being not only of the workers themselves, but also of their families and, ultimately, of all the people organized as a State - for if large numbers of citizens are forced to live in inadequate circumstances, the rest of the citizens are bound to suffer. 

Working conditions in industry were very bad in the first years 169

ECONOMIC LIFE 

Courtesy U. S. Department of Labor 

Machines without and with adequate safety devices. of capitalism. Since then they have been greatly improved. First, social reformers in the different countries where capitalism flourished secured legislation forcing employers to improve conditions. Catholics continuously tried to secure more favorable working conditions, their most influential work being done after the year 1891, when Pope Leo XIII issued his famous encyclical letter, Rerum Novarum (On the Condition of the Working Classes), which Catholics have since regarded as the Charter of the Workingman. 

Second, the workers themselves banded into labor unions and helped to secure for themselves better conditions and more just wages by demands made as a group upon individual employers, by collective bargaining, and by political influence to secure the passage of favorable labor laws.

Third, employers themselves have undertaken many improvements in working conditions. Sometimes they realized that injus-

tices had been committed toward the worker, and wished to remedy these. Sometimes they were afraid of governmental or popular inquiry into their methods, and improved conditions for this reason. 

Occasionally they saw the advertising value of providing attractive workplaces which the public could visit, or of paying spectacularly high wages which the public would talk about in connection with their product. More often, they realized that well-paid, happy workers, whose recreation needs are provided for, will produce more than poorly paid, discontented employees, who lack funds or initiative to secure needed recreation for themselves, and are worried about the event of possible sickness or unemployment in the family. 

Because some countries developed better working conditions than others, the International Labor Organization was founded in 1919 and was affiliated with the League of Nations, although separate from it. The United States did not join this organization until 1934, but it followed its work and carried out its recommendations even before this time. More than fifty governments subscribed to the 

I.L.O., for their respective States, and their representatives met at intervals to decide upon minimum standards which they all agreed to maintain. The I.L.O. might well be a means of eliminating bad working conditions throughout the world, and it is to be hoped that its influence will increase and not diminish in the future. 

The chief causes of dissatisfaction for the worker are these: (1) the payment of inadequate wages; (2) unhygienic or dangerous working conditions; (3) excessively long working hours; (4) the employment of women and children at tasks unsuited to their sex, age, or strength. We shall consider each of these conditions in turn, and learn what has been done to improve them in the United States. 

Every normally employed man should be paid a living wage; that is, a wage sufficient to maintain himself, his wife, and his children in reasonable comfort.

Because a man must work in order to live, and because he is a rational being possessing human dignity, it is obvious that he has a

ECONOMIC LIFE 

strict right to an adequate wage to live as a human being. Because he is also a social being, and because the majority of men will marry, a normally employed man should receive a wage sufficient to maintain not only himself, but also a wife and children. It is now generally recognized that a man who has done an honest day's work, and who has faithfully fulfilled his duties toward his employer, is entitled to what may be termed a "family wage"; and that a woman who must work has a right to an income sufficient to maintain herself away from home in an honorable and decent manner. 

Persons who conduct a small business for themselves, or have their own family farm, ought to be able to make a profit sufficient to provide for adequate well-being. 

An adequate living wage is one which enables the worker (for the married man, his wife and children also) to live in what Pope 

Leo XIII termed: "reasonable and frugal comfort." This does not mean luxury, but a moderate attainment of the requisites of life. It means food sufficient in quality and quantity to maintain the efficiency of the individual members of the family; sufficient clothes to provide warmth and decency; adequate housing to provide a healthful, moral environment; sufficient fuel to supply warmth; sufficient light for reading purposes; sufficient funds to provide for a reasonable amount of recreation and social intercourse, medical attention, insurance against risks, a befitting education, and church membership.

Of course, the value of work done by a worker may be equal to much more than a living wage, for workers differ in physical strength, in skill, intelligence, and education. Some occupations are unusually dangerous; some demand exceptional intelligence and skill; some men put forth greater efforts and work more quickly than others. Then, too, it is difficult to compute a living wage because its money equivalent varies according to the size of the family, the domestic ability of the housekeeper, and the locality. Prices 

¹ Pope Leo XIII, On the Condition of the Working Classes, p. 27, Paulist Press pamphlet edition (Five Great Encyclicals, p. 22).

Courtesy U. S. Department of Labor 

These women are making garments under sweatshop conditions. Notice the crowding, poor lighting, and generally unsatisfactory surroundings. are lower in some districts than in others, so that the real purchasing value of a low wage in one place may possibly be much higher than the real value of higher nominal wages elsewhere. Nowadays, women expect a husband's wages to be sufficient to pay for beauty products, laundry work, soap, ready-made clothing, and a variety of prepared, baked, and canned goods, which the housewife herself made in former years. It is difficult, therefore, to lay down any legal standard minimum.

Within recent years, wages have been increased in various ways. 

Labor unions have bargained for higher wages for their members. A few employers share small fixed percentages of their profits with the workers, in industrial profit sharing or copartnership schemes.

ECONOMIC LIFE 

Certain states have set up minimum-wage standards.

The Federal 

Public Contract Law of 1936 (Walsh-Healey Act) provided that all manufacturers or dealers engaged in business under federal contract exceeding $10,000 yearly should pay their workers at a rate determined by the Secretary of Labor as a minimum for the industry, for a working week of 40 hours. Since 1938 the Federal Government has established minimum-wage standards for all workers engaged in interstate commerce, with some exceptions. Some states are following with state laws of a similar nature to maintain uniformity. 

The difficulty involved in the question of a legal minimum wage is that the employer too often considers the legal minimum to be the maximum wage he need pay his workers. It is difficult, too, for government authorities to decide just what constitutes a living wage, for the reasons given above, especially because living costs vary in the cities and in the rural districts, as well as in different sections of the country, so that arrangements should be made to take care of the differences in order to be just in the minimum-wage standards set.

The Fair Labor Standards Act, passed in 1938, affects all persons engaged in interstate commerce, with the exception of professional people, executives, those engaged in certain seasonal trades, and some others. It states that an Industry Committee must be set up "as soon as practicable ... for each industry engaged in commerce or in the production of goods for commerce," and is to include representatives of employers and employees in the respective industries, together with disinterested representatives of the public. 

It also states that for the persons concerned, the minimum wage paid must be 30 cents an hour for a 40-hour week until 1945, and 

40 cents an hour for a 40-hour week from 1945 onwards, or whatever rate (not less than 30 cents on hour) the Industry Committee and the Federal Administrator of the Act may agree upon. At any time a committee may decide on the minimum wage of up to 40 cents per hour in the industry concerned. Overtime must be paid at a minimum of one and one half times the normal rate. It was de-

clared illegal to lower wages which are higher than the minimum set by the Act. 

It would seem that laws in favor of a family allowance system would be better than minimum-wage legislation, for this type of wage payment would better provide for human social needs. Most standard budgets for minimum-wage rates consider a "family" living wage as based on a family of four or five, on the assumption that an average family will not have more than two or three children. Prior to the 1939 war, Belgium, France, New South Wales, New Zealand, and certain other countries had made the payment of "family allowances" obligatory, and a few private schemes for such payments are in operation in other countries. The systems vary, but they are all based on a plan somewhat like the following: The employers in the group pay weekly into a central fund a definite fixed sum of money for each worker in their employ. Each employer is pledged to pay to every employee a standard wage which is considered sufficient not only to keep the worker but also a wife and a small family. 

A worker who has more than the specified number of dependents receives an allowance over and above his standard wage for each additional dependent. This additional sum is paid by the employer, who is reimbursed from the central fund for the extra amount paid to the worker. In this way the employer has no incentive to employ only men with small families, and yet each man does get a family wage sufficient for his needs instead of a wage paid him merely according to what he produces, as is the case in most capitalistic societies.

Workers have been increasingly protected by legislation from unhygienic or dangerous occupations and from environment not conducive to good moral standards.

State laws are now usually adequate to protect so far as possible the worker's health and morals. Laws usually provide for clean, sanitary premises, adequate and separate toilet facilities for workers of both sexes, adequate lighting, protection of dangerous machinery

For the protection not only of workers, but also of the public, most states have established health laws requiring periodic examination of restaurant workers, hairdressers, and others to disclose those having contagious diseases.

The length of the working day has been gradually reduced. 

Human nature requires that man should not be a slave, but should have time for rest and recreation, for the use of his intelligence, and for the fulfillment of his religious duties. A reasonably short working

Labor unions and a certain amount of state legislation gradually led to a reduction of working hours to an average of about eight a day, but the Fair Labor Standards Act of 1938, mentioned previously, brought still greater benefits. It provides for a maximum working week of 40 hours at normal rates of pay, but does not hinder overtime provided the overtime rate be not less than one and one half times the regular rate subject to the special prescriptions of the Federal Administrator and the Industry Committee of the industry concerned. These benefits apply to industry, however, for

ECONOMIC LIFE 

the farmer must still work many hours longer each day than those employed in industry and commerce. 

Child labor has been greatly reduced within recent years. 

A certain amount of work around the home is good for any child, because it develops in him good habits of work at a time when habits are easily formed, and it gives him a correct idea of the dignity of labor and of his duty to cooperate with other members of the family. Child labor, however—a technical term which means the remunerative employment of children away from home, or for long hours, at tasks unsuited to their age or strength is certainly detrimental to their physical health and to their mental development. 

The employment of young children restricts their education, deprives them of healthful, carefree play, dulls their intelligence, and usually leads them to blind-alley occupations where they have no opportunity of fitting themselves for the better positions obtainable in later life by the more healthy and better educated. When girls are employed away from home at too early an age, not only is their health often seriously affected, but they may receive little training in caring for a home, and may make poor wives and mothers in later life. The early employment of children, therefore, deprives them to a large extent of the opportunity of becoming valuable citizens of the State.

Child labor is due to a variety of causes: to the industrial system providing for it, to the poverty of parents, to the dissatisfaction of children with school life and their anxiety to be free and to earn their own living, and sometimes to the selfishness or ignorance of parents.

All states now have laws compelling attendance at school until a certain age, or until a certain grade of education has been reached; specifying the age limit below which even part-time gainful employment is prohibited; restricting the number of hours a day which children under a certain age are allowed to work; and requiring certificates testifying to health, age, and education before a child can secure a position. Some states have much better laws than

Courtesy National Child Labor Committee 

Two victims of child labor.

The six-year-old girl is employed 4 hours a day picking hops. The Fair Labor Standards Act of 1938 forbids such work during the times when State laws require the children to be in school. The boy was employed in a lumber mill. Such work is illegal under the Act. others as regards child legislation. The United States Children's 

Bureau does much valuable work in investigating the working conditions of children in agriculture and industry. 

The Fair Labor Standards Act of 1938 prohibits any producer, manufacturer, or dealer from shipping or delivering for shipment in interstate commerce any goods "produced in an establishment situated in the United States in or about which within thirty days prior to the removal of such goods therefrom any oppressive child labor has been employed." The Act does not apply to certain classes of child labor. It defines "oppressive child labor" as "a condition of employment under which (1) any employee under the age of sixteen years is employed by an employer (other than a parent or a person standing in the place of a parent) . . . in any occupation, or (2) any employee between the ages of sixteen and eighteen years is employed by an employer in any occupation which the Chief of the Children's Bureau in the Department of Labor shall find and by

ECONOMIC LIFE 

order declare to be particularly hazardous for the employment of children between such ages or detrimental to their health or wellbeing."

The industrial employment of women requires special safeguards for them, and wherever possible it should be made unnecessary for married women to work outside the home. 

Practically all the states have special legislation to protect women workers. Women are not so physically strong as men, and they need to be physically well if they are to marry and have children, so that the children may get the best possible physical inheritance and home care. This protective legislation for women usually limits the number of hours daily which a woman may work, prohibits women from night work and from mining and other dangerous occupations. Farm labor and domestic service, however, are generally specifically exempt from regulation—a condition which it would seem advisable to remedy.

Census figures show that the number of women employed outside the home has increased greatly in recent years, and the percentage increase is much higher than the growth in population. The reason seems to be the greater opportunities for unmarried women to work in commerce and industry, and also an increased number of married women who work. We called attention in the chapter on the problems of family life to the bad social consequences which frequently arise when married women work outside the home. Most 'Statistical Abstract of the U. S. Census statistics show that in 1940 only 249,521 children between the ages of 14 and 15 were gainfully employed.

married women who work do so because of the insufficiency of their husband's wages, or because for some other reason the family is wholly or in part dependent on their earnings. If a family living. wage were universally paid, this necessity would be greatly diminished. Of course, during World War II a great number of women have worked in industry because of our defense needs. 

Sweated labor combines bad working conditions with inadequate wages, long working hours, and child and woman labor of an oppressive type.

Sweated labor is a term given to a situation where the maximum amount of work is extracted from workers for a wage less than the minimum necessary for the maintenance of human need and dignity, and often under conditions of insanitation and discomfort. Most sweated labor is carried on in the workers' own homes, because conditions in factories have been improved by state legislation. The work is usually of a simple type that it is not considered economically possible to pay well, such as sewing buttons on cards or on garments, making artificial flowers or lamp shades, putting cords on pencils, stringing beads, shelling nuts, and making cigars and cigarettes, and the routine work connected with ready-made clothing. This work is often done by children or by the sick, in povertystricken unhealthy homes. Sometimes it is done by those suffering from contagious illnesses or diseases, and not only increases the illhealth of the worker but also endangers the health of those who buy the goods.

While it seems natural that the consumer should buy goods as cheaply as possible, it is really the consumer's moral duty to pay a just price for his needs and to refuse to take goods made by sweated labor. On the other hand, it is difficult to know whether sweated labor has been employed or not: some goods are sold cheaply because of the savings of mass-production methods, or the use of synthetic products. Sweated labor has considerably diminished in the United States within recent years, although too much of it still exists. The National Consumers' League did much to eliminate

ECONOMIC LIFE 

sweated labor conditions. It issues a label to be affixed to goods made under normally satisfactory working conditions. With the increase of labor unionism, and also enlightened federal and state legislation, sweated labor will probably eventually disappear. It was made possible only under conditions of extreme liberalism, where the employer was able to take advantage of the poor worker's needs for his own profit.

Chapter Eleven  The purpose of this chapter is: 

1. To realize the essential insecurity of the worker in our capitalistic system, and the need for improved conditions in this respect. 

2. To know the meaning of insurance, including social insurance. 

3. To know the provisions which have already been made for the following risks: industrial accidents and diseases; premature death, leaving unprovided dependents; unprovided old age; unemployment. 

The worker is subject to many risks by reason of his employment in our economic system.

The special risks connected with employment under our capitalistic system are these: industrial accidents and diseases; the leaving of unprovided dependents because of the premature death of the breadwinner; unprovided old age; unemployment.

Since the majority of men are dependent upon working for others for a wage, whether or not they can provide for possible risks or contingencies depends upon whether their wage is sufficient to make such provision. However great their will to provide for such risks as early incapacity, death, or unemployment, few wage earners can actually so provide by savings out of wages. Most of these risks are, therefore, better taken care of by insurance. 

Insurance is a private method of providing for the financial expense of risks. Where persons are unable or unwilling to undertake private insurance, or consider it inadvisable, social insurance may provide a solution.

The principle of insurance is this: a certain number of people are subject to a specified risk which will not come to all of them at the 185

Widows of insured workers if they have young children in their care.

SOCIAL SECURITY BOARD FEDERAL SECURITY AGENCY Courtesy Federal Security Agency 

Poster showing benefits to be received under the Social Security Act of 1935. same time, and to some of them may never come. They, therefore, pay a small amount each into a common fund. These small payments together provide a sufficiently large sum to meet the needs of those contributors on whom the risk falls.

If all workers received a living wage, they would have a sufficient income to insure against the risks of life. Unfortunately, all do not receive a living wage. Then, too, some people are improvident and do not consider the duty which they have to their dependents of making adequate provision for them when this is possible. But besides this, where industry is responsible for such important risks as unemployment, it may be inadvisable to have private profit-making insurance companies retain a monopoly of providing insurance against these risks.

Of course, some private insurance is undertaken by individuals, and also by specified groups of individuals, for example, by members of certain labor unions, or by certain employers who insure all their employees under a "blanket insurance" against risks of various

types. Yet because of all the difficulties mentioned above, social insurance seems to be a needed provision for the well-being of the citizens of an industrialized State with two possible exceptions: old age (which might be provided by a straight pension from the State) and industrial accident and disease (which is probably better cared for by the present compensation laws which we shall soon describe). 

By social insurance is meant group insurance for workers, to which the worker is compelled by law to contribute either the whole or part of the cost. Usually the employer or the state pays part of the contributions. This insurance entitles the worker to a certain sum of money (or goods and services, such as medical attention and necessary drugs and surgical appliances) as compensation for losses arising out of certain risks. Social insurance was begun in Germany at the end of the nineteenth century. It was later adopted by Great 

Britain, France, Belgium, and a number of other countries. The 

United States has been considered backward in providing the worker with insurance help against the industrial risks he undertakes, but this situation was remedied first in a few states such as Wisconsin, and has been greatly improved by the Social Security Act of 1935, as amended in 1939.

In most States, the worker is protected by special legislation against expenses incurred by industrial accidents and diseases. 

One of the chief risks for the worker is that of accident or disease (such as lead poisoning), as a direct result of his industrial employment, which will lead to temporary or permanent incapacity for work. For a long time the worker received compensation from the employer only if his injury was due to the specific negligence of the employer, with no contributory negligence on the part of others. At present, however, most states realize that the employer should be held responsible for accidents and diseases contracted by a worker in the course of his employment. All states except one have laws governing the employer's liability to compensate for industrial accidents and most of them compel the employer to insure against this liabil-

ECONOMIC LIFE 

ity. A lesser number of states also compel the employer to insure against his liability for occupational diseases contracted in his employment. The laws vary in the different states, but most of them exempt agricultural workers, domestic servants, and other categories of workers, and also employers of only small groups of workers. 

As insurance rates are lower where working conditions are safer, employers' liability legislation has indirectly led most employers to provide much safer working conditions than before. In addition, many states have laws specifically calling for the protection of the worker by the provision of safety notices, safety appliances, and the like.

The Federal Government gives funds to states which have made provision to rehabilitate workers who are unable to carry on their ordinary work after an industrial accident or disease has been incurred. Most of the states have taken advantage of this federal help, which is administered by the Office of Education within the Federal 

Security Agency. 

Where the breadwinner dies, leaving unprovided dependents, definite State provision is needed to take care of them. 

Under the Social Security Act of 1935, as amended in 1939, the widow, orphans, and dependent parents of a worker insured against old age may receive benefits under certain conditions. We have outlined these provisions in the section of this chapter on old age. If a worker dies who does not come under this old-age social security plan, his wife, children, and other dependents will have to rely upon charity unless he has left sufficient savings for them, or was privately insured. In practically all the states, however, aid is provided for dependent children so that they can stay at home, if their mother is able to care for them properly. This is a form of mother's pension, which compensates her for bringing up worthy citizens of the State, by caring for her children in their own home rather than leaving them to the uncertain care of others. Under the Social Security Act of 1935, state aid is supplemented by federal assistance for depend-

A mine shortly after an explosion which killed 45 men. Mining is one of the most hazardous of all occupations. ent children whose parents are dead or incapacitated and who do not receive benefits under the old-age social security scheme. 

Unprovided old age is a risk which is probably best cared for by pensions, or by social insurance annuity payments. 

Many workers are faced with the impossibility of providing adequately for their old age, especially now that younger people rather than older ones are needed in industry. 

Of course, some workers are able to save sufficient funds, or to take out private annuities to provide for their own old-age needs. 

Others have friends or relatives to support them. Still others receive pensions because they have worked for organizations which provide them after a certain period of service. During this century, however, very many aged persons have been dependent upon the charity of relatives, friends, or relief agencies, and many have had to enter

ECONOMIC LIFE 

charitable institutions. Old people were much better off in the days when more people lived in country districts, where living was cheaper and housing space more commodious. 

Certain States abroad have for long provided pensions either to all people over a certain age, or at least to those with low incomes. Germany was the first to introduce such a scheme. In general, these pensions have not been considered as charity, but as a reward for a long period of time spent as honorable citizens of the State. Many aged couples are welcome in their children's homes, provided they are no financial burden. The payment of a small pension or annuity to the aged is no more costly than housing them in institutions, or providing what is known as "outdoor relief," a term which we shall explain in Chapter Sixteen. In many cases, too, workers who have a fixed sum of money to which they may look forward, are encouraged to save enough in addition to be able to stay in the surroundings to which they were accustomed. Where it may still be necessary for an old person to be cared for in an institution, the pension or annuity will make him self-supporting, and free him of the feeling that he is being given charity.

Until recent years the United States has been rather backward in providing government old-age pensions, although there were some state pension plans for the needy aged and a number of private ones. In 1935, however, the Federal Social Security Act contained two provisions for the needy aged. The provisions of this Act, amended in 1939, were as follows: First, an annuities system was established for workers other than a few categories such as agricultural workers, domestic servants, ships' crews, government employees, and employees in nonprofit-making institutions. Under this scheme both employer and worker are required to pay joint contributions into a fund, at rates varying according to the worker's salary.¹ When the worker reaches the age of 65, if he ceases to work, 1 At the present time the rates payable are 1 per cent each for both employer and employee.
