ALBERT J HORAN BAILIFF Keystone View 

A "bread line" in one of our large cities. This is one of the results of unemployment. he is entitled to monthly annuities of from $10 to $85 a month, regardless of his income or property holdings. The amount of the annuity depends upon the amount paid by him and his employer into the general fund. In addition to the worker's own monthly retirement benefit, supplementary monthly benefits are payable for his wife, after she has reached the age of 65, or for dependent children, until they reach the age of 16 (or, 18 if still in school). The supplementary benefit payable to the aged wife or child will equal half of the worker's monthly retirement benefit, provided the total of all monthly benefits payable to the worker and his family does not exceed (1) $85; (2) twice the worker's individual monthly retirement benefit; (3) 80 per cent of the worker's average monthly wage, whichever amount is the smallest. If the insured worker dies, leaving a widow and dependent children or parents, or simply dependent children or parents benefits are payable as follows:

ECONOMIC LIFE 

To the dependent children, one half the father's individual monthly retirement benefit until they reach the age of 16 (or 18 if still in school). To the widow, three fourths of the deceased worker's individual monthly retirement benefit, if she is 65 or over, of if she has in her care a child entitled to child's benefits. To a dependent parent 65 years or over, who was wholly dependent upon the worker at the time of the worker's death, one half the deceased worker's individual monthly retirement benefit, provided benefits are not being paid to a widow or dependent child.

If an insured worker dies and leaves no survivor entitled to monthly benefits at the time of his death, a lump-sum payment equal to six times his monthly retirement benefit may be made to the widow or widower, child or parent. If no one of these is living, a payment to cover the burial expenses up to but not exceeding six times the monthly benefit will be made to the person who paid them. 

The second provision of the Social Security Act for the aged applies to those who do not get benefits under the first part. It makes provision for financial assistance to states which have laws to care for the dependent aged. If state pension schemes conform to certain requirements, the Federal Government will give part of the monthly pension up to a maximum of $20 per person per month. 

Prolonged unemployment is one of the worst risks to which the worker is subject in our capitalistic system. 

Under a capitalistic system, the worker is dependent upon an employer providing him with work. Whether or not the employer retains him in his employ depends upon factors often beyond the worker's control - the employer may be a poor business manager, business in general may be in a period of depression, or one or the other of the chief objective causes of unemployment listed below may arise to deprive the worker of his means of livelihood. Until quite recently, if the propertyless worker could not find someone to employ him for a wage, he was often dependent upon charity, especially if he lived in a city with no land to grow food supplies.

Ewing Galloway

Another result of unemployment. A group of young men with nothing to do, "bumming" a ride on a freight train.

Unemployment may be due to a variety of causes, and these vary, of course, with each individual case.

Natural causes, such as fires, earthquakes, crop failures, floods, and other disasters may lead to unemployment. These are accidental

ECONOMIC LIFE 

and do not arise from any defect in the social organization, and relief measures are the only remedy for them. 

Personal reasons may cause unemployment. A person may be unemployed because he lacks any specialized education, or because of inadequate preparation for industry, physical disabilities, illness, accidents, or such moral deficiencies as laziness, untruthfulness, and inability to mix well with other workers. These personal reasons could be diminished by proper home and school training in early life for encouragement in Christian living, and by a more widespread introduction of industrial education into the school system and an increase in vocational guidance.

Economic and general social reasons are responsible for most of the unemployment in modern capitalism. Economic organization in general is badly planned: the spirit of Christian solidarity and social cooperation is lacking in the world, with the result that people work for individual profit rather than for the common good of all. Not only is the result of this lack of planning and of Christian brotherly love seen in the national economic system, but it has led to defective educational systems, to the bad distribution of wealth, and to other social defects which will be discussed later, including a lack of cooperation with other nations in foreign trade. Three types of unemployment seem to be due to economic factors: 

a) SEASONAL UNEMPLOYMENT. Since some trades or professions depend on the season or on the weather, those engaged in them are unemployed for certain periods of the year. This type of unemployment is found in the building and painting trades, the summer tourist business, fruit or vegetable picking and canning, the manufacture of furs and wholesale clothing, or the making of novelties used only about Thanksgiving and Christmas time. Seasonal unemployment can be reduced in a variety of ways by the employer responsible for it. For example, a large food-packing company which formerly prepared only bacon for market, now handles a variety of products, such as candy, chewing gum, and canned goods, and provides year-round work for its employees. A motor-car manufactur-

ing firm also makes domestic food coolers and cash registers, and transfers its employees from one branch of its business to another, according as demand warrants. A New England concern, which originally manufactured jewelry boxes only, now makes all types of paper goods. 

b) CYCLICAL UNEMPLOYMENT. Up to the present time, business seems to move in cycles: there are periods of prosperity, followed by periods of depression. In a period of prosperity, manufacturers tend to overstock their goods, so that eventually they are forced to employ fewer men and to reduce prices until the surplus is disposed of. To meet the competition, newly manufactured goods must be sold at lower prices also, and wages must be reduced so that a profit can be made. The greatest number of the world's consumers are the workers themselves; and those who receive reduced wages, or who are unemployed, purchase less goods. In an endeavor to induce consumers to buy, prices are still further reduced and wages again lowered, until most businessmen find it difficult to maintain their businesses and unemployment becomes widespread. Because of fear of further losses, building and repair work are kept at a minimum, decreasing available employment still further. Finally, the time comes when prices are so low that those with money on hand undertake extensive alterations and repairs to their buildings, have new buildings erected, and buy goods in anticipation of future needs. 

These business activities lead to increased employment. The re-employed workers purchase more goods than when they were unemployed, and others likewise make more purchases. These extra purchases increase demand, create employment, and lead to higher prices. Thus the upward movement of what is known as the "trade cycle" begins, and a boom period is once more in the making, seeming inevitably to be followed later by another depression time. 

Such cycles are also influenced by foreign trade demand, by banking, and by money prices abroad.

This type of unemployment can be prevented only by long-term planning of a type not yet undertaken under the capitalistic system,

ECONOMIC LIFE 

pdw toespre he rubor svinar ody szod), be 

Courtesy International Harvester

One of the causes of unemployment the invention of labor-saving devices. 

The one man with the machine can cut more grain in much less time than three men cutting by hand. Besides, the machine shown not only cuts, but threshes and cleans the grain as well. Technological improvement would not cause unemployment if economic organization were more adequately planned. including world trade cooperation. It can be helped to some extent, if public authorities will defer construction and major repair work to slack periods, to provide employment when needed. 

c) TECHNOLOGICAL UNEMPLOYMENT. This is unemployment caused by the invention of laborsaving devices, so that one or two

men can do with machinery the work formerly performed by many more workers. An example of such a laborsaving device is the cotton-picking machine. No one would wish to forego the use of machinery. If it saves much arduous labor we should be unintelligent not to make use of it. But men are more important than machines, and if newly invented machinery is going to throw men out of work, some plan to prevent this ought to be worked out before the machinery is put into use. The men to be affected ought to be placed in some similar line of employment, and if this is not possible, every man who is supplanted by machinery should receive special vocational training for a new line of employment, or he might be paid a lump sum of money sufficient for adjusting himself to a necessary change. The financial benefits of these inventions should not go wholly to the capital owners in the form of dividends, nor wholly to consumers by way of reduced prices. The workers might share in the benefit by a reduction in working hours without reduced pay, which would spread out work for more persons. For those unfortunate workers who will be unemployed, it certainly seems but just that similar work should be found for them. 

Various measures have been taken in the United States to alleviate the problem of unemployment.

The greatest depression in the history of our country took place in the 1930's. Various measures had to be undertaken to alleviate the distress. Private forces were inadequate to cope with the situation, and so the government rightly undertook its duty to promote the well-being of the country, by passing various laws to give government help to promote a better organization of economic life. 

The chief organizational measure undertaken was the N.R.A. which was instituted in 1933 and abandoned in 1935 when the Supreme Court declared it unconstitutional. 

The Federal Government also passed the Wagner-Peyser Act in 1933, which set up a nationwide scheme for the conduct of state employment bureaus, connected with the United States Employment

ECONOMIC LIFE 

Courtesy Civilian Conservation Corps 

Camp survey crew of the CCC laying out a road. Through the CCC the federal government gave work to unemployed young men and opportunity to become acquainted with some occupation or trade. 

Service. Many such bureaus were established during World War I, but because of lack of federal appropriation of funds for their upkeep, they were not very useful from 1919 to 1933. In 1939 these state employment bureaus were connected with a Federal Bureau of Employment Security which was founded in connection with the newly organized Federal Security Agency. This bureau was to co-ordinate help to the states in connection with employment bureaus, and with the social insurance provisions against

unemployment set up through the Social Security Act of 1935, amended in 1939. The state-administered employment bureaus became nationalized on January 1, 1942, because of the need of greater interstate cooperation during the war. Employment bureaus are of great use to the unemployed if employers will register their vacant jobs there, because then they have a greater chance of hearing about all available employment. 

Prior to World War II three other government agencies were especially important in the provision of work for unemployed citizens. Service with the armed forces, as well as defense work, later made them temporarily unnecessary.

First, there was the Works Project Administration, set up to provide such work as the improvement and construction of roads and public buildings, slum clearance, reforestation, reclamation of desert lands, irrigation schemes, and the construction of parks and recreation grounds. The different states contributed part of the funds needed to pay workers engaged in WPA projects which were of especial benefit to those states, and not strictly within the federal sphere. 

Second, the government set up the Civilian Conservation Corps, the CCC. This aimed at providing useful camp work for young men who were unemployed, and to give them a trade so that they could later secure private employment. Its aim was specifically to help young people to maintain a virile character, so that prolonged unemployment would not "pauperize" them, that is, accustom them to receiving help from others instead of using their initiative and striving to find work for themselves. 

Third, the government provided funds through the NYA, or National Youth Administration, so that students in high school and college could be paid for clerical or similar work on a parttime basis, and so be enabled to continue their education. 

The Federal social insurance provisions have had far-reaching effects. A few states had an unemployment social insurance scheme prior to 1935, but in general the United States was behind most other

ECONOMIC LIFE 

Public Assistance

States provide old-age assistance.

Until recently the United States has been rather backward in providing government old-age pensions. This situation has now been largely overcome. countries in the world in making this provision for one of the greatest risks of industry under individualistic capitalism. The 

Social Security Act provides that all employers of eight or more persons are to pay a tax of 3 per cent of the worker's wage into a government fund. Workers employed in agriculture, in domestic service, as seamen, and by the government, by relatives, or by religious, charitable, and educational institutions, are exempt. Railroad workers are also exempt because they are provided for by the Railroad Retirement Act of 1937, and by the Railroad Unemployment Insurance Act of 1938. Since the government wishes to promote state provision for unemployment, the Act has made no provision for the federal distribution of funds. Instead, states are urged to provide a certain type of unemployment insurance, to be financed by a pay-roll tax equal to or greater than the federal tax. If this is done, employers may offset up to 90 per cent of the federal pay-roll

tax by contributions to the approved state unemployment fund. 

Benefits vary in the different states from $5 to about $15 weekly. 

Although Congress was authorized to appropriate annual financial subsidies for the management of the program, first through the Social Security Board and, since 1939, through the Bureau of Employment Security, obviously most of the expenses of the scheme come from the direct tax on employers, and there is much discussion as to whether this additional tax will not hamper industry, eventually lower wages, and lead to further economic distress. Whatever be the merits or demerits of this particular scheme, however, it seems that until the widespread insecurity of employment is overcome by better economic organization, and until all workers receive a living wage so that they themselves can insure against the risks of unemployment, some form of social insurance against unemployment is essential to national well-being. Most of the states of the union have now passed unemployment insurance acts to take advantage of the funds available through the Social Security Act for their citizens.

Chapter Twelve LABOR UNIONS The purpose of this chapter is: 

1. To realize that under the capitalistic organization of economic life the worker is at a disadvantage unless he forms labor unions for collective bargaining, and that the worker's right to organize labor unions is upheld by the Church and also by the law of the United States. 

2. To learn the organization of labor unions in the United States. 

3. To know the chief methods employed in collective bargaining by both workers and employers, and especially to learn under what circumstances a strike may be both just and necessary. 

4. To learn the advantages and disadvantages of collective bargaining and to realize the cause of some of the bad aspects which have arisen in this connection. 

With the growth of individualistic capitalism, the distribution of wealth became ever more concentrated, and the workers became poorer until they learned the advantages of organization in labor unions.

The guild system of the Middle Ages was by no means ideal. 

Nevertheless, it was built upon the Christian concept of general social solidarity and the need of everyone working for the mutual good of employer, worker, and the public. The Christian social concepts which motivated the members of the guilds were provided for by means of guild regulations, and included the idea of a just price to be charged to the consumer. Because guild regulations became too minute and hampering to private enterprise, and also because of the decay of the idea of working for the common good, with the advance of Protestantism, individualism took the 205

ECONOMIC LIFE 

place of a more correct conception of society. Catholics, Jews, and 

Protestants alike imbibed this new spirit of individual liberty without restraint, so that, when capitalism developed, with its need of large capital resources for large-scale business organization, profit seeking grew and capitalists and organizers made large profits out of business enterprise. The condition of the worker ought to have improved with the greater productivity made possible by the use of machinery and the organization of business on a large scale. For a long time, however, it grew increasingly worse, for workers became mere earners of minimum subsistence wages, and most of them came to live in towns near factories and workshops, and were without the means of providing for the risks inherent in the industrial system of today, as discussed in the last chapter. During the nineteenth century working conditions became worse than ever before in the history of the western world. As was shown in Chapter 

Nine, the organizers of business enterprise controlled the other three factors of production: land, labor, and capital. Since capital is so essential in large-scale industry, organizers - managers and investment bankers - conduct business for profit to the stockand bondholders rather than for the social good of the country as a whole, showing no regard for the human dignity of the worker. 

A consideration of the worker's dignity as a human being includes, of course, his right to a family living wage whenever he does a full day's work, and his right to good working conditions and to a socioeconomic organization which will ensure him a minimum of risks. This latter right, of course, the worker as a citizen and a voter must help to secure for himself through the organization of the State, with its government. 

The social aspect of economic organization was forgotten. Pope 

Pius XI in his Quadragesimo Anno has shown us what occurs when this takes place, for he says:¹ "The obvious truth is that in labor, especially hired labor, as in ownership, there is a social as well as 1 ¹ Pope Pius XI, On the Reconstruction of the Social Order, p. 23 (Five Great 

Encyclicals, p. 144).

LABOR UNIONS

Land, labor, capital, and organization are all essential in the production of wealth. Part of the consumer's payment covers rent and expenses. Under capitalism the balance goes to interest on bonds and to dividends on stocks, in which the organizers frequently have a large share. a personal or individual aspect to be considered. For unless human society forms a truly social and organic body; unless labor be protected in the social and juridical order; unless the various forms of human endeavor, dependent one upon the other; are united in mutual harmony and mutual support; unless, above all, brains, capital, and labor combine together for common effort, man's toil cannot produce due fruit. Hence, if the social and individual character of labor be overlooked, it can be neither equitably appraised nor properly recompensed according to strict justice." 

Eventually, through the neglect of the social character of economic organization under our capitalistic system, conditions grew so bad that measures were taken to improve them. The workers realized that they could form an organization for themselves to help to improve conditions. Despite the organizers' monopoly of land, capital, and organization, and despite the weakness of the workers' position, the workers themselves provide the fourth necessary factor in production, which is labor. Alone, their bargaining power may be much weaker than that of the employer, but since their labor is

ECONOMIC LIFE 

necessary for the conduct of his business, they can attain an almost equally strong bargaining position by organizing labor unions. 

Despite the improved condition of affairs by means of the direct action of labor unions, and by the indirect action of both labor unions and humanitarians, through the promotion of legislation to assist the condition of the worker, even today the wealth of the country is very badly distributed. In 1935-36 it was discovered² that only one third of the total number of families and individuals living away from their families received an income of $1,450 and over; another third received the very low annual income of $780 or less; and the annual income of the other third was between $780 and $1,450. A labor union is a work group formed by workers to maintain or improve their working conditions by means of collective bargaining.

A labor union is an association of workers banded together to maintain or improve their working conditions by means of collective bargaining (bargaining as a group). A labor union may be either of two types. It may be a trade-union, comprising workers engaged 2 

² Consumer Income in the U. S. 1935–36, National Resources Committee, U. S. 

Printing Office, Washington, 1938, p. 95. 'Ibid.

LABOR UNIONS 

209 in the same trade or occupation, such as an association of bricklayers or bakers or printers; or it may be an industrial union, composed of workers engaged in the same industry, for example, a union of builders, miners, railroad employees, or of clothing workers. There is also a type of union called a company union. This is an association of all the workers within a factory or shop to discuss working conditions within their group, and to present their ideas and wishes, through a committee, to their employer. This type of organization is useful in any given company, but it is not as useful in collective bargaining as a labor union which organizes the employees of a number of different companies. This chapter deals not with company unions but with labor unions.

Each member of the labor union pays an annual fee in weekly or monthly installments, to provide for necessary expenses, for payment to workers who may be on a union strike, and for the salaries of representatives called business agents. Some labor unions also have mutual insurance associations, and have set up education funds for members.

The union bargains collectively with the employers of its members, through its business agents. Business agents are specialized representatives who can usually deal more competently with employers than could individual workers, because of their generally more expert knowledge of the labor market and of wages and conditions within the industry in all parts of the country. Because the business agents are employed by the union, they are independent of the employers with whom they negotiate, and therefore they can state the workers' case fearlessly without risk of losing their own employment. This cannot be done in a company union, useful though the company union may be for intercompany matters, such as discussing recreation facilities, ideas for improving output, and the like. 

Labor unions strive to secure closed-shop contracts from employers. A closed shop is a workshop or factory where only members of a labor union are allowed to work. A closed shop is unfair when it is used in connection with a closed union, that is, it is unfair if workers

The Church has consistently taught that workers have the right to organize into labor unions.

For a long time now, notably through the Encyclicals Rerum 

Novarum (1891) of Pope Leo XIII, and Quadragesimo Anno (1931) 

of Pope Pius XI, the Church has taught that workers have a right to band together in labor unions. This does not mean, of course, that the Church upholds all the practices of every labor union. As 

Pope Leo XIII has said: "Workman's Associations should be so organized and governed as to furnish the best and most suitable means for attaining what is aimed at, that is to say, for helping each individual member to better his condition to the utmost, in body, mind, and property. It is clear that they must pay special and principal attention to piety and morality.* 

*Pope Leo XIII, On the Condition of the Working Classes, pp. 33-34. (Five 

Great Encyclicals, p. 27.)

LABOR UNIONS 

211 In his encyclical letter written especially to the United States in 1939, Pope Pius XII confirmed this view, pointing out that the particular form that labor union organization might take would vary according to conditions in the different countries. He says: "Because sociability is one of man's natural requirements and since it is legitimate to promote by common effort decent livelihood, it is not possible without injustice to deny or to limit either to the producers or to the laboring and farming classes the free faculty of uniting in associations by means of which they may defend their proper rights and secure the betterment of the goods of soul and of body, as well as the honest comforts of life. But to unions of this kind, which in past centuries have procured immortal glory for Christianity, and for the professions an untarnishable splendor, one cannot everywhere impose an identical discipline and structure, which therefore can be varied to meet the different temperament of the people and the divers circumstances of time."5 

In 1937 the Association of Catholic Trade-Unionists (ACTU) 

was founded for the following purposes: 

1. To bring Catholic workers into labor unions; 

2. To spread the social teachings of the Church so that workers will realize that Christianity recognizes the workers' rights and needs. 

3. To make Catholic membership in labor unions an organized force for the permeation of the unions with a Christian spirit of charity as well as a knowledge of justice regarding the rights of all the classes and people in the State. 

ACTU is now organized in a number of cities. It publishes a journal called The Labor Leader in New York, Chicago, Detroit, and Milwaukee. It promotes schools for Catholic workers, where they may learn the principles of labor unionism, the organization of their particular union, the general social teaching of Christianity, and also the elements of parliamentary law, so that they can take their part in the formal meetings of the unions to which they belong. 'Pope Pius XII, To the Church in the United States, p. 19.

Harris and Ewing

William Green, president of the American Federation of Labor. 

Labor unions are upheld by law in the United States.

The Wagner National Labor Relations Act of 1935, designed to take the place of the defeated N.R.A. legislation in protecting the right of organized labor, specifically states in Section 7 that: "Employees shall have the right of self-organization, to form, join, or assist labor organizations, to bargain collectively through representatives of their own choosing, and to engage in concerted activities, for the purpose of mutual aid or protection."

LABOR UNIONS 

213 

Harris and Ewing 

Philip Murray, president of the Congress of Industrial

Organizations. 

Section 8 of the same Act declares that employers must not, among other practices, interfere with, restrain, or coerce employees in the exercise of the rights guaranteed in Section 7; discriminate in regard to the hire of union members, or make terms or conditions of employment to encourage or discourage membership in any

ECONOMIC LIFE 

national labor organization; refuse to bargain collectively with the representatives of employees. This Act was, of course, a distinct victory for labor.

There are two major labor unions within the United States: the 

American Federation of Labor and the Congress of Industrial 

Organizations. 

The history of labor unionism in the United States is one of the formation of rather small and unsuccessful unions throughout the nineteenth century until, in 1881, the American Federation of Labor was founded as a federation of national and international (Canadian) labor unions. The American Federation of Labor (A. F. of L.), headed by William Green, is based on voluntary cooperation of the individual unions belonging to it.

In 1935 a number of the union delegates at the annual meeting of the A. F. of L. criticized the fact that the federation was chiefly one of trade-unions, and that it was not furthering the unionization of such mass production industries as steel, rubber, automobile manufacturing, or helping the unskilled to secure the benefits of unionization. It was pointed out that with the increased mechanization of industry and the decreased employment of skilled craftsmen the bargaining power of unions was declining. A proposed program to enlarge the policy of the A. F. of L. and to promote industrial unions in the United States was defeated. In the same year, however, John L. Lewis, president of the United Mine Workers Union, with seven other industrial union presidents, formed a Committee for Industrial Organization to hasten the unionization of the unorganized workers in mass production and other industries, upon an industrial basis. In 1936 the A. F. of L. suspended from membership those unions which had joined the C.I.O., while still maintaining membership within the A. F. of L. ranks, and since that time the C.I.O. has become an independent federation of industrial unions. 

There were approximately one million C.I.O. members at the time of its formation. Today the C.I.O. (whose name was changed to

LABOR UNIONS 

215 Congress of Industrial Organizations in 1938) comprises some 42 unions and claims 4 million members. Philip Murray was elected head of the C.I.O. in 1940. During the past few years union membership has greatly increased, and today the A. F. of L. also claims over 

5 million members (5,400,000 in 1942). At the time of writing there is still a serious rivalry between the two federations. 

There is also a communistic union in the United States: The Industrial Workers of the World (I.W.W.), founded in 1905. This is a loosely bound group of any workers who subscribe to its revolutionary ideas and apply for membership. It claims some 130,000 members.

Disputes between the employer and a labor union are best settled by amicable agreement, by conciliation, or by arbitration. 

It is the business agent's duty not only to make terms with employers on behalf of the labor unionists he represents, but also to discuss with them any disputes which may arise regarding working conditions, wages, or the treatment of individual members. 

Disputes settled privately are said to be settled by conciliation. 

If the dispute is serious, and neither side will compromise or accede to the demands of the other, the best solution of the difficulty is recourse by both parties to mediation by means of arbitration. 

Arbitration is an agreement by both parties to submit their case to an independent judge, called an arbitrator, who will weigh the merits of the respective arguments and pass an impartial judgment to which both parties should agree to abide. 

Among other provisions, the Wagner National Labor Relations Act of 1935 established the National Labor Relations Board, composed of three nonpartisan members to be appointed by the President for a period of five years. The purpose of the Act was to make the bargaining power of the worker more nearly equal to that of the employer, and to protect the worker from "unfair labor policies." The duty of the Board is not conciliation in general disputes, but to watch over the operation of the Act. If any unfair

ECONOMIC LIFE 

Associated Press 

A clash between pickets and a nonstriking worker. A strike may be an entirely just means of setting grievances, but a real effort must be made to bar violence, destruction, and unjust coercion of all types. labor practices as set forth in the Act are perpetrated, in interstate industries, then it is the duty of the Board to set a time for the compulsory hearing of evidence on both sides, and to hear the evidence. The decision of the Board is binding on both workers and employers.

The Department of Labor has a special division entitled the United States Conciliation Service, founded in 1913, whose work is to investigate disputes and to try to bring about peaceful settlements. This division has no law to enforce. Special boards were created by the Railroad Labor Disputes Act of 1934 to settle

LABOR UNIONS 

217 railroad disputes. Several states have provided for permanent state boards of conciliation and arbitration for industrial disputes. These facilities, however, merely provide the machinery for the settlement of disputes by arbitration, for neither party to a dispute is obliged to submit the case to these federal or state agencies or boards, or to accept their decisions. 

The chief weapon of the labor union is the strike, and in certain circumstances a strike may be both just and necessary. 

Just as the employer may use the weapon of discharge under legitimate circumstances, so the worker, under certain conditions, has the right to refuse to work and to declare a strike. A strike is a simultaneous and concerted cessation of work by a group of workers to enforce a demand upon an employer, the strikers taking measures to retain the positions they have temporarily vacated by means of picketing and the use of the boycott. 

Picketing is a technical term which usually means establishing workers' agents near the employer's place of business to persuade or coerce nonunion labor from taking the work vacated by the strikers. A boycott is a combination of wage earners to enforce a demand upon an employer by withholding, or inducing others to withhold, business dealings from him. 

Section 13 of the Wagner National Labor Relations Act, after enumerating the procedure to be taken to prevent unfair labor practices, specifically states that nothing in the Act is to be "construed so as to interfere with or impede or diminish in any way the right to strike." Catholic social teaching also upholds the right to strike in certain circumstances. Some people have misinterpreted the words of Pope Pius XI because, when describing the Fascist State of Italy, in his encyclical, Quadragesimo Anno, he says that in this corporate State "Strikes and lockouts are forbidden." He does not approve of this condition, however. On the same page "Pope Pius XI, On the Reconstruction of the Social Order, p. 30 (Five Great 

Encyclicals, p. 151).

ECONOMIC LIFE 

he says: "It is feared that the new syndical and corporative institution possesses an excessively bureaucratic and political character and that, notwithstanding the general advantages referred to, it risks serving particular political aims rather than contributing to the initiation of a better social order." 

Although many strikes and boycotts have been unjust, sometimes the workers' only redress against an employer is a resort to these means. As they lead to very grave consequences to the worker himself, to his family, to the general public, and to the employer, workers may justly make use of their monopolistic power in this manner only on approximately the same conditions as apply to a just war: 

1. The strike or boycott must have been called on account of a just grievance, and there must be no malicious intent; 

2. Peaceful means of settling the grievance by all other possible means must have failed; 

3. The means taken must not involve violence or destruction of property. 

4. There must be reasonable hope of success, or at least a hope of ultimate advantage to the worker. 

5. The benefit resulting from the strike or boycott must exceed the damage caused, both to the employer and to the workers. 

The sit-down strike, where workers actually occupied the employer's workplace and refused to move until their demands were acceded to, was a new technique developed in 1937 and declared illegal by a decision of the Supreme Court in 1939. 

A more constructive measure, sometimes used by labor unions, is the union label. The union label is a mark which some labor unions ask employers of union labor to affix to their products or to display in their places of business. The public is then urged to buy only products bearing this mark. The use of the label is frequently not only effective in inducing manufacturers to employ union men, but it assists the consuming public to know whether or not goods are manufactured under just labor conditions; and

LABOR UNIONS 

219 both the workers and the producers of the goods can thus retain the public confidence in the good workmanship, as well as in the sound labor policies under which they were produced. 

The lockout is the employer's chief weapon against labor unions. 

A lockout takes place when an employer refuses to continue in his employ laborers who will not work on his conditions. As the strike is sometimes imperative to secure justice from an employer, so a lockout is sometimes necessary to force employees to come to reasonable terms. A just lockout must have the same characteristics as a just strike.

Sometimes employers set up “black lists," and refuse to employ workers known to be connected with the labor movement. Some employers, too, try to force their employees to belong only to their company union, and not to become affiliated with an outside organization. As we said, a company union is very useful, particularly for settling internal disputes, but it would usually be to the disadvantage of the bargaining power of the workers to belong to the company union alone. 

Collective bargaining by means of labor unions has many advantages. 

1. As has just been explained, workers, whose individual bargaining power is inherently weaker than that of the richer employer, are effectively enabled to meet the employer on more equal ground when represented by a labor union agent, and are thus protected from unjust treatment. 

2. The workers who belong to a labor union have the advantage of the union agent's ability to drive a better bargain than would be possible individually, for the union representative can keep in touch with conditions in the industry and in the country in general. 

3. All employers have to pay labor union members the same wage, and give them approximately the same working conditions, at least as to hours of labor. The just employer is thus protected from the

ECONOMIC LIFE 

competition of those who, without the compulsion of labor unionism, might give their workers lower wages, and make them work longer hours, and thus be able to undersell competitors who are treating their employees justly. 

4. Labor unionism, by eliminating wage competition among workers, makes skill and efficiency the determining factors in a man's employment, to the advantage of the employer. 

5. A labor union pledges itself to keep all agreements made with the employer on behalf of its members, which is another advantage to the employer. 

6. Labor unions furnish the machinery through which the employer and employee may cultivate friendly relations and obtain a clearer insight into their individual problems. 

There are also some disadvantages to labor unionism. These, however, are outweighed by the advantages. 

Labor unions were at first declared illegal. Although the workers' need of mutual protection by means of labor unions has now been recognized by law, nevertheless labor unions, as they now exist, have certain disadvantages. The chief disadvantages of labor unionism have been as follows: 

1. Labor unions have often caused increased hostility between employer and employed, instead of working toward that cooperation which is necessary for the common welfare. 

2. In certain cases, labor unions have led to a labor monopoly equally as obnoxious as any monopoly of capital, and have resulted in unjust and unreasonable demands by workers from employers, and in disastrous strikes. 

3. There is a tendency for labor unions to benefit skilled workers and to neglect those who are poorly paid, who are in greater need of protection. Unskilled workers are gradually being organized, however, especially through the C.I.O. organization. 

4. The interests of the consumer and the general public are often disregarded when unionists are seeking to enforce their claims.

LABOR UNIONS 

221 

5. Labor unions have sometimes led to "racketeering," where a man has been able to secure control over a union, and has dictated his terms to both employer and worker, getting large sums of money from both parties: from the one, by threatening a strike or boycott if payments are not forthcoming, and from the other by refusing permission to belong to the union without large fees for the privilege, or by failure to make a correct accounting for the expenditure of union funds.. 

6. Some labor unions have abused their semimonopolistic power to restrict the output of workers. Others have charged excessive entrance fees to members, so that many men were unable to join. 

Others, again, have demanded equal wages for all men in the union, without sufficient attention to individual differences in efficiency and skill. Some unions have limited the number of apprentices received, without just cause, to create an artificial shortage of labor in a particular occupation. The rise of “jurisdictional disputes" has also been serious, where work has come to a complete standstill while two unions of men doing similar yet different work have disputes as to which of the two should allow its members to perform a certain operation. 

7. High industrial wages lead sometimes to high prices of goods needed by farmers, who are not unionized and find these costs very burdensome at times.

Special legislation has, on occasion, been necessary to curtail the power of the unions, but despite these adverse aspects, the advantages of labor unions definitely outweigh the disadvantages and, as we said, the right of a worker to organize into a workers' association (labor union) for the betterment of his conditions is explicitly taught by the great social encylicals Rerum Novarum and Quadragesimo Anno. If the practices of some labor unions are bad, this is probably because people with high Christian ideals and a spirit of apostolic service have not joined the unions or, if they are union members, they have not taken the time and the trouble needed to gain the necessary knowledge to be given positions of responsibility.

Chapter Thirteen  The purpose of this chapter is: 

1. To realize the need of reorganizing our economic system and to learn some of the major proposals for change which have been made. 

2. To learn the type of economic organization proposed by Pope 

Pius XI, called corporatism, and to realize that corporatism is not identical with the "Corporate State" but may exist in any good type of political organization. 

3. To know what is meant by collectivism, including communism and some of the chief types of socialism. 

4. To know the economic system under Fascism and Nazism. 

5. To realize why the corporate State in Italy and Portugal, and also the N.R.A., are not identical with the corporatism proposed by Pope 

Pius XI. 

6. To learn about Distributism. 

7. To make a study of the chief types of cooperation. 

Pope Pius XI pointed out an excellent scheme for reorganizing social and economic life without foregoing many of the advantageous features of capitalism. 

Most people today are quite convinced that there is something seriously wrong with our organization of the work group or occupational society. Certain industries and trades are organized by their owners for greater profits; certain classes of workers are organized for their own benefits in labor unions; some industries, such as the railroads and other public utilities, are sometimes managed by the State for the common good, or are regulated by State law. General organization for the good of everyone within the country, and mindful of the good of persons in other countries in the world is, however, entirely lacking in capitalistic society. 225

ECONOMIC LIFE 

In his encyclical on the "Reconstruction of the Social Order" (Quadragesimo Anno), issued in May, 1931, to commemorate the fortieth anniversary of the Rerum Novarum, Pope Pius XI furnished us with a general plan for the reorganization of occupational society. 

In this encyclical he shows that the individualistic spirit of cutthroat competition which has existed since the industrial revolution, and the sharp class distinctions. between employer and employed, cannot much longer endure without the complete breakdown of the existing organization of economic society. "Just as the unity of human society cannot be built upon class warfare," he says, "so the proper ordering of human affairs cannot be left to free competition. . . . Free competition ... Free competition . . . though within certain limits just and productive of good results, cannot be the guiding principle of the economic world." 

The Pope wished, therefore, that all social groups within each State become imbued with the spirit of justice and charity, and that individualism in all spheres be replaced by the idea that society is one organic whole, all the members of which should work together for mutual good. So far as economic groups are concerned, he advocated a general plan as follows: 

He hoped that all workers, without exception, would voluntarily band together into labor unions, and that employers should also voluntarily join employers' associations, within each trade or industry. For each trade or industry, therefore, there would be a labor union and an employers' association, and these would be able to discuss their separate interests and the practical issues of their side of the business enterprise. Representatives of these separate associations for employers and employed would then combine in local joint committees, so that "Brains, capital and labor combine together for common effort," and both parties could discuss their problems and their needs. Such a combined organization would 

¹Pope Pius XI, On the Reconstruction of the Social Order, pp. 28-29 (Five 

Great Encyclicals, p. 149). 'Ibid., p. 23 (Five Great Encyclicals, p. 144).

WORKERS 

IN EACH 

FACTORY TRADE UNIONS (TO UNITE . 

WORKERS) EMPLOYERS AND MANAGERS EMPLOYERS' ASSOCIATIONS (TO UNITE EMPLOYERS) JOINT BOARD REPRESENTATIVES OF WORKERS AND EMPLOYERS) 

obviously not countenance employees who did not fulfill their duties to the employer, nor employers who took advantage of the weaker position of their employees. The 

Pope wished, of course, that each worker should receive a living wage, but he hoped that the "wage contract should, when possible, be modified somewhat by a contract of partnership . . . in this way wage earners are made sharers in some sort in the ownership, or the management, or the profits." 

Where the employer finds it impossible to pay a living wage, the Pope says that employers and employed must then "join in their plans and efforts to overcome all difficulties and obstacles, and let them be aided in this wholesome endeavor by the wise measures of the public authority. In the last extreme, counsel must be taken whether the business can continue, or whether some other provision should be made for the workers. The guiding spirit in this crucial decision should be one of mutual understanding and Christian harmony between employers and workers."4

STATE PRIVATE INDUSTRY (JOINT ACTION OF INDUSTRY & STATE WHERE NEEDED) 

The Pope's plan for occupational society. 

Business is not only to be organized into local groups in the Pope's scheme. His Holiness would have representatives of the local associations meeting in national councils (vocational groups) for each trade or industry, to decide all matters of major importance. 'Ibid., p. 23 (ibid., p. 144). 'Ibid., p. 34 (ibid., p. 145).

ECONOMIC LIFE 

But he does not wish employers and employed to band together solely for their own selfish ends. He is of the opinion that not only must there be a reasonable relationship between wages and profits, but that a reasonable relationship should also be taken into account between these and "the prices obtained for the products of the various economic groups, agrarian, industrial, etc." He would have the State leave the settlement of all business of minor importance to these smaller groups, so that it will "carry out with greater freedom, power, and success the tasks belonging to it, because it alone can effectively accomplish these, directing, watching, stimulating, restraining, as circumstances suggest or necessity demands.”

Finally, in order to safeguard the general interests of all concerned, he would have national economic conferences held for employers, workers, capitalists, consumers, and representatives of the government. And since, as he so wisely says, nations are "largely dependent one upon the other, and need one another's help," he would like to see international "economic cooperation by prudent pacts and institutions."

In this way harmful competition would be eliminated, and employers, capitalists, workers, consumers, all the people of the State, would be united, so that occupational society would become truly a harmonious organism, working for the common good. 

It is sometimes said that this idea of the corporate order (corporatism) is the same as the corporate State of Fascist Italy, or the same as Guild Socialism. This, however, is not so, as will be evident when we discuss some of the schemes to reorganize economic life which have been proposed by others. 

Ibid., p. 25 (ibid., p. 146). 

Ibid., p. 26 (ibid., p. 147). 'Ibid., p. 29 (ibid., p. 150). For a further brief discussion of the Pope's scheme of corporatism, see the Statement of the Archbishops and Bishops of the Administrative Board of the National Catholic Welfare Conference, The Church and the Social Order, pp. 1923; also Ross, E. J., What Is Economics?, pp. 238-242; Ross, E. J., Fundamental 

Sociology, pp. 425-429.

Some people have proposed collectivism as a remedy for the defects of the capitalistic economic system. 

So many different philosophies and plans are represented by the terms socialism and communism that it will be best for our purpose to group them under one common term collectivism, and to use this word in the most general sense to indicate the system which would change the capitalistic system through collective ownership of one or more types of property or enterprise now in private hands. These systems all deny that man has a natural right to own private property.

Socialists consider that we would probably be better organized if while retaining private ownership of consumption goods (goods to be used privately and not for business enterprise), some or all means of production, sometimes called productive property or economic goods (goods used to produce other goods in economic life, such as machinery, buildings, tools), were to be owned in common. Some socialists want only land to be owned in common, through the State: such are the single-tax socialists. Others want little more than public utility companies (railroads, gas, electricity, and water supply, and similar enterprises) to be State owned. Others, such as the guild socialists, would like to see industry owned by the workers and employers combined, and meeting at regular intervals with the government.

Communists want everything to be owned in common, by the State, and administered for the good of all, so that there would be no private ownership in anything.

Both communism and socialism are bad philosophies which go against the natural law, for they deny man's natural right of ownership. This right is discussed in Appendix II. It must, of course, be fully realized that this right is a general right only. Pope Pius XI, in his encyclical Quadragesimo Anno, deplores the fact that some persons have "falsely and unjustly" accused Pope Leo XIII and the Church as upholding "the wealthier classes against the prole-

ECONOMIC LIFE 

tariat," and he calls attention to the fact that while one has a right to one's property, lawfully acquired, one has a duty of "putting one's own possessions to proper use."10 This duty is not a matter of civil law, but is a matter between the owner and God. The State, of course, has, within the limits of necessity, a right to restrain ownership for the common good. Thus the State has the right to purchase property, even against the will of the owner, provided the purchase is necessary for the general good of the citizens and a just price is paid. Similarly, the State has the right to tax property to provide itself with the funds necessary to carry out its duties, and it may grade taxation so that larger property owners pay a proportionately larger tax.

Bolshevistic communism, established in Russia in 1918, is a bad system of economic life, because it denies man the natural right of ownership. It is also a bad system of political life, because it fails to give a correct place to man's individual liberty, and to his important end of living: to attain eternal happiness. God's place in life is ignored, and the State is considered all powerful. The 

Russian State is therefore called a totalitarian State: a State which wishes to control everything about man. The alliance which our country made with Russia in World War II to help to overthrow the Nazi domination of Europe, may be considered as one with the Russian people in distress, who cannot be blamed for their government,¹¹ and not as an approval of the political tenets of bolshevism. 11 Fascism was an economic and a political system founded to oppose communism, but it, too, resulted in a totalitarian State. 

Fascism was founded by Mussolini in 1922 to oppose communism and to uphold the capitalistic position of the bourgeois middle class. 

Instead of aiming at communist working-class predominance, Fascism aimed at the glorification of the State by class collaboration. 

All the major branches of economic life were divided into twenty- "Pope Pius XI, On the Reconstruction of the Social Order, p. 15 (Five Great 

Encyclicals, p. 137). 

10 Ibid., p. 16 (Five Great Encylicals, p. 138). "See what Pope Pius XI says on this subject, On Atheistic Communism, p. 17 (Five Great Encyclicals, p. 186).

two corporations composed of equal representatives of workers and employers, plus technical experts and representatives of the Fascist government party. These corporations were not autonomous as were the medieval guilds, and as the Pope envisaged a corporate economic organization; instead, they were instruments through which the 

Fascist government carried out the regulation of production for the political power and prosperity of the Italian State. Political, social, and intellectual freedom was abolished in the interests of the State. Education was considered primarily a matter of State and not of parental control. Religion was tolerated, but was somewhat though not wholly subordinated to State interests. In other words, Fascism was a corporate State organization, making the State totalitarian in its hold over the people. The corporate economic order of Pope Pius XI, though similar, is in effect quite different. 

It recognizes all man's natural rights, including liberty, and gives the State its correct place. It is a system which could function within any good political organization, hence it could function equally well in a democratic State and in a good monarchical State. 

Nazi socialism was also a political system founded to oppose communism and is even more totalitarian than is Italian Fascism. 

German Fascism is best known as Nazism, a word derived from the title National German Socialist Workers' Party. Originally it had nothing in common with socialism, since it was founded to protect capitalism against socialism and communism. All economic life came under the control of the State, when Nazism was inaugurated by Hitler in 1933, but no corporate State has been established. Labor unions have been changed by law into a political organization called the Labor Front, and members of the Nazi political party are placed in all positions of authority. All economic and social life is considered as subordinated to the State, for the glorification of the German people. Religion ranks as a secondary matter, to be permitted only in so far as the State does not consider it harmful to the nation. The State completely dominates the life

ECONOMIC LIFE 

of the people. Education is entirely a State matter, for the development of Nazi leaders (and followers). Since women's best service to the State is considered to be that of providing a home and raising future citizens, the freedom of women has been gradually curtailed, so that they will learn household arts. They are encouraged to marry early. Nazism is chiefly a political system, but it was founded upon an economic need of the people, and in its economic aspects is completely State dominated. 

Portugal inaugurated a corporate economic order in 1935 which is close to the plan of Pope Pius XI, but not identical with it. 

The N.R.A. was an American attempt to organize economic society in a corporate manner. 

In 1935 Dr. Salazar, a university professor, set up a corporate economic order in Portugal on the lines suggested by Pope Pius XI. 

This included corporations for the economic organization of the country, with the State's position subordinated to that of guiding, directing, and restraining them, as mentioned in the encyclical. The 

State is not considered to be all important, and the Church is given its place in social life. The Portuguese State, therefore, is not totalitarian. On the other hand, it may be said to be authoritarian, because Salazar wields all the power within the State. This situation is not, of course, necessarily bad. Indeed, Salazar seems to be a good and wise ruler; but as his successor might be anything but that, the 

Portuguese system may not develop along desirable lines in later years.

It was the Pope's idea that workers and employers should organize into a corporate organization of their own volition, whereas in Portugal the organization was forced upon the people within the State. This was true also of the N.R.A., which was established in the United States in 1933 and was declared unconstitutional in 1935. 

Many Catholics, including the well-known Monsignor John A. Ryan were in favor of the N.R.A., precisely because it, too, was somewhat similar to the Pope's scheme, although not so complete as the latter. Too much government bureaucracy was built around the

N.R.A., and there was no provision made for the necessary cooperation between all industries and trades. Many American people were opposed to it either on economic or on political grounds, so that they did not cooperate as they should, and evasions of the law were so numerous that even had the Act been constitutional, it could hardly have succeeded. 

Distributism aims at working out an economic system which will improve the present situation.

Distributism is the name of an interesting plan to eliminate some of the worst features of capitalism, which are overurbanization, the unemployment problem, and the lack of property owning by workers, together with the maldistribution of wealth. This theory began with Hilaire Belloc's Servile State in 1912. It was taken up by 

Cecil Chesterton during the 1914-18 war, and was revived by his brother G. K. Chesterton in 1925. The latter founded G. K.'s

Weekly to discuss distributism, but since his death the journal has been called The Weekly Review. The chief organ of distributism in America is Free America. 

The distributists wish each family and each nation to become as self-sufficient as possible. Because they consider that capitalism will soon completely fail, they urge as many as possible to settle on the land. Although they do not necessarily wish to abolish largescale production, they look for an extension of small factories to be either partially owned by the employees or organized as producers' cooperatives, which we shall discuss in the next paragraph. This change is to be made possible by taxing large corporations. A much greater percentage of the population is to work on small plots of land purchased with the savings which the payment of a living wage to present workers would enable them to lay aside for the purpose, and assisted by cooperative marketing and banking schemes. Wherever possible, small family stores and workshops are to provide for local needs, the local market being the medium of exchange for the farmers' surplus produce and the

ECONOMIC LIFE 

work of craftsmen. Property ownership would thus be spread over a greater number of persons. Industries which could not be broken up in small regional units, such as railroads, mining, shipping, and so forth, would be nationalized under the distributism plan. 

Nationalization of certain industries is sometimes for the common good, and is not necessarily socialism; for example, at the present time the postal system forms a national industry although the United States is by no means a socialist State. Distributism is quite contrary to socialism, for it advocates a wider distribution of private property ownership, and not an abolition of ownership. 

The picture which it paints, of a rural State, that retains some of the benefits of capitalism ideal, but it is difficult to see how distributism could be put into effective operation in our present system.

Consumers' cooperation was founded by a group of workers in 1844, and of the three types of cooperation, has done most to improve the workers' condition.

A change in our economic system which has occurred within capitalism has been the growth of consumers' cooperation. Consumers' cooperation is an association of consumers who seek to eliminate the middleman and his profits by arranging to supply themselves with goods instead of buying from private retailers. It was started in Great Britain by a group of workers in 1844, and is most successful today in Great Britain and Sweden. 

Consumers' cooperative societies are usually operated in a locality in this way: Anyone who wishes to become a member may do so upon taking up one share in the enterprise at a cost of about $5, payable, if necessary, in installments of approximately 25 cents. 

Further capital investment is permitted, if desired, up to a maximum usually fixed at $1,000 to prevent the danger of possible future capitalistic control. Interest is paid on capital shares at the same rate as that on deposits in savings accounts at the local banks, but the organization is strictly democratic, for each member has but one vote, regardless of his capital investment. The organization has

conveniently located stores run by hired employees, under the direction of managers who are appointed by vote. Members buy goods at these stores at current market prices. Profits are computed quarterly, after a deduction of all expenses, such as salaries to managers and employees, rental charges, interest on capital shares, and cost of goods sold. A percentage of these profits is placed on reserve for depreciation and unforeseen contingencies, or for the development of the business, and the balance is divided among the members, not in proportion to capital holdings, but in proportion to the amount of goods purchased during the quarter. The money which members receive back in this manner is called "savings returns" or "dividends," and usually amounts to about 10 per cent of the money spent in purchases during the quarter. 

It has been objected that consumers' cooperation eliminates the small tradesman. This is true, to some extent, in poorer districts, although even here many of the poor cannot take advantage of cooperation because they rely on their credit at the local stores, and all the business of the cooperative organization is on a cash basis. In wealthier districts, however, people usually prefer the greater choice of goods and the greater attention given them in privately owned concerns. In any event, the chain store has taken the place of many small tradesmen, and the cooperative would be a rival to these large-scale capitalistic organizations rather than to the remaining small storekeepers.

Cooperative stores are a great boon to those with small incomes. 

Members reduce their cost of living very considerably, because by cooperating in this manner with other consumers, they can take advantage of large-scale buying and obtain for themselves the usual profits of the middleman. Habits of thrift are also promoted, for not only must members pay for their purchases in cash, but they automatically accumulate savings in the form of "savings returns" on purchases which, being paid to them quarterly, are often invested. 

Cooperative stores can also successfully compete against the quasi monopolies held in some places by the capitalistic chain stores.

ECONOMIC LIFE 

Courtesy CUNA 

The new headquarters of the Credit Union National Association in Madison, Wisconsin. This photograph is made from an architect's drawing which shows the building as it will be when completed. 

Credit cooperation is very widespread in the United States, and renders material assistance to the worker. 

Credit cooperation is that whereby individuals who have small amounts of capital to save, pool their resources and eliminate the profits of the banker or moneylender by lending to members in need of ready funds. This form of cooperative banking business originated in Germany. The first credit union, as these cooperative banks are

often called, in the United States was founded in St. Mary's Catholic parish in Manchester, New Hampshire, in 1909. Credit unions were actively promoted by the late Edward A. Filene of Boston, who in 1921 formed the Credit Union National Extension Bureau for that purpose with $1,000,000. The Social Action Department of the National Catholic Welfare Conference also actively promoted them. A number of states passed laws to permit credit unions to exist within their boundaries, and the Federal Credit Union Act was passed in 1934. Filene's Bureau was then discontinued, but was succeeded by the Credit Union National Association, with headquarters at Madison, Wisconsin, to federate state-wide credit union leagues. Almost every state of the union now has credit unions and a credit union league.

Credit unions restrict their membership to persons of good character who have a community of interests. For example, a credit union may be formed by members of a parish or school, or by employees of a factory, office, or store. Usually every member of the credit union is encouraged to save a certain amount regularly, even though the sum involved may be very small. This method encourages thrift and provides an easy, safe, and convenient system of saving small sums of money. A dividend is paid regularly on these savings (which are usually considered as capital invested in the credit bank) at a rate depending on the income of the credit union. 

To provide the money necessary to allot this dividend, the credit union lends out the savings to members at a rate of interest not usually exceeding one per cent per month on the unpaid balance of the loan. This money provides a reserve to cover bad debts, and to meet necessary expenses, but any surplus profits that may be made are divided among members either in proportion to their borrowings or, as is more usual, in proportion to their savings. Bad debts are very rare, however: first, because loans are made only to members, and no member is admitted who is not of good character; second, collateral is required wherever possible, although loans are also made on character and needs; third, loans are made

ECONOMIC LIFE 

only for productive or provident purposes (a productive purpose, for example, would be to enable a man to buy a tool which will help him make larger profits in his business or to provide a washing machine for a poor mother who could then earn a living by doing laundry work, or to purchase an outfit of clothing for a girl or boy just leaving school and needing to present a neater appearance in order to obtain employment; provident purpose is conceived as something which raises the borrower's living standard without involving a burdensome debt); fourth, loans can be insured at very low cost with the Credit Union National Association, so that if the borrower dies before he has repaid a loan made to him, the loan is automatically paid, thus relieving his survivers of the debt, and the bank of the risk of loss.

As just stated, loans are made only to members, but any member, no matter how small his capital investment, may obtain a necessary loan. This enables a person who lacks the financial standing necessary to secure a bank loan, to obtain credit which he needs; it makes it possible to secure loans which might be too small to be handled by the local bank; it prevents a man in need of money from entering into the clutches of the loan shark or moneylender, which is too often the fate of the poor; and it maintains the independence and self-respect of members who, without the credit facilities thus provided, might be forced to resort to charity. 

Producers' cooperation is another means of changing the capitalistic system while retaining most of the features of modern life and also man's natural rights.

Producers' cooperation is the term given to undertakings where the workers are at the same time either complete or controlling owners of a productive enterprise. Producers' cooperation is of two kinds. Either it is a voluntary association of industrial workers who seek to eliminate the employer and to provide for themselves capital, labor, and management for their enterprise; or it is a voluntary association of small employers and agriculturists who, by pooling

Courtesy Land O'Lakes Creamery 

The Land O'Lakes creamery, one of the most successful producers' cooperatives in the country. their resources, can buy and sell more advantageously and secure credit which might otherwise be difficult or impossible for them to command.

In the first type of producers' cooperation, the cooperation of workers who themselves own a business, the workers usually appoint a committee of management. For remuneration they either depend solely upon a division of profits, or each worker is paid an agreed wage, and then receives also his share of any surplus. 

There are several important disadvantages in producers' cooperation on account of which the plan probably never will become very widespread nor benefit many workers. These are: 

1. Workers cannot normally provide the large outlay of capital necessary in modern large-scale production in industry. 

2. They cannot afford to risk the loss of their capital during depression times, whereas a similar loss in a corporation would be borne by the many stockholders. 

3. Workers usually dislike taking necessary orders from foremen and others in command, and frequently they cannot find men in their ranks to fill these offices, and are forced to hire experts to organize, buy, and market, and then they often resent the payment

ECONOMIC LIFE 

of higher wages to those who fill these offices. Disagreements of this nature can only lead to eventual failure. 

4. In times of prosperity more capital is needed, yet if more workers are taken into partnership, profits have to be divided among a greater number, which may mean very little money for each individual partner if a depression follows. The tendency, therefore, is to hire workmen under ordinary conditions of wage payment during prosperous periods, and under these circumstances true cooperation in the technical sense is no longer present, and the enterprise becomes a form of capitalistic partnership. 

The second type of producers' cooperation has been the more successful. Small employers in industry and agriculture can secure the advantages of large-scale production and eliminate many of the social disadvantages of modern capitalistic organization. This type of cooperation originated in France and Germany in the early part of the past century. It is especially beneficial to farmers. For example, it enables the small farmer to buy seed and fertilizers in wholesale quantities, and to share with other members the machinery and other equipment needed in modern farming business, such as tractors, modern dairy equipment, motor trucks for taking produce to the market, grain elevators, and other capital goods. In this way the small farmer is able to withstand the competition from large-scale farming methods and to maintain himself prosperously on the land. Producers' cooperation of the agricultural type just described has been particularly successful in Belgium, Denmark, Nova Scotia, and in certain parts of the United States.
