Unit Four  

People need more than the establishment of the fundamental social groups of family, Church, State, and work group in order to live a completely satisfactory human existence. They need an education, and they need recreation, too. Either privately or through the State they ought to organize social life so that everyone has an opportunity to develop gifts and aptitudes and to live happily and healthily. Some people have greater needs and handicaps than others, and if they cannot provide for themselves, society ought to be so organized that they will be adequately taken care of. 245

EDUCATION 

SHIVK 

THE OUR FUTURE OF DEMOCRACY FOREIGN "ISMS HOLLY- 

A sacred trust. An artist's idea of the importance of education.

Chapter Fourteen  The purpose of this chapter is: 

1. To learn the true meaning of the word education, and why we have a right to an education. 

2. To realize the important place of both home and school in providing an education. 

3. To know why the State should supplement private provision for education. 

4. To learn how the school system is organized in the United States, and why Catholics provide their own schools whenever possible. 

Education is the training and knowledge which human beings acquire, by which their capabilities are developed, they are given the heritage of the past, they are enabled to live good social lives, and they learn what to do to attain their true end. 

Pope Pius XI in his important encyclical on the Christian Education of Youth, said that education "consists essentially in preparing man for what he must be and for what he must do here below, in order to attain the sublime end for which he was created." This is an important aim. To attain it education gives us knowledge and teaches us conduct. The word education comes from the Latin educere, to bring out or lead out. In the strict sense of the word it means the unfolding or development of our capacities. As shown in Chapter Three, by means of education our physical, mental, and moral potentialities are developed, we are enabled to benefit from the rich accumulation of culture which is our social heritage from the past, and our thoughts and actions become similar to those of 

¹Pope Pius XI, On the Christian Education of Youth, pp. 4-5 (Five Great 

Encyclicals, p. 39). 247

A scene in a modern American classroom. the group to which we belong. It is essential for sound social living. 

However, since the basic reason for living is to know and serve God, education is useful only if it helps man to attain this end. 

Education, unlike animal training, does not come to us solely by external means. To be effective, conscious education must be selfeducation; we must wish to receive the knowledge or the training. 

Certainly for all our higher thought processes, we must use both our intellect and our will. By the use of our higher faculties of the mind we can reason logically, think abstract thoughts, judge facts and come to logical conclusions, and add to the sum total of knowledge already acquired by human beings. With our will we can make decisions, and even decide to act contrary to the desires of our lower nature because of the principles presented to us by our intelligence.

Even our mind and will need training, however, for the more frequently our judgment is exercised and we are asked to make decisions, and the more frequently our will is called into operation, the better will they function. It is for this latter reason that what is often called the "older type" of education is still extremely valuable. 

Sitting quietly in a classroom, listening to others, having to give reasonable answers to questions, is valuable training in will power, in the concentration of the higher faculties, and in the use of the judgment. Not only does an education in the classics usually give us an appreciation of the finer things of life and make us more highly cultured, but it gives us, too, a definite training in logic and precision of thought which are of great value in later life. 

On the other hand, as Pope Pius XI pointed out in the encyclical mentioned above, when concerned with education we must not place "too great insistence on the etymological meaning of the word," and claim "to draw education out of human nature itself and evolve it by its own unaided powers." We need grace, and education both religious and secular, to develop to our fullest capacities and to fulfill adequately the true end for which we were created. 

Man has such a fundamental need of education that he has a right to as complete an education as possible. 

Since an education is necessary for the development of our capacities, we have by nature a right to an adequate education, physical, mental, cultural, technical, moral, and religious. We need to have the possibility of our whole self developed, with all its gifts and aptitudes.

First, we have a right to have our physical health and strength developed in the best possible manner. 

Second, our intelligence should be developed so that we can understand and grasp the knowledge presented to us by environment of all types. 

'Pope Pius XI, On the Christian Education of Youth, p. 4 (Five Great 

Encyclicals, p. 38).

Third, we have a right to receive the full heritage of good habits, customs, and ways of doing things, and the technical, literary, and aesthetic heritage of our group. By means of education we can receive the whole social traditions of the past and make them ours, and we can be helped to develop this social heritage as a living tradition, real for the needs of the times, not merely as meaningless, lifeless habits gained by imitation and instinct, as animals carry on the ways of living of their kind.

Fourth, we have a right to an education which will enable us to live socially in conformity with the ways of our group, not only in the home and other groups with which we come into immediate contact, but through governmental and other agencies in the wider social life involved by international relations. Preparation for this social life includes training in good manners according to the habits of our group.

Fifth, unless we are otherwise provided for, we need a vocational training, a technical or theoretical education which will enable us to earn a living according to the occupational organization of our times. Economic life is quite complicated under our western capitalistic system. Theoretically we have greater opportunities to develop our talents and to live fuller lives, but to make this possibility an actuality, we need vocational training, especially since the decline of the apprenticeship system, and we also need vocational guidance. 

Finally, and most important of all, we need religious knowledge and training. Our character needs to be trained and our moral potentialities developed. We need to be trained in right principles, in forming correct judgments, in seeing truth and acknowledging it. 

This need includes a knowledge of God the Creator, and our duties toward Him, and toward our neighbor in social life. 

This right to a complete education does not justify attempts such as are being made in some countries to force all the people of a State into the same mold. We all have different gifts, aptitudes, desires. Our right is more adequately provided for if we are given an opportunity to acquire basic principles, knowledge, and skills,

A school workshop. These boys are being trained in auto mechanics. and then to develop the knowledge and training by our own adaptation, using our mind and will, so that our education becomes truly personal.

The home is the first and foremost center of education, but parents usually need the help of outside agencies, especially the help of the school.

Education begins in the home, and this home education is very important. For, in the first place, the early training which the child receives often sets the whole later trend of the child's character and interests. Children who have adequate attention paid to them from birth, who are played with, helped in their attempts at imitation, and adequately trained, are usually more intelligent than those who are left too much alone, or are insufficiently trained for their inherited endowments to be developed to the full. Even seeming mental defi-

3 ciency may result from the neglect. In the second place, all during childhood and adolescence, the child is usually under the environmental influence of the home for a greater number of hours than with any other social group. 

Parents cannot, however, provide for the whole of a child's education without the help of outside agencies. Unless the family be large, the child needs playmates from outside the home for adequate social development. Much education comes from play groups. Play companions help to develop one another's characters by showing disapproval of selfish or other undesirable conduct. Often play companions teach each other many skills, such as how to skate, fish, and ride a bicycle. However, imitation of surroundings and the limited instruction usually provided by playmates and the home do not provide the child with a complete education. The wider social relations of today necessitate much instruction by experts. Such facilities as newspapers, magazines and books, libraries, museums, art galleries, and the like, are insufficient for a complete instruction without expert guidance. Few people can afford an adequate number of private tutors, nor would this be usually socially desirable. Specialized education is, therefore, usually acquired in the public or the private school.

The school group is next in importance to the five natural societies. It is a primary, face-to-face group, but is subsidiary both to the family, to the Church, and to the State. 

The group which provides for the more complete education of man than is possible by training within the family and by mere social contact, is called the school group or educational society. This group consists of persons especially trained to perform the important function of education, and a group of pupils who are entrusted to these teachers by their parents. Since we have a fundamental need of education, and are under the influence of the school from early years to maturity, the groups which supply this want are primary, face- 'Cf. Shields, Rev. T. E., The Making and the Unmaking of a Dullard.

to-face groups, ranking next in importance to the natural societies. 

Education, whether in private or in public schools, does not make up for home training, but is complementary to it. Children belong first and foremost to their parents. The school is merely an aid to the family, to supplement and not to supersede it. Despite the importance of the school group, therefore, teachers possess only so much authority and jurisdiction over their charges as the parents delegate to them. For their own protection, however, schools may bargain with parents and impose conditions on which pupils will or will not be accepted. But even where free public education is concerned, the parents have a natural right to demand that the education given to their children shall include at least minimum requirements, including training in morals and instruction in whatever religion they designate. Only where parents are morally or mentally incapable of educating their children, and no other persons provide what is lacking, must the State supply for their incapacity by taking complete charge of their nonreligious education. In every case, of course, the rights of the Church in the religious education of the child must be respected. The Church has rights over the Catholic child, and a duty to establish Catholic schools for two reasons: 

First, Christ conferred on the Church authority to teach and govern all the faithful when He gave the divine command: "Going, therefore, teach ye all nations. . . teaching them to observe all things whatsoever I have commanded you." Second, as mentioned before, the child has a natural right to a complete education, and this includes a right to an education which not only insures that he is not learning wrong principles and habits, but which provides him with positive moral and religious instruction and environmental influence. The Catholic child brought up in non-Catholic environment during his school hours forms non-Catholic friendships and imbibes non-Catholic ideas, and hence can rarely grow up with a true Catholic spirit and a complete knowledge of his religion and of how to 'Matt. 28:19, 20.

live it in a truly Catholic manner. The right of the Catholic child to be sent to a Catholic school when one is available ought therefore, to be observed by his parents and by the State. 

It is the right and the duty of the State to promote education, and if the State is to prosper it is essential that it have a good educational system.

The State, as has been mentioned, exists for the common good of its citizens, and has the duty to protect its members and to supply whatever they cannot provide sufficiently by their own unaided effort. The State, therefore, must promote good educational opportunities for all its citizens: first, because the citizen himself needs an education and has a natural right to it; second, because prosperity, security, and economic and cultural advancement all rest on the education which young citizens receive, and no State can prosper unless its citizens have been taught to be law abiding, hard working, and frugal, and have been given opportunities for the acquisition of technical and cultural knowledge. Hence, it is the right and the duty of the State to insure to every citizen an opportunity to acquire at least a minimum education suited to his position in life, and to set down certain minimum requirements which the school must provide. 

If parents fail to provide their children with adequate education, the State must insist that these children receive public instruction. 

It is especially important to the State that citizens be educated, if its government is a democratic one. In a democracy every adult man and woman has a vote, and people must have knowledge if they are to vote intelligently and express their opinion on public affairs. 

A well-organized State sees to it that a complete education, as outlined in a previous paragraph, is available to every citizen, either through the home or other private agencies, or where these fail, through the public educational system. 

Education, whether in private or in public schools, does not make up for home training, but complements it. Children belong first and foremost to their parents. Parents should do their best to give their children a complete education, and only when this is impossible

ERROR FULLNESS OF LIFE CATHOLIC EDUCATION IRRELIGION RADICALISM 

Courtesy Catholic News, N. Y.

A complete education necessarily includes religion. (as it usually is today) and when no other means are provided is the State bound to supply the need. The State's duty is not to dominate education, but to supply deficiencies. 

With the exception of the important omission of religious training in many of the public schools, the educational facilities provided within the United States are very complete. 

Many of our public schools omit the most important function of education to help the child to know God and how to serve Him. 

This is a fact which Pope Pius XII deplored, when he wrote in his

The Library of Congress, one of the finest in the world. special encyclical letter to the United States: "We raise our voice in strong, albeit paternal, complaint that in so many schools of your land Christ is despised or ignored, the explanation of the universe and mankind is forced within the narrow limits of materialism or of rationalism, and new educational systems are sought after, which cannot but produce a sorrowful harvest in the intellectual and moral life of the nation."

Apart from this important omission of religious training, however, the educational facilities, both public and private, in the United States are highly developed and usually complete. There are a very large number of elementary schools, high schools, colleges, and universities of all types, often expensively equipped. There are also 'Pope Pius XII, To the Church in the United States, pp. 10-11.

A stack room in the Library of Congress. 

many evening schools, and correspondence courses conducted. are also

The United States is particularly well furnished with libraries. 

The Library of Congress is one of the finest in the world, and most of the larger cities have very completely equipped library facilities. 

Most States also have a State library. Many smaller cities and towns have moderately well-equipped facilities, and private or public traveling libraries serve many rural districts. The libraries of many "Note, however, that the 1930 Census showed that 4.3 per cent of the population of school age is illiterate.

small townships have been furnished through the philanthropic gifts of the Carnegie Foundation.

Many cities have public or private museums and art galleries, or both; they have theater, opera, concert, and lecture series, Little 

Theater groups, and other cultural facilities which provide recreation also, and will be discussed in the next chapter. The various radio stations of the country provide a fairly large proportion of intellectual and cultural programs, there are numerous motion-picture theaters, and there is a wide variety of books, magazines, and newspapers published. Of course, some of this reading matter, and certain radio and cinema programs, which are designed to provide recreation and direct or indirect education as well, educate in an undesirable manner, and citizens of higher principles hope to abolish their bad features. Nevertheless, very real and worth-while education has been acquired by people through their media. Other education in the 

United States, formal and informal, is provided by many agencies outside the home and school-by clubs of various kinds, civic, parochial, and otherwise, Boy and Girl Scouts, the C.Y.O., 4-H clubs, parent teachers' associations, vocational guidance centers, and the like.

The organization of public schools in the United States. 

In the provision of public education in the United States, centralization is considered un-American. Our principle of public administration is that the individual States should do as much as possible for themselves, leaving to the Federal Government only such services as they cannot adequately manage for themselves or are concerned with interstate affairs.

Each State, therefore, has a board of education, which supervises the public education provided by local authorities, and establishes educational requirements and the minimum school leaving age. 

Funds for this service are secured by State and local taxation, but considerable help is also given out of federal taxation funds. 

Local authorities, city or county, provide the bulk of the schools

Courtesy Edwards Motors Co. 

School bus service is maintained by many communities. Children living some distance are taken to school and brought home again by bus. In some communities the bus serves pupils of parochial as well as those of public schools. of the United States, financed by taxation. Each local community erects its school buildings, provides equipment for them, appoints teachers, and decides upon local educational needs. The administrative work is usually done by a group of citizens called the school board or the board of education, elected or appointed by local government officials.

Many local authorities provide special education for such handicapped children as the blind, deaf, crippled, mentally deficient, and others, for if these children are given the proper training and vocational guidance they are usually able to overcome their handicaps in later life and earn their living in the world. They also provide continuation schools for adolescent and adult education, and special vocational training facilities. With the raising of the school-leaving age in most States, many local authorities have likewise added at least a junior college to their educational system. Often this addition has led to a reorganization of the school system, so that students do not spend eight years in the grade schools as before, but instead, divide up their school years as follows: six years in the grade school,

four years in high school, and possibly two years in the junior college, leaving but two years to complete a regular college course; or six years in the grade school, three years in the junior high school, and three years in the high school with instruction in some college subjects. A health service, textbooks, free meals, and school bus service are also often provided by the local authorities. In some districts small rural communities have joined together to provide adequately equipped consolidated schools, to which children over a comparatively wide area are transported by school buses. 

The Federal Government's aid to the educational system of the United States is limited, but very real. The United States Office of 

Education within the Federal Security Agency gathers information about the schools and colleges of the country, gives information on educational matters at home and abroad, and advises state, county, and city schools about their school administration. It maintains the 

Library of Congress, provides the educational system of the District of Columbia, and organizes and controls federal participation in vocational rehabilitation, and other educational services. Of recent years the government has subsidized workshops and other educational needs to train students in work which would be needed to defend our country in time of war. 

Emergency federal aid to education during the depression of the 1930's has included the NYA which gives financial aid to students and subsidizes their part-time work, and the CCC whose work of character building and conservation is considered more important than the construction enterprises undertaken. 

Catholics consider the maintenance of Catholic schools to be a necessity and maintain that every Catholic child has a right to a Catholic education where this is obtainable, with a corresponding duty on the part of the parents to send their children to Catholic schools whenever this is possible. 

We said earlier in the chapter that every child has a right to a complete education, and that a complete education necessarily includes religion. The Church is, of course, one of the most important

The library of Trinity Preparatory school, Ilchester, Maryland. This is typical of the splendid facilities to be found in many Catholic educational institutions. educational agencies outside the home. Through its teachings man can come to a knowledge of the truth, and can learn right principles and the means available to lead a moral life and attain his final end. The Church, however, is powerless to give a complete religious education unless it maintains schools where all subjects are taught. 

The reason is clear. Religion cannot be compartmentalized: all man's activities should be conducted in accordance with reason and with his final end. Pope Pius XI made the need of education in 

Catholic schools very clear in his encyclical on education. He says: "The frequenting of non-Catholic schools, whether neutral or mixed, those namely which are open to Catholics and non-Catholic alike, is forbidden for Catholic children, and can be at most tolerated, on the approval of the Ordinary alone, under determined cir-

cumstances of place and time, and with special precautions." He continues: "Neither can Catholics admit that other type of school (least of all the so-called State school obligatory on all) in which the students are provided with separate religious instruction, but receive other lessons in common with non-Catholic pupils from non- Catholic teachers." He makes his reason for these statements very clear either the child will receive no religious education at all, or religion will be taught as a "subject" distinct from the rest of the curriculum. As he says: "When literary, social, domestic, and religious education do not go hand in hand, man is unhappy and helpless. From this it follows that the so-called 'neutral' or 'lay' school, from which religion is excluded is contrary to the fundamental principles of education. Such a school moreover cannot exist in practice; it is bound to become irreligious." 198 The public school system in the United States does not usually permit of religious instruction of any kind, and hence, as Pope Pius pointed out about education in general, is more on the side of irreligion than religion. Catholics in the United States, therefore, have provided an admirable private system of education of their own. 

Catholic schools are of two kinds: Private institutions in which students pay fees which usually cover the cost of their tuition; parochial or city Catholic schools, which are mainly supported by the parish, although students usually pay a small fee in part payment of tuition and textbooks, and the teachers, both members of religious communities and lay people, work for a minimum salary. Some parish schools accept children from needy families without payment, but in poorer districts large numbers who cannot afford the small cost of Catholic schooling have to be sent to the local public schools. Where 

Catholic schools are available, it is the grave duty of parents to make use of them, and as the Pope says, they cannot send their children to non-Catholic schools without the permission of their

In many universities these precautions have been taken by the bishops themselves, who have provided chaplains for Catholic students. It will be of interest to Catholics to know that in 1938 there were 7916 Catholic elementary schools in the United States, with a total of 2,086,071 students; 2164 Catholic high schools, private and parochial, with a total of 345,218 students; and 229 Catholic normal schools, colleges, and universities with a total of 153,441 students. There were also 175 major and preparatory seminaries with 17,728 students.

In some districts, Catholic children receive part of their share of 'The latest year on which information is available. National Summary: Catholic Colleges and Schools in the United States, National Catholic Welfare Conference, 1940, p. 71.

taxation allotments in the form of free health service, the use of school busses, free meals, and the like. In other districts these services are denied to them, and the provision for Catholic children is not equivalent to that for public school students. In certain parts of the country Catholic parents have sought without success for taxation allotments for their schools. It does not seem unreasonable to expect that where education is paid out of taxation, those Catholic parochial schools which meet the ordinary demands of the district should receive their share of taxation allotted for educational purposes. 

Failing this, it seems that taxpayers should be allowed to decide for which class of school their taxes must be allotted, so that Catholics could designate Catholic schools for this purpose. The beneficiaries of this tax allotment would be the children rather than the school or Church, and hence the State would benefit by its future citizens receiving more adequate educational provisions. On the other hand, Catholics in some districts are opposed to any state or local subsidies, because they fear that too much nonreligious interference will result so that their educational aims will not be adequately carried out. The problem is one which needs much consideration before a satisfactory solution can be arrived at.

Chapter Fifteen RECREATION AND  The purpose of this chapter is: 

1. To realize the human necessity for play and recreation. 

2. To learn something about the different modern forms of recreation and amusement. 

3. To realize the importance of supervised play for the young, and especially to realize the social importance of family recreation. 

4. To discover why modern economic and social organization has created recreation problems and to discuss the best means of improving conditions.

Play and recreation are essential for our well-being. The play group is a primary face-to-face society. 

Play is a natural activity of the young. Children do not need to be taught to play, although they will probably develop to a greater extent if their play is supervised. People of all ages, however, need physical and other types of recreation. 

The word recreation means to re-create, to refresh or revitalize oneself after the fatigue of work. "All work and no play makes Jack a dull boy" — there is a deal of truth in such old saws. Recreation may be of three types: active recreation with others, passive recreation with others, and recreation taken alone. 

Play and certain other types of recreation are usually enjoyed with others, although such physical recreation as hiking may be taken alone. The play group is a primary face-to-face association, which is of immense importance in personal and social development, especially for children. Play and other primary recreation groups teach good social habits, such as respect for the rights of others, the need of cooperation with others, and obedience to authority. Play 268

especially develops our bodies, and our nerve and muscular control. 

It also aids in the development of mental alertness, courage, resourcefulness, self-control in defeat or victory, and other useful mental, moral, and social qualities. In other words, physical recreation helps us to develop in a good fashion, physically, mentally, morally, and socially; it cultivates positive good habits, and promotes the formation of virile character, a spirit of social service, and good citizenship.

Sometimes we may recreate with others, not in a primary group but as part of a secondary group. This is group recreation of the depersonalized type, the passive recreation of the kind provided by many commercial amusements, for example, watching spectator sports, or enjoying a motion picture or theatrical performance as part of the audience.

People now need more time for recreation than they did when economic and social life was more simple. This is true not only because modern life is a strain on the nervous system and many tasks in modern industry are monotonous, but also because this age of specialization often leads to a somewhat one-sided development, so that well-chosen recreation and other leisure-time activities are needed to make up deficiencies in our use of all our gifts and capacities, and in the development of a well-rounded personality. 

Recreation is necessary for relaxation, diversion, and mental stimulation, and can be useful for physical and social development, too, as we have shown. It affords relief from the strain and monotony of daily tasks, creates mirth and happiness, and certain types of recreation can develop body, mind, and character. Amusement is not necessarily recreation, but recreation would not fill its purpose if it were not pleasurable.

Because individuals vary greatly in their tastes, gifts, and needs, recreation takes many forms.

Recreation, of its very nature, must be the expression and satisfaction of our own legitimate desires by the employment of our time

as we think fit. Wholesome recreation and amusement is of many types, both passive and active. Modern inventions have led to a great variety of choice in recreation, and some people like one type, while others have different preferences. Some persons prefer hunting, fishing, or such active recreation as dancing, skating, riding, basketball, and other sports, or such games as billiards and bridge. 

Others prefer passive amusements such as reading, listening to concerts, going to the theater and the motion pictures, visiting art galleries or museums, watching spectator sports. Others prefer working at a hobby, or find sufficient relaxation in conversing with congenial companions. Still others like to travel short or longer distances, to go for picnics, to ride in automobiles.

Reading is one of the finest of the passive types of recreation, for its result is usually to develop our knowledge of actual persons, places, and things or, vicariously, our knowledge of human nature. 

Worth-while reading provides an art and verbal beauty which is one of the highest expressions of man's mind. It is one of the most worth-while amusements particularly for those who are ill, or old, or solitary.

More people read in our modern western world than ever before in history. This is obvious from the number of magazines, books, and newspapers published yearly, and from the circulation of books and periodicals in public libraries. This increase in reading can be traced to a number of facts: (1) educational opportunities are more widespread than ever before, and therefore more people are able to read; (2) people have more time to read; (3) library facilities are more available; (4) commercial enterprise has learned how to make certain types of reading attractive to the masses, even though much of this reading matter is useless, and not a little of it positively harmful, to the reader. 

What do the following statistics suggest about the reading habits of Americans ?¹

Sunday newspapers. 943,155 15,562,327 25,880,325 42,385,807 33,199,248 The radio is another form of passive home amusement, and American people spend many hours listening to the programs provided by the 600 or more broadcasting stations dominated by one or other of the three great broadcasting groups: the National Broadcasting System, the largest of the three, the Columbia Broadcasting Company, next in size, and the Mutual Broadcasting System which is considerably smaller. It has been estimated that 26 million families in the 

United States have one or more radios in their homes, and there are about 5 million radios installed in automobiles. A Federal Communications Commission was created by Act of Congress in 1934, which took over the work of the Federal Radio Commission, assigns wave lengths and licenses broadcasting stations. In the United 

States, advertisers pay for the programs. In this, our system differs from that in Canada and in most of the rest of the world, where owners of radios must pay a small annual fee to cover the cost of programs without the intervention of advertisers. Many fine programs have been sponsored by various advertisers, but obviously if his goods have a very popular appeal, the advertiser tries to give a program which will attract the largest number of listeners. Sometimes, therefore, the programs provided by certain advertisers are of a low type culturally, and educate people in poor taste instead of helping them to cultivate the higher qualities of their human powers. 

Radio programs may be very worth while from the viewpoint of cultural development, providing information on world affairs, music, literature, and also wholesome amusement and recreation, including music for dancing. One of the virtues of the radio as a form of amusement is, too, that it can be switched off and on at will. One of its worst features is that radio owners sometimes tend to waste many hours in idle listening in of little value to themselves even

Courtesy National Broadcasting Co. 

The master control desk at Radio City. This is the "nerve center" of the 

National Broadcasting System's networks. Radio has become, in a few years, a very popular means of home amusement. from the recreational viewpoint; others attempt to listen and do other things at the same time, which usually results in the lessening of a very valuable human power-that of concentration on the task at hand and leads to a habit of general inattention which, if cultivated, may be a serious handicap to a young person in later life. 

Hobbies. A hobby may be a passive amusement, but usually it is a purposeful activity which involves a certain amount of creative effort apart from one's usual occupation, the main motive of undertaking it being recreation and enjoyment rather than financial gain. 

As a diversion from their usual employment, therefore, people may undertake to do, make, collect, or learn something. They may occupy themselves with such pursuits as sketching, gardening, carpentry, photography, the collection of stamps or birds' eggs, and language or nature study. "S. Van Dyne" and G. D. H. Cole, for example, are university professors who write detective novels as a hobby. 

Some hobbies may be undertaken alone, while others, such as dramatics, choral singing, sports, or work in parish clubs and civic groups, are undertaken with a group. Hobbies which can be undertaken alone are, with the reading habit, the recreational activities

which furnish a person with the most lasting enjoyment for life. 

During periods of ill-health and in old age, particularly, those who can amuse themselves by reading and with a hobby, find their greater amount of leisure time a period of worth-while enjoyment, and by their added knowledge the interest of their conversation attract good companions.

Motion pictures provide amusement for the majority of persons. 

The United States has a greater proportion of motion-picture theaters than any other country in the world, partly perhaps because the industry originated and first developed here, and is more highly organized here than elsewhere. There are over 100 million attendances at motion-picture theaters weekly in the United States. 

Some motion pictures have been of a very high type of artistry and dramatic skill; many have provided wholesome amusement and also valuable education; but, unfortunately, many films are quite undesirable. As a result of agitation for a higher level of standards in the films by the Central Conference of American Rabbis, the Federal Council of Churches of America, and by the Legion of Decency and other Catholic Agencies, a Code Production Administration was established in 1934 by the Motion Picture Producers and Distributers of America, Inc.

Films are now graded as to their suitability for children or for adults only. Occasionally some of these latter films are not suitable for anyone of decent morals, and sometimes films may have a bad effect on children in the audience - for example, gangster films may give them the idea that wrongdoing is only wrong if one is caught. The State has a duty to preserve the morals of its citizens from any grossly immoral film or theater productions, just as it has the right to censor immoral books and litera ture. By commission of The Bishops' Committee on Motion Pictures within the N.C.W.C., the office of The National Legion of Decency in New York issues weekly "white lists" which are prepared by

The Motion Picture Bureau of the International Federation of Catholic Alumnae.

Recreation may be provided in several ways, the most important being through the home group. 

The home is an ideal place for recreation, and the community of interests established in a home where members take their recreation together will make the home spirit thrive. Reading and personal hobbies are best carried on in the home. Members of the family can also play games together, have family outings, picnics, and longer vacations; they can listen together to music or the radio, work in the garden, or have some other mutual hobbies. Children whose parents provide them with play facilities at home — table tennis, shuffleboard, and many other games do not demand much space can get needed play and recreation under the supervision of their parents. By playing with their children, parents will learn more about them, be able to correct the character defects they show in play and will also be able to teach them games and skills for future use with outside recreational groups. Families where parents and children share recreational activities in common will hardly become "broken homes". instead, the members will surely be a more closely united social group approaching the ideal of mutual family companionship. 

Supervised play is essential for the young. The school is an important social group which supplements the family in providing face-to-face primary play group facilities. 

Not all recreation can take place within the home, even though such supervised play is the ideal situation proper for the development of younger children. People need the companionship of those of their own age, and often have interests which are not shared with other members of the family, or which cannot be undertaken in the home. Because of lack of home facilities, many sports of importance in the physical development of the child must be enjoyed away from home. The play instinct is strong in man; young people ought certainly to be allowed to express themselves as much as possible in playing their own games and choosing their own leaders. Yet for

גה ގ A home workshop. During the years of the depression many men took up hobbies such as this as a means of recreation during spare time consequent upon unemployment. The hobbies, in many cases, turned out to be not only enjoyable recreation but a source of income as well. young people, the play group is a primary one, and a large part of their day is spent with it, so that its influence on their unformed characters is very great. Therefore, although a more free rein ought to be given to their personalities and individual development when at play than in the classroom or at work, supervision is still highly desirable. Young people need the help, encouragement, and suggestions which can be given them by a trained and tactful supervisor. 

This directed recreation outside the home will not only help them to learn games and skills which will prove useful in their recreational activities when older, but it will also aid their character formation to the greatest possible extent.

Much of this supervised play for the young is provided outside the home by the school, which, equipped usually with an auditorium and gymnasium, trains students in various types of sports, enables them to enjoy occasional dances or other social functions, and provides a means of learning different hobbies and skills, and of de-

veloping good taste in later choice of recreational activity. The ideal as regards plays and sports is, of course, not to develop the gifted and win trophies and outside applause, so much as to develop skills for all, the ability to cooperate with others and to take advantage of the other character and health benefits of such activities. 

The increasing amount of leisure time now available to all citizens brings its problems and responsibilities. 

As we showed in an earlier chapter, when capitalism first developed people worked from an early age, for very long hours, and had little leisure time at their disposal. Today, labor union agitation, better social legislation, and a realization of the greater industrial efficiency of happy, healthy workers, have resulted in few people working more than eight hours daily outside the home, and in most people having an annual vacation. Laborsaving devices in the home, modern housekeeping methods, and smaller families have also given wives and mothers a great deal more leisure time than ever before. 

Then, too, older people usually have more free time than before, because workers often retire from work at an earlier age, and pensions are provided in many states to support aged couples, with federal provision for many workers. Children, also have more leisure time at the present day. Their school day is considerably shortened, and there is usually less homework to do than formerly; besides, children must now stay in school for a longer number of years there was a time when many children worked in industry at the age of 12 or even earlier, whereas now some states have a school leaving age as high as 18 years, and the lowest is 14 years, in some of the southern states. There are also large numbers of unemployed in the periods of depression which are more acute in our modern capitalistic system than in a less highly concentrated economic organization, and the unemployed often find time hanging heavily on their hands.

This larger amount of leisure time at most people's disposal involves problems and responsibilities.

Football is one of the most popular of the spectator sports. This view gives a fine idea of the difference in numbers between those taking part in the game and those watching it.

As intelligent human beings, we need time to develop our capacities and gifts to their fullest extent. As human beings, too, we need time for relaxation from our ordinary work, and for social intercourse. Adequate leisure time gives us an opportunity for better social living, for closer companionship between members of families and friends, for cultivating tastes in literature, music, art, travel, and other human interests formerly left mainly to the "leisured classes." Adequate leisure gives us an opportunity, too, for a happier, more truly human existence. Unfortunately, not everyone has the knowledge, will, or opportunity to use his leisure time to best advantage for himself and society. Young people need to be educated in the right use of leisure. Idleness usually leads to unsocial conduct. Daydreaming becomes creative thought only with a few. A

sound leisure-time program is one which gives a person a wellrounded personal development, physical and mental health, and social as well as cultural and spiritual growth. As intelligent human beings we have a responsibility to develop in this manner, if we can. 

The problem of leisure time today is twofold: First, how are we to provide ourselves with facilities for the adequate use of leisure? 

Second, how are the majority of citizens to be assured an education which will train them in the right, useful use of leisure?

Many private nonprofit-making as well as commercial recreation agencies exist in the United States.

Where school facilities for recreation are inadequate, and certainly for those whose school days are past, other nonfamily agencies are essential to provide a social organization for recreational activities. 

The number of such societies is almost legion. Americans are noted for founding organizations of all types, in greater proportions than people in most other lands seem to consider necessary. President 

Hoover's Research Committee on Social Trends showed that in the United States in 1931 there were 500,000 members of luncheon clubs alone, and that tax receipts on club dues of over $25 annually amounted to as high as $12,521,091.³

There are nonprofit-making clubs and associations for recreation and amusement of all kinds and descriptions, some with very low dues or none at all, others with dues so high that only the wealthy can belong to them. For young people there are, for example, the parish groups we have referred to, Boy and Girl Scouts, Camp Fire Girls, 4-H clubs, and numerous others, whose aim is to form character as well as to provide recreation and skills. Then there are a host of civic clubs, women's clubs, athletic associations, and such clubs as the Knights of Columbus, the Rotary, Elks, Kiwanis, Lions, American Legion, Y.M.C.A., and Y.W.C.A. Some employers provide opportunities to their employees to form their own recreational 

Recent Social Trends, p. 936. 'Ibid., p. 934.

groups. In districts where many people live who are unable to pay club dues from their own personal resources, and which are sometimes far from parks and open spaces, social settlements have been established — sometimes called neighborhood clubs or community centers. These often provide a home, as it were, where the people of a poorly provided neighborhood can get together for a few hours to forget their troubles by some form of recreation or cultural education. Parishes often provide similar facilities. 

The need of recreation and its influence is so great, and so much ill-afforded money is spent on commercial amusement which is often positively harmful, that the Church is filling a real need in providing social and recreational facilities for its members. Leisure time spent under the auspices of the parish cannot but be morally healthful, and the facilities offered have the additional advantage of being near the homes of the people, so that they are not obliged to travel too great a distance or to spend an undue amount of time away from their families. Moreover, these activities help to keep the parish united as a social group, They give pastors and others the opportunity of showing that religion is not something apart from life, and that true piety does not exclude happy and healthful fun. They also give Catholics the opportunity of meeting their fellow Catholics, which will lead to fewer mixed marriages and fewer Catholics coming under the influence of nonreligious people, to the possible loss of their Faith. According to the needs of different parish groups, therefore, parishes might provide plays and concerts, dances, lectures, social functions, clubs for games, and dramatic, social, literary, and other societies. Some parishes might have their own athletic grounds. In these various ways the parish can supplement the family and become the center of the social life of the people. 

Aware of the increasing amount of leisure time at people's disposal, and of their need of recreation and physical sports, many commercial amusements are available in the United States, particularly in cities and towns. The importance of these commercial amusements in the lives of modern American citizens is shown by the following

A map showing per capita expenditures for amusements by state. for recreation needs must include the following:5 one acre of park or playground for every hundred inhabitants; playgrounds and playing fields within easy walking distance for children; provision for swimming, golf, tennis; indoor recreation centers; adequate leadership.

When private facilities for recreation in any community are either inadequate or too costly for the poorer members, then if church groups, schools, or other civic groups do not or cannot supply the deficiency, it is the duty of the local state group to provide. This community duty has been adequately taken care of in many localities, but in others much improvement is still needed, and the total local, state, and national government expenditures on recreation is a very small percentage of what the nation as a whole expends on commercial or nonprofit recreation provided by private agencies. 

Public provision of recreational facilities are particularly nec- 'Cf. Pangburn, W. W., Adventures in Recreation (New York: A. S. Barnes and Co., 1936), pp. 108-112.

MUT FOR MY AMO 

The play group is of great importance in personal and social development. 

Here is a group playing in a public park. essary when private provision fails, for several reasons. First, because of man's need of recreation, especially now that his leisure time has been increased. Second, because much juvenile delinquency and even adult crime has been directly traced to lack of recreational facilities. Third, because in modern city life cities may be centers of culture, and yet many persons living away from home may be utterly without normal social contacts unless attractive and inexpensive clubs and recreational groups are easily available for them.

In country districts the need for community planning for recreation may not be so obvious. Yet time hangs heavily on the hands of many a rural dweller. Often, time might be more profitably employed, and truer relaxation might result if more facilities were offered for recreation, such as an open space for sports, the provision of schoolroom, a stage, or other buildings on at least one night a week for adult recreation, and a local library of some kind. 

Most cities of any size have public parks and playgrounds. 

Larger cities may have, too, zoological and botanical gardens,

aquariums, and the like. The parks are often provided with facilities for tennis, golf, bathing, skating, and similar physical recreation, although sometimes a small fee is charged to users which may make them inaccessible to the very poor. Playgrounds are often provided with a paid recreation superviser, which is an ideal situation for the well-being of the children who use them. The Federal 

Government and most states, as well, maintain open-air resorts for camp sites, picnic grounds, motoring, fishing, hiking, and sightseeing. Many of these parks have been developed around some of the natural scenic spots of the country, historic sites, or about places where some of the wonders of nature may be seen, as in the Petrified 

Forests and the Grand Canyon. The states of New York, Indiana, 

Michigan, and Pennsylvania have especially well-developed state parks. The Federal Government spends about 10 million dollars yearly on the National Park Service section of the Department of the Interior in connection with the 15,500,000 acres which are available as National Parks. Persons who hold commercial concessions in these places are limited in the charges they may ask from clients, so that the advantages are within the financial possibilities of persons of fairly moderate income. 

In many cities where civic planning has been neglected in the past, streets are sometimes roped off to provide a play space for the children free from traffic hazards, and public schools are kept open in the evenings for children's recreation, as well as for adult clubs and games.

It has been computed that a tax of $3 per person per year, would be sufficient to provide completely adequate recreation space in any large city. This would be a small amount to pay for increased community health, happiness, and general development; with decreased social dissatisfaction, delinquency, and crime. Recreational provisions essential for the development of sound social living, with an opportunity for all kinds of true development, are still needed matters for the attention of the public authorities in many parts of our country.

Chapter Sixteen  The purpose of this chapter is: 

1. To understand the problem of poverty and dependency in the 

United States today, to realize the causes of this situation, and, failing the elimination of the causes, to learn what is being done to relieve the needy poor. 

2. To know what is meant by the term social service and to learn the chief social service methods of giving relief. 

3. To understand the reason for community chest drives and social service exchanges.

Poverty and dependency make one of the greatest social problems of our times.

Poverty means the deprivation of something necessary for subsistence and physical energy. The term poverty is often used in a vague and relative sense, for some people consider themselves poor if they are unable to afford an automobile, or the education of a son or daughter at an expensive private school; others, with more cause, regard themselves as poor because they cannot buy the clothes necessary for their station in life, or because they can afford little amusement, and cannot take a yearly vacation. The real poor are those who are unable to provide themselves with sufficient food, clothing, shelter, or other necessaries to maintain full bodily vigor. 

Destitution is a state of poverty in which there is entire deprivation of the means of subsistence. A person without the means to purchase food, or clothing, or shelter, may be termed destitute. Since man has a right to life, it is the duty of society to take care of a destitute person. 288

Dependency is another term used in connection with poverty. 

Here we must distinguish between the general and the specialized meaning of the word. In the general sense it means the state of those who depend upon others for support. For example, unless a child has a private income, he is dependent upon his parents or others for support until he is able to earn a living for himself. Most wives depend upon the income of their hubsands for economic support, although, of course, many of them increase the purchasing power of the income by their household work and management. Old people and those who are ill or physically handicapped frequently also depend upon others for a livelihood. As a social problem, however, dependency describes the state of a dependent person who is unprovided for by his family and friends. A dependent, as we shall use the term in this book, is a person who receives private or public assistance from other people who are not members of his family or his friends, because these persons cannot or will not take care of him. Under this category of dependency comes the state of the unprovided aged, orphans, widows, handicapped, defective, ill, and those who, though able bodied, are for some reason unable or unwilling to earn a livelihood for themselves and their families. 

Poverty in itself is not necessarily evil, for wealth brings with it many temptations and the means of satisfying unworthy desires which the poor avoid because of their lack of material means. On the other hand, the effects of poverty on society as a whole are very bad indeed. Poverty, and especially dependency, invariably undermine physical and mental ability. The poor can rarely develop their gifts and talents fully, and therefore society loses their potential contributions. Most poor people are badly housed in overcrowded rooms, and are undernourished, two conditions leading to ill-health and disease which are costly to the community in hospital and clinic services, in lessened productive efficiency, and in the possible spreading of disease to others. Frequently poverty leads to such moral results as lack of self-assurance and initiative, discouragement and depression, which again make for lessened potential efficiency.

Courtesy U. S. Housing Authority 

The home of a poor family. It is evident from the photograph that these people do make an attempt to keep their quarters clean and in order. 

Worst of all, bad housing areas often give the poor undesirable associations, and the contrast between the way of life of the rich and their own leads some people to habits of vice and crime. 

Primitive people, who lead a very simple existence, usually avoid any condition of dependency in the specialized sense used in this book, by arranging that the tribe takes care of all dependent people. 

We, who claim to be more civilized, have usually become less thoughtful of others. Among people in cities, relationships with others are very impersonal, and one half does not even know "how the other half lives." In a well-organized community there ought to be very few destitute and dependent persons, yet obviously there is much dependency in the United States, when we remember the statistics given in Chapter Twelve, which showed that more than one third of our families receive an annual income of $780 or less. Why does this condition exist?

The causes of poverty and dependency are many: objective and subjective.

The causes of poverty and dependency are so many that it is difficult to discuss them adequately. Invariably a person is not poor for one single cause alone, but as a result of complex personal and social conditions.

Some theorists say that poverty is due chiefly to overpopulation, and that if families were universally small there would be less poverty. This opinion seems to be false. The world is rich enough in natural resources to support all the people living in it, and the larger the family the more demand it creates for goods and hence the more work it provides for others. 

The chief objective causes of poverty today are twofold. First, there is the general lack of regard for moral principles in business organization and business dealings, so that business is frequently conducted simply for the greatest amount of profit, or the highest pay, with the least expenditure of energy, regardless of human rights. As was discussed in Chapter Ten, those who depend for their livelihood upon wages are paid according to output, not according to needs and many wage payments are less than a family living wage. 

Second, and chiefly as a result of the first cause, there is a lack of planned organization for economic life, so that individual gifts and aptitudes, as well as the resources of nature, are not used to their fullest extent, and often people willing to work are unable to find suitable employment. The economic situation of the rural tenant farmers and share-croppers in the rural South is especially distressing, and it is a situation which can only be completely remedied by the establishment of a better agricultural organization. Sometimes a faulty educational system fails to provide suitable vocational training for the ordinary citizen, as well as for those who because of physical or mental handicaps need special equipment for earning their living. Bad housing conditions and insufficient provision for health needs are also the occasion of poverty and dependency.

With some people poverty is brought about by subjective causes. 

There are people who are poor, not because the social organization in which they live is inadequate, or because their rights are disregarded, but because of subjective causes, voluntary or involuntary. 

They may spend money unwisely or unnecessarily, to "keep up with the Jones's," or for some other reason, without sufficient regard for future needs. Some persons are lazy, incompetent, lacking in initiative, quarrelsome, or have other character or personal defects which keep them frequently or chronically unemployed, or employed only at a low wage. On the other hand, some subjective causes of poverty are involuntary, and in no way to be criticized in the poor who suffer from them. Such, for example, is the inability to work because of a personal physical handicap because of the result of malnutrition. Some families are impoverished because of an extra burden of medical or hospital expenses they have had to bear. As we have already noted, poverty nearly always results in lessening the efficiency of the poor.

The relief of the dependent poor is not only necessary from the social viewpoint, but it is a precept of Christianity. 

Because of the social solidarity of the human race, citizens have a positive obligation to see that social and economic life is so organized that justice is secured by all. Yet although the removal of the causes of poverty rather than the relief of the dependent poor, is of fundamental importance, a consideration of the social effects of poverty will lead to the obvious conclusion that existing poverty and need must be relieved where they occur. Besides, over and above the social reason for the relief of the poor and needy, there is the teaching of Christianity on the subject. Not only do we owe justice to our fellow men, but we Christians have an obligation in charity. 

Let us briefly consider what the Scriptures teach on the subject. 

Christ Himself said: "The poor you have always with you." And we have His positive command: "Thou shalt love thy neighbor as 'Matt. 26:11; Mark 14:7; John 12:8.

thyself," coupled with the terrible warning: "Depart from Me, you cursed, into everlasting fire which was prepared for the devil and his angels. For I was hungry, and you gave Me not to eat: I was thirsty, and you gave Me not to drink. I was a stranger, and you took Me not in; naked, and you covered Me not; sick and in prison and you did not visit Me. . . . Amen I say to you, as long as you did it not to one of these least, neither did you do it to Me."³ 

Again, we read: "Faith without works is dead"; that we must "do the truth in charity";5 and that "he that hath the substance of this world, and shall see his brother in need, and shall shut up his bowels from him: how doth the charity of God abide in him?”

Christians have always tried to carry out this essentially Christian concept of charity toward others, and the history of the work of the Church for the relief of those in want is very impressive. It was early communities of monks and nuns who first developed hospitals and the care of the poor and the ill in their monasteries and convents. 

For long centuries it was the Christians, working privately or attached to some religious community, who took care of the destitute, visited the poor and gave them material and spiritual consolation, looked after orphans, needy widows, and the aged, and cared for the ill and the dying.

Even today the help given by specifically Christian organizations to the dependent poor is very great. Catholics especially have numerous organizations through which the work of Christian charity is carried out. The best known of these organizations, and the largest, is the Society of St. Vincent de Paul. This Society was founded in 

Paris in 1833 by a young French layman, Frédéric Ozanam, spread rapidly, and today is found in almost every country of the world. 

The members of the Society obtain funds from private donations, and distribute them to the needy in their own homes. By visiting the
