An artist's conception of St. Vincent de Paul visitors with one of their poor families. The Society of St. Vincent de Paul is one of the most active of Catholic charity organizations. poor, members consider that they are receiving a favor from the poor rather than conferring a favor, for they secure the friendship of those whom they visit, and also gain the indulgence offered by the Church to those who do the "works of mercy" in this manner. In some places the Society maintains homes and institutions for those who cannot be cared for adequately in their own homes. 

It is impossible to find space here to outline the work of the

numerous other Catholic societies of the same type. The National 

Conference of Catholic Charities, organized in 1910, embraces practically all Catholic societies in the United States which do social work. It provides a clearinghouse for exchange of views between the foremost Catholic social workers of the country and the superiors of all the important Catholic institutions. It publishes the Catholic 

Charities Review ten times a year, as its official organ, and holds an annual convention.

The application of scientific methods of social service is the best way of relieving the poor and the needy at the present time. 

It is generally recognized today that the poor are not always most effectively helped by haphazard giving of private alms. Certainly it would be regrettable if all private initiative in this respect were to be abolished, for such a situation would destroy the possibility of mutual understanding and even friendship between the almsgiver and the recipient. Nevertheless, scientific social work is necessary for a well-organized community program for the relief of the dependent poor, the ill, the handicapped, and the defective. We shall deal in this chapter only with the general aspect of the treatment of poverty.

Social service is the practical application of the principles of sociology and psychology to the relief of the poor and to the adequate provision for the ill, the handicapped, the defective, and the delinquent. Social case work is social service carried out in its relation to the individual in need. A social worker is one whose main occupation is to take care of individual needy persons or families in a modern scientific manner.

The aim of social service or social work is to avoid the duplication of services and waste, and also to give the needy person all necessary help in the most scientific manner. 

The first step in social case work is the discovery of each person or family in need of help. Then, second, after the necessary temporary treatment, help, or "relief" has been given, a trained social

worker interviews the individual or family to learn the causes of the need. Third, a scientific diagnosis of the need is made, taking into consideration all the causes of it which have been discovered. Fourth, the "case" is treated so that all possible needs are scientifically cared for and all causes are eliminated in so far as this is possible. 

Sound social work avoids the pauperization of the dependent poor. 

This is a term which expresses the state of those who are so accustomed to receiving help from others that they lose all initiative, self-reliance, and self-respect, become unwilling to help themselves, and expect charitable assistance as their "right."

Poverty is relieved by both outdoor and indoor relief, according to circumstances. Indoor relief is often provided on the cottage plan.

The terms outdoor and indoor relief are technical expressions to denote two methods of relieving the poor and needy. 

Outdoor relief means the care of the dependent poor, the ill, and the handicapped in their own homes or in foster homes. Indoor relief is the care of those in need in institutions. As a general rule it may be said that outdoor relief is preferable, for people are usually happier in their family surroundings, and happiness is in itself a powerful aid toward general well-being. Social workers never break up a home unless there seems to be a positive need for this; so general family relief of poverty is always given as outdoor relief. Children are always kept with their family or relatives whenever possible, and when this is not possible a foster home is often provided for them, so that they need not be sent to an institution. 

The aged, the handicapped, and defectives are also looked after in their own homes when this is possible, and are usually sent to foster homes rather than to institutions when they have no suitable home of their own.

A foster home is a home provided by a family which takes care of people sent to them by social service agencies, in return for a specified payment. Social workers call at the foster home regularly, to see that the persons sent there are being properly treated.

Acme This photograph was taken in an "old age colony." Elderly couples here live in individual cottages while still enjoying the benefits of community life. 

Where care in an institution seems to be better than care with the family or in a foster home, many institutions consider the cottage system to be better than housing large numbers of children or adults together. Under the cottage system persons in the institution live in groups of thirty or fewer, each group in an individual house. 

Each house is in charge of a "housemother" or "housefather," or both, according to needs, who supervise the whole establishment. 

The houses are placed in colonies, close enough together for centralized management, but the life in each cottage is as near a normal family life as possible. Each member is encouraged to take a personal interest in his or her particular cottage, and at the same time individuality is allowed full play. A spirit of loyalty to the group is fostered, as also mutual kindness and self-helpfulness. Necessary work in the cottage is performed by the members according to their individual capacities. Some do housework, others gardening; those

who are young and need to learn a trade may work in the colony workshops. The cottage system has been found most successful for orphans, the aged, and for defectives who are in need of the special medical care which only an institution could provide. It has also proved an effective way of training certain types of delinquents. 

Well-organized charitable agencies usually avoid duplication of services by means of a Social Service Exchange. 

To prevent unnecessary duplication of work and overlapping of social service facilities, both private and public, in any given area, most communities have established a Social Service Exchange · sometimes called a Confidential Exchange or a Central Index. All the charitable agencies of the city or district which wish to take advantage of the Social Service Exchange contribute the amount of money needed to finance it. The Exchange is an office to which every subscribing agency sends brief particulars of each individual or family to which charitable relief is given. The records are confidential, and are open only to authorized representatives of the members of the Exchange, so as to protect the dependent poor from the curiosity of those not immediately concerned with giving them relief. When a new case comes to the notice of an agency, it immediately inquires at the Exchange, to learn if the person or family to be assisted is known to other organizations. Then, if a record of the case is found, the inquirer secures all available information from the agencies noted on the record. This makes another investigation unnecessary, thus saving time and expense; it also prevents persons cheating an agency and receiving the same type of help from two separate organizations. Through the Exchange, an agency can also sometimes collaborate with other organizations in rendering assistance in a particular case. For instance, a city relief agency might supply food and pay the rent to a needy person, the Society of St. Vincent de Paul might give clothing, and arrangements might be made with a hospital to provide necessary free medical attention. 

Members of a local Social Service Exchange often form what is

usually known as a Council of Social Agencies. This organization holds meetings at which members can exchange views and the result is usually a much greater unification and co-ordination of charitable assistance in the district which it serves. The charitable organizations of the country also hold an annual National Conference of Social Work, where social workers from the different organizations can exchange points of view and experiences. There are numerous national associations specialized branches of social work and, as we said earlier in the chapter, Catholic social workers and Catholic charitable organizations are also organized nationally and hold an annual conference. 

conference. Catholic hospitals have their separate organization.

In many districts social work agencies secure their funds by community-chest-drives.

One of the greatest problems of social workers is the obtaining of funds to carry on their work. Group financing of private charities has proved an effective way of removing this disability. Councils of 

Social Agencies led the way to community chests, which is the name given to the central money-raising organization for financing nearly all the social work in a community. In 1942 there were 619 such chests in the United States, through which a total of 96 million dollars were raised for charity."

The participating agencies in a community chest usually appoint representatives to a board, which also includes prominent businessmen, bankers, and others, to serve as representatives of the local contributors to the fund. Prior to the yearly campaign for money, each participating agency is required to prepare a budget for the coming year. This involves planning ahead not only for current expenses, but also for necessary extensions; it generally involves also the adoption of uniform accounting systems. Next come extensive preparations for the systematic solicitation of donations from all citizens, use being made of up-to-date publicity methods to in- 'Cf. Social Work Year Book, 1943, p. 221.

FF "Do Unto Others

OCT. 

16-26 

COMMUNITY FUND Courtesy Milwaukee Community Chest A Community Chest appeal. 

sure as large contributions as possible. The chest then makes an active campaign for funds during a fixed period of one or two weeks. The goal aimed at is the amount of the consolidated financial needs of the participating agencies, and an extensive drive is made to reach or even to exceed this figure. Proceeds are divided among the participants in the agreed amounts. Usually contributors to the chest are promised that during the ensuing year they will not be asked for further donations by the agencies within the federation. 

By this method the participating agencies usually obtain more

funds than they would by private endeavors to secure donations, and as the chest board insists that they maintain high standards of operation, the money contributed is used to good purpose, and the poor often benefit by better service. Occasionally, the administrative boards of the chests have favored the strong and powerful members, at a sacrifice of the interests of the smaller agencies; and in some districts there have been complaints that the board unduly interfered with the work of the individual agencies, and did not understand their aims. For these and other reasons, Catholic charities have not always participated in local federated finance drives through the chests, although they have done so in many localities, to their entire satisfaction. 

One of the chief arguments against the community chest is the fact that donations to it are often made public, so that the finer side of charity voluntary, spontaneous, and inconspicuous giving is entirely abandoned. Yet against this, it may be said that some people give to a community chest who would not donate privately to charity. Besides, those who are fortunate enough to possess wealth need not limit their charitable contributions to the chest fund alone.

It is the duty of the State to assist the dependent poor and needy when private charity is inadequate, and citizens are bound to contribute their fair share of these expenses. 

Many hold that private charity or almsgiving is the most effective way of relieving needs and this is, indeed, frequently the case. 

In the United States by far the greater proportion of help to the dependent poor is through private individuals and private agencies. Private charities, however, are not always adequate. They are often haphazard and do not take care of all needs, or they may be insufficient. People usually feel more charitably inclined in periods of great disaster, such as flood; or at times of national celebration, for example, at Christmas time. They may fail to realize or refuse to recognize the needs of the poor during the rest of the year. For

this reason, where private charities prove insufficient, the State is bound to see that the dependent poor are satisfactorily provided for, and citizens must bear their fair share of the cost by taxation or some other equitable method by which the State may raise the necessary funds. There is, however, a danger of the State undertaking charitable work to an excessive degree. Such a situation results in bureaucracy and should be avoided. 

In the United States, the State's duty of caring for the needy is undertaken both locally and nationally. 

Each city or county has its local poorhouse for vagrants and those awaiting further care, and also looks after delinquents, including juveniles who may need special training. Usually there are some community provisions for the ill, the aged, the handicapped, and the orphan, though these latter classes may be adequately taken care of by private agencies. State or county provision is made for the handicapped and the insane. Much of the distress formerly caused by unemployment is now eliminated by social insurance payments through the Social Security Act of 1935. 

Most states provide financial assistance to needy widows who can look after their children in their own homes. This state aid for dependent children is now supplemented by federal aid, under the Social Security Act; orphans whose fathers were insured against old age under the Social Security Act are now entitled to benefit payments.

Most states also provide financial assistance to the aged who have no means of support, and this aid has likewise been supplemented by federal finances under the Social Security Act. In addition, many workers are now insured against old age under the same Act, and receive a pension when they retire from work. Since the 1939 amendment of the Act, widows of workers who die, their children under sixteen (eighteen if they are attending school), and in some cases dependent parents, also receive some benefits, as was explained in Chapter Eleven. Of course, payments under an insurance scheme are not charity, but receivable as a right by the insured parties.

Most of the states also make some financial provision for the needy blind, which has been supplemented by federal assistance since the 1935 Social Security Act. 

The Social Security Act likewise provides for federal grants of money to states to provide maternal and child-health services, services to crippled children, and general provision for child welfare, public health services, and vocational rehabilitation. Loans to needy farm families have been allotted through the Federal Farm Security 

Administration. 

Except where insurance is concerned, or loans to be repaid, the federal and state funds to provide the services we have just mentioned come from general taxation. 

Health needs and general provision for handicapped persons will be discussed in the next chapter.

Chapter Seventeen  

The purpose of this chapter is: 

1. To realize the importance of general good health in the community, and that private care is not sufficient to insure good health for the majority, with adequate provision for illness and physical and mental handicaps. 

2. To learn what is being done by local governments and by the 

Federal Government to improve the health of citizens. 

3. To understand the special problems and needs of the handicapped: the crippled, the blind, the hard of hearing and the deaf, as well as mental deficiencies and the insane.

To maintain the community in good health, and to take care of the handicapped is a major problem of sound social living which cannot be adequately taken care of by private endeavor alone. 

All ill-health, and physical and mental handicaps deprive the general community of a certain amount of well-being and security. 

Communicable diseases are a menace to public welfare in general. 

Illness and deficiency lower the efficiency of the persons concerned, so that the community as a whole loses some of their potential productiveness. In addition to this material loss, suffering usually causes worry and mental pain either to the sufferer or to his relatives and friends, and the less happy and contented people are, the less harmonious is social life in general. 

That the health of the people of the United States is a serious problem must be evident when we examine some statistics. The 

United States Public Health Service has reported that on an average winter day in 1935-36 six million persons were unable to attend work or school, or carry on their ordinary activities! In 1937 the 307

Social Security Board stated that out of each million persons in the United States, an average of 460,000 are ill once or twice a year, and 70,000 are ill three or four times a year; one fourth of those who become ill are disabled for periods varying from one week to an entire year.¹ In the same report it was computed that the general cost of illness in families with incomes of less than $2,500 annually, was 1500 million dollars for medical care, and about 900 million dollars as loss of wages resulting from illness.2 

In addition to temporary or chronically ill persons, census statistics show a large number of blind, deaf, crippled, mental defectives, epileptics, and insane.

To a large extent ill-health and physical or mental deficiencies are provided for privately, but private endeavor alone is insufficient.

Those who can meet the cost of medical care, medicines, hospital services, and the like, usually cover the expenses of their own illnesses and physical needs. Very many, however, cannot afford private attention, and if they are not insured to cover the risks involved, they must rely on charity. Many private doctors, nurses, hospitals, and clinics give free or low-cost attention to those who need it and are unable to pay. Nevertheless, low-cost or free private health service is very inadequate in some districts and then, too, poor persons often hesitate to ask for assistance, and postpone approaching a doctor or hospital until it is too late for effective help. 

To remedy this situation certain group medical schemes have been inaugurated as a solution for those with an income sufficient to pay for them. Members of these group schemes make regular monthly or annual payments for themselves or their families, which entitle them to complete or partial medical and hospital attention either free or for a lower fee than is usual. In general, however, the fee for membership in such schemes is beyond the purse of the 1 

Social Security Board, Social Security in America, pp. 315, 316. 

2 'Ibid., p. 315.

really poor; an insufficient number of health risks are included; and the schemes are available only in certain districts. 

No community is prosperous in the full sense of the term unless its citizens are in good health, and unless provision is made to prevent the spread of illness and disease and to provide for those who are physically or mentally defective. Private endeavor is usually insufficient to take care of all the nation's health needs, and for this reason State assistance is required. 

Local and state governments are doing much to preserve the health of citizens.

Most local governments provide such essential health services for town and city people as pure-water supply, street cleaning, the collection and disposal of garbage and sewage, and the enforcement of sanitary codes for dairies, food markets, restaurants, and other specified premises. These measures are important to prevent the spread of communicable diseases and general insanitary conditions. 

Each of the states has general laws to protect and improve good health, including laws to secure adequate ventilation and other measures necessary to maintain the health of persons employed in factories and workshops and of children in the schools. Each state has also its regulations concerning the licensing of doctors and nurses who desire to practice within its borders. Even if every family had an income sufficient to cover the expenses of illness, and to take adequate care of any members who might be physically or mentally defective, there would still be need of such protective or preventive laws.

Local and state governments often go even farther in their provision for the health needs of the community, realizing that if private endeavor is inadequate, then the welfare of the community is definitely better served if the state provides the deficiencies. 

For children, most schools provide health education on cleanliness, the right type of clothing and the food needed for health, and simple first aid for home accidents. Periodical examinations of stu-

dents are conducted in the schools, for the prevention and detection of deficiencies. Very often hospital and dental services are provided for those who need them; there are special schools and special facilities for handicapped and defective children; and free meals may be provided for needy children, to insure that they be not deprived of essential vitamins and minerals through lack of adequate food. Many communities have public hospital facilities, with free treatment, for needy adult persons too, and visiting nurses take care of ill people in their own homes, either free or, if the patients can afford a small fee, then at minimum cost. 

While all these services are very adequately provided in some communities, certain cities, and most rural districts, are very deficient in suitable health provision for those who cannot afford to pay for their own needs, and where private aid is insufficient or even lacking.

The Federal Government also renders important health service to the nation.

The Federal Government's duty is to supply whatever health facilities are needed in the nation, which private endeavor and local and state governments have not provided, or are unable to supply. 

The Federal Government performs its duty in this respect in a variety of ways.

The Public Health Service, within the Federal Security Agency, is specifically charged with all matters relating to the protection and improvement of the public health. Its chief function is research in the cause and methods of prevention and control of disease. It cooperates with local and state health agencies, publishes public health information, and maintains quarantines at important ports of entry into the United States, to avoid the entrance of persons with contagious or communicable disease. It maintains hospitals for those who have served in government services. 

The Food and Drug Administration within the Department of 

Agriculture administers the Food and Drug Acts, inspects and

U. S. Public Health Service 

A Public Health Service physician listens to a small boy's heartbeat in a pre-school clinic. analyzes the various foods and products falling within its jurisdiction, and publishes information to help manufacturers and others to keep within the law.

The Bureau of Narcotics in the Treasury Department guards against the presence and misuse of harmful drugs in the country. 

The Children's Bureau of the Department of Labor was established in 1912 to take care of the administration of maternal and child health services. It administers federal services for crippled children, the child welfare services under the Social Security Act of 1935, and the provisions of the Fair Labor Standards Act of 1938 relating to child labor. In 1921 the Sheppard-Towner Act provided an annual federal appropriation of $1,240,000 to be administered through this bureau for the health care of children and mothers, but the Act was repealed in 1929 and adequate federal aid was not again allocated for this health service until the Social 

Security Act of 1935. 

The Federal Board for Vocational Education was established in 1917 to cooperate with the states in rehabilitating and restoring to remunerative employment any persons disabled in industry or otherwise. This service was increased in scope by the Social Security 

Act of 1935 and since 1939 it has functioned as part of the Office of Education within the Federal Security Agency. 

Of the health measures of the Social Security Act of 1935, amended in 1939, the Federal Government is authorized to distribute among qualifying states annually, $5,820,000 for maternal and child-health services; $3,870,000 for services to crippled children; $1,510,000 for child-welfare organization, particularly in rural districts, to be allotted through the children's bureau; $3,500,000 for vocational rehabilitation; and $11,000,000 for public-health services. For needy adult blind persons, who are not living in institutions, the Federal Government, through the Bureau of Public Assistance of the Federal Security Agency, will contribute one half of whatever the state pays to each person up to a maximum of twenty dollars.

Because it found the health of our poorer class of farmers in such a deplorable state, the Federal Farm Security Administration of the United States Department of Agriculture worked out a group med-

Courtesy Henry Street Visiting Nurse Service 

A visiting nurse caring for a young child in the home. Such service is important in maintaining health standards in the nation. 3 ical care plan. First the Administration obtains the approval of the State Medical Association where it wishes to establish the program, and secondly, the active cooperation of county medical societies. It then invites those families which have received the loans mentioned in Chapter Nine in connection with rural economic distress, to take advantage of the scheme. Each family which has received a loan is invited to pay from $15 to $30 a year per family according to their income, into a common fund. The family can choose a physician and secure all needed medical care, and hospitalization. The bills of the doctor and the hospital are then paid out of the local group fund. By the end of 1940, some 80,000 farm families belonged to the scheme, in 33 states. It must be noted 

'Cf. Memorandum on The Medical Care Program for Farm Security Admin istration Borrowers, issued July, 1940.

that these plans are private schemes, based on the self-help idea according to the ability to pay. It is a voluntary cooperation by families and their physicians. It is only their organization which is sponsored by the Farm Security Administration. Because of the extreme poverty of migrant families in California and Arizona, and a few other states, the Farm Security Administration, with the cooperation of state health and relief departments, formed the Agricultural Workers' Health and Medical Association in 1938, to give free or low-cost medical attention to those in need of it. 

The crippled and those with permanently broken health are cared for in a variety of ways. 

In 1930 it was estimated that the number of crippled children in the United States was between 289,000 and 365,000, but the Social Security Act of 1935 gave the number of crippled in the country as approximately one for every 100 population, or a total of about 1,300,000.

The causes of crippling are many, ranging from hereditary defects, to deformities due to tuberculosis, rickets, paralysis, arthritis, prenatal injury, birth accidents, and accidents after birth. Happily, the number of children who are crippled as a result of malnutrition and other effects of poverty is now greatly diminished, because of the more widespread medical and material assistance offered to mothers and prospective mothers, free meals and milk to needy school children, and similar social services. Advances in medical science have also contributed to the cure of those suffering from infantile paralysis, arthritis, and other diseases.

The treatment of cripples is, of course, provided mainly through hospitals. Yet more than medical treatment is needed to make a cripple financially independent in adult life. Vocational training can often rehabilitate adults to take their place in industry, though not necessarily in the occupation which they formerly pursued. 

Handicapped children, however, require not only industrial train- 

'White House Conference Preliminary Committee Reports, p. 453.

ing to fit them to earn a livelihood whenever possible, but also such education as is compatible with their state of health. They often need help, too, to become accustomed to whatever orthopedic appliances they may have to wear. Sometimes day schools are sufficient for their needs, possibly with the provision of special transportation and suitable desks and other equipment. Often it is best for a crippled child to become a resident in a special institution, despite the danger of the "institutionalizing" effect. In this respect 

Catholic cripples in the United States are better provided for than those who are handicapped in other ways, for there are numerous Catholic hospitals and institutions to care for them. 

Since the passing of the Social Security Act of 1935, which gave financial provision for states maintaining services for crippled children, state facilities have been provided by all the states of the union. A special division of the Children's Bureau within the Department of Labor handles this federal service. 

The blind need special training and care. 

The official Census of 1930 reported a total of only 69,593 blind persons in the United States. This figure is generally considered to be incomplete, and current estimates place the number at between 200,000 and 250,000.5 Blindness is therefore a serious problem as regards number, as well as regards the special handicap which the blind must surmount in becoming economically independent and able to take their place in normal social living. 

The causes of blindness are varied. Some blindness is congenital, as a result of heredity or some other prenatal cause. Inherited blindness may appear either at birth, or as a progressive defect, for example, nearsightedness. At least 80 per cent of blindness, however, results from lack of attention to the eyes at birth, or from injuries, accidents, inflammation following measles or other diseases, or degenerative changes due to old age. 

It is now generally recognized that blind children need special 

5 Social Work Year Book, 1941, p. 75.

Keystone View 

A blind man learning to make brooms. The blind can be taught to be self-sustaining. training and care, which can be secured only in schools set apart for that purpose. As the best results are obtained from those who have had their senses of touch and sound developed in early years, this training should begin with kindergarten work and not be postponed until the end of the school period. Despite the danger of "institutionalization" to children who are brought up in institutions, many people consider that blind children are best away from the home environment, where they may be neglected, or pampered and spoiled for fighting their own way in the world in later years.

A number of local authorities, however, provide day schools for the blind. By learning to use a typewriter and with the help of a reader, many a blind person has attended an ordinary high school and college. Blind persons have been helped especially in recent years by the "seeing eye" dogs, which are especially trained to guide their masters without human assistance in streets and thoroughfares. 

In training the blind particular attention is now paid to physical education, and to instruction in music, and in industrial arts, such as basket making, weaving, and metalwork. For many years, special books have also been provided for the blind, so that blind persons are no longer dependent on the kindness of others for their entertainment and instruction. These books in Braille are expensive, however, and free libraries for the blind are often sadly in need of funds. Many blind persons have made excellent success in life, though they generally need the help of an institution to find them work, as they are particularly handicapped in obtaining employment. A number of Catholic schools and societies are providing translations of suitable Catholic books, but there seems to be a need for an extension of these services and for a greater number of Catholics to take a friendly interest in blind persons living in their neighborhood. 

For the adult blind the federal and state governments provide vocational rehabilitation through the Office of Education, and also special pensions to the needy blind, as already mentioned. In addition, the Federal Government, through the Federal Security Agency assists in the work of the American Printing House for the Blind. 

Modern care of the blind does not end with providing for those who are already handicapped in this manner. Babies' sore eyes (opthalmia neonatorum) which caused much blindness has been almost eliminated by the compulsory dropping of a weak (1 per cent) solution of nitrat: of silver into every infant's eyes at birth.

Appropriate factory laws have greatly diminished eyestrain. The increase of skilled medical attention for all children of school age, the free testing of eyesight and provision of glasses, and special

Courtesy Mr. G. E. Denman

A class in a city-operated deaf school. Each group in this school uses its own multiple hearing aid for academic instruction. sight-saving classes for those with defective vision, have helped greatly to diminish preventable blindness. 

The hard of hearing, the deaf, and deaf-mutes, while not so handicapped as the blind, also need special care and training. 

The 1930 census reported 57,123 deaf-mutes in the country, but it has been estimated that there are from 90,000 to 120,000 deaf persons in the United States, and about ten million persons who are hard of hearing. Although deafness is a severe handicap in life, it is not insurmountable. 

Social Work Year Book, 1941, p. 167. 

'Ibid., p. 168.

There are various causes for deafness. One of them is heredity, particularly as a result of marriage between close relatives. Heredity, however, is usually considered a minor factor, and the principal causes are meningitis, brain fever, primary ear disease, and various contagious or infectious diseases, such as measles and diphtheria. 

The deaf who are taught lip reading or a combination of lip reading and sign language can usually take their place in the world and suffer little from their handicap. Special schools, however, are needed to give them this necessary help, and usually special instruction is provided in those arts and industries where deafness is least a handicap. As with blindness, increased facilities for the medical inspection of children is greatly diminishing deafness, particularly when due to primary ear disease. 

The deaf and dumb are, of course, in a much more serious position, but it has been discovered that mutism is sometimes due simply to lack of adequate training. If the deafness occurs in childhood and there is no special training, the deaf person is necessarily mute, for we learn to speak largely from hearing others. This is not always so, however, and deaf-mutes need especial training. 

The Federal Government maintains the Columbia Institute for the Deaf in Washington, D. C., which includes an advanced department known as Gallaudet College, the only college in the country especially founded for deaf persons. Many schools and institutions for the deaf, a number of them Catholic, are to be found in various parts of the country. Unable to hear oral instruction, and often forced to attend special schools under non-Catholic auspices, the Catholic deaf are in great need of an extension of Catholic services. 

In some churches special apparatus is installed in certain pews for the benefit of the hard of hearing. The number of priests doing special work for the deaf increases yearly. 

Mental deficiency provides another serious social problem. 

Mental deficients are those who have never been of average intelligence. Their bodies may attain normal growth, but their intel-

lects are either undeveloped from birth, or development ceases at a very early age. Those who are thus handicapped mentally are of three main types:8 

1. An idiot is a mentally defective person usually having a men tal age of less than three years or, if a child, an intelligence quotient of less than 20. In other words, idiots are unable to preserve themselves from ordinary physical dangers and need constant care and attention. 

2. An imbecile has a mental age of 3 to 7 years inclusive, or an intelligence quotient of from 20-49. Imbeciles are, therefore, never able to manage their own affairs and also need constant care. 

3. A moron has a mental age of 8 years or upwards or an intelligence quotient of between 50 and 69. Morons do not exceed the mentality of a child of 12 years.

Since there is no sharp dividing line between the normal person and the high-grade moron, it is difficult to estimate the number of mental deficients in the United States. In 1936 there were 112,678 mentally deficient and epileptic patients in institutions in the United States, most of which institutions were state owned and operated. Many mental deficients, however, live at home, and it has been estimated that there are approximately one million persons in the United States who are so dull mentally that they would prove unable to cope with any unusual situation which might arise.10 On the other hand, it sometimes happens that a child who appears to be abnormally dull or "retarded" is not necessarily of low intelligence, but lacks interest or attention in his studies, or for some reason is of abnormally slow growth.

Heredity undoubtedly plays an important part in mental deficiency, but there are many other causes also, such as prenatal injuries or defects, undernourishment or mental worry of the mother, 'Mental Defectives and Epileptics in Institutions, 1936, U. S. Dept. of Commerce, Bureau of the Census, p. 14. 'Ibid., p. 4. 

10 Cf. Social Work Year Book, 1941, p. 353.

accidents, illnesses such as measles or encephalitis, and bad environment which is not conducive to mental development. In regard to the last named cause, if, for example, a mother is mentally deficient and cannot give proper attention to her child's mental needs, it is very possible that the child will grow up dull in intelligence unless his social environment outside the home is exceptionally good. 

Mental deficients need protection from harmful influences all their life, for they rarely attain the measure of prudence necessary to care for their own affairs. Special schools are often provided for them by local educational authorities; sometimes they are placed in colonies or institutions, and sometimes they are placed in foster homes. If educated for some suitable employment, such as agricultural work for boys and domestic service for girls, and if trained in good habits, high-grade morons are capable of earning their own living and of leading useful lives in the world with a minimum of supervision. A certain amount of supervision is, however, necessary, for they never attain normal prudence and social stability. 

Prevention of mental deficiency chiefly takes the form of general health care, and general advice to those with seemingly inherited mental defects that they should not marry.

The insane are given very scientific help in most parts of the 

United States today.

Insanity is a relative term, based on a so-called norm of average conduct and intellectual power which is very elusive of definition. 

The sane are those who are in touch with reality, those who can control their thoughts and emotions, who are capable of selfmanagement, of adjusting themselves to environment, and of reacting normally to situations. Just when sanity ceases and insanity begins may be difficult to decide in borderline cases. On the other hand, some insane people are easy to discover because their conduct is far removed from that of the sane. 

The insane are those who developed mentally and emotionally in a normal manner in early life, but whose minds later became

diseased. In 1939 a total of 514,712 persons in the United States were in hospitals for the insane or were on parole from such hospitals.¹¹ 

Insanity may be inherited, that is, one may be born with a predisposition to become insane at a certain age. Many cases of insanity, however, are due to what is called an organic disorder, being caused by brain destruction, injury to the nervous tissue, glandular deficiencies, and the like. 

It is now recognized that insanity is a disease to which anyone is liable, and that a remedy can often be found for it if the patient is given correct treatment at an early stage. Hospitals have therefore been erected for the treatment of the insane in most districts of the country, and there the patients may receive necessary treatment either as outdoor assistance or, more usually, as temporary inmates. People used to consider insanity as a disgrace, but sensible persons no longer do so, with the result that many persons who have been insane at one time or another of their life are able to take their place as normal persons in the world. 

It is also recognized today that much insanity can be avoided, even when there is an inherited predisposition to it, by sound, sensible living and by adequate training of the emotions and the will. Well-integrated persons run much less risk of becoming insane. 

For this reason, good education includes will training, and insists on the performance of difficult and sometimes even distasteful tasks. In many schools courses on mental hygiene have been instituted: courses which teach how one may avoid insanity so far as this is possible.

There is no question but that the social welfare of the community is not adequately achieved until physical and mental health needs are adequately taken care of, and until everything possible is done to prevent unhealthy conditions.

Chapter Eighteen  The purpose of this chapter is: 

1. To realize the serious effect of crime, and to know that there are many factors leading to crime which might be eliminated if our social living were better organized. 

2. To realize that the State has a right to punish delinquents, and a duty to protect its citizens, but that modern ideas of punishment include an attempt to reform the wrongdoer. 

3. To learn something of the special problem of dealing with young people who begin to show a lack of self-respect and a neglect of good citizenship even before they reach adult life.

Social welfare requires that the community be protected against criminals and delinquents.

To enable men to live harmoniously in civic society, the State must pass laws for order and well-being, conformable to the natural law. It is the duty of citizens to obey just laws, for they are necessary for the community well-being. Social welfare requires that the 

State do everything possible to prevent infractions of the law, in order to prevent the disharmony which arises from these transgressions. When they are broken, the transgressors should be punished. Crime and delinquency are a behavior problem. The delinquent shows an utterly selfish disregard for the rights of others, and an unwillingness to take his share of responsibility for sound social living. It is our purpose to examine this problem in the United 

States, to discover what may be done to prevent it, and also to learn how we deal with those who break the law. 

In general, the term delinquency expresses either a crime or a misdemeanor, although the word is often used to mean less serious 327

This and the chart on the following page shows the crime in its relation to age. The large number of criminals in the lower age groups is disturbing. transgressions of the law. Such serious crime às burglary, robbery, homicide, brutal assault, treason, and arson, is known as a felony. 

Delinquents who commit felonies are termed criminals, for they have willfully and seriously injured or done violence to another's property or person. Delinquencies which are lesser violations of the law or violations of relatively unimportant laws, are called misdemeanors. Most crime is a sin, for it is against the moral law and also against the law of the State. Citizens are bound to obey the just laws which the State enacts for the maintenance of law and order and for the development of the common good. Some misdemeanors may not be sins, and, of course, not all sins are crimes in the eyes of the State, although they may even be serious delinquencies before God.

Crime and delinquency is a very important problem in the

United States, for it seems to be increasing yearly. There were about 180,000 prisoners in state and federal prisons and adult reformatories in 1939, in comparison with 1930 census figures of 120,000. Many criminals and delinquents remain at large in the community and are not apprehended by the authorities; others, having been tried and convicted, are on probation and parole, terms which we shall explain later in this chapter. In addition to this, there are many juvenile offenders against the law, who are housed in reformatories, placed in foster homes, or are allowed to remain in their own homes on probation.

The most serious aspect of crime is, of course, that it involves sin against God. From the sociologist's viewpoint, the main problem is the lack of harmony which arises in society when delinquencies are committed, or when it is feared that they may come 1 ¹ Cf. Social Work Year Book, 1941, p. 38; 1937 figures were officially given as 

145,038 (cf. The Statistical Abstract of the United States, 1939, p. 76).

about. From the economic side one must consider the cost of maintaining the court system, the police force, prisons, and reformatories; one must also realize the losses occasioned by wanton destruction of property; and in addition there is the tremendous cost of insurance against crime, and the cost of safes, burglar alarms, armored cars, and other devices. 

The chief factors leading to crime and the remedies which may be proposed to eliminate them. 

1. DEFECTIVE RELIGIOUS AND MORAL EDUCATION. Most crime is due to a defective moral attitude, resulting from lack of true notions of rights and duties toward God, toward one's neighbor, and toward oneself, and to a neglect of training in habits of virtue and self-restraint. Every human being has certain bad tendencies, due to the inheritance of original sin and to faulty environment and the acquisition of bad habits. No person, however, inherits a criminal disposition. With the right attitude toward life a person can always maintain a satisfactory social life as a law-abiding citizen. Home life and the educational system ought to help the child to grow up with worth-while attitudes and ideals, yet some persons become delinquents precisely because the home or the school has failed to train them when young along right lines of conduct. Catholics realize the importance of prayer and the Sacraments in providing grace to overcome temptations. 

2. DEFECTIVE HOME LIFE AND BAD COMPANIONS. Children are particularly susceptible to the influence of home and play environment. Some parents are shiftless, ignorant, lacking in self-restraint, selfish, emotional, immoral, or delinquent, and for these reasons neglect the proper training of their children, giving them instead the disadvantages of bad example and bad environment. Bad housing conditions, uncongenial home life, parents who drink too much (thereby showing weakness of character and lack of self-control), the breakup of the home because of divorce, are all factors, too, which may lead to children growing up undisciplined, associating

with bad companions, and ultimately adopting criminal careers. 

Better community planning, and a more correct idea of social values seem to be the only remedies for these causes. 

3. INDUSTRIAL CONDITIONS AND A LACK OF VOCATIONAL TRAINING. 

The very visible discrepancies between the rich and the poor, in combination with present-day materialism and overvaluation of wealth, often lead to present-day temptations to steal, embezzle, and commit frauds. A child who takes up a "blind-alley" occupation is without a trade when he grows up, and is usually forced to adopt some occupation which will give him less than a living wage and will often submit him to the temptations just mentioned. Of course, he is not bound to give in to the temptations, and will not do so if he has strength of will and has received a good earlier 

4. COMMERCIAL AMUSEMENTS AND THE PRESS. Much crime is suggested by newspaper reports, cheap magazines, motion pictures, and plays. Gambling, intemperance, and drug addiction also lead to crime by weakening morale and conscience, and inciting to passion. Most of these circumstances can be largely eliminated by a sane censorship of the theater, the screen, and the press, and by a better provision of wholesome noncommercial recreational facilities by private associations and local government authorities. 

5. PUBLIC ATTITUDE TOWARD CRIME AND THE PENAL SYSTEM. 

The moral attitude of a community toward crime, the integrity or corruption of local law enforcement agencies, and quality of court procedure greatly influence the prevalence of crime. If the police accept graft or show favoritism in any way, or if the police force is inadequate for the size of the community, crime usually flourishes. 

The reason for this is that people who have not been properly trained, or who disregard their social obligations, think that their crimes will go undetected, or that if they are caught they will be able to save themselves from punishment. Certain aspects of our court system have, at times, been severely criticized. Occasionally the courts are lax in prosecuting criminals and in punishing them

adequately, graft among court officials sometimes occurs, the jury system is not a perfect one for the jurors are often subject to emotional appeal, and there are other imperfections which need to be remedied in certain districts. The only solution to these problems is, first, the education of citizens to regard the law as their greatest protection if they wish to maintain sound social living; and secondly to educate citizens who will wish to take their part in civic affairs and will have the necessary knowledge to advocate worth-while remedies for the abuses which may exist. 

The prevention and the punishment of delinquency is one of the chief duties and problems of the State. 

The function of the State is the protection of citizens and the promotion of the common good. One of the government's chief duties, therefore, is not merely to make wise laws which are in conformity with the natural law, but also to punish offenders in a fitting manner, and to eliminate the causes of crime so far as this is possible.

It would seem to be the State's duty to promote, in so far as it can, the measures already outlined for the prevention of crime. 

As for the punishment of crime, this should be done with four objectives in view: (1) Punishment should be retributive, but expiation should not be excessive nor necessarily in proportion to the crime. (2) It should have a deterring effect; punishment should be as unpleasant as is compatible with justice and the other objectives of punishment, so that the offender himself will be inclined to make a better choice in the future and will refrain from committing the same delinquency again. (3) It should have a preventive effect, that is, punishment should be such that others will be prevented from wishing to commit crime. (4) Although the State's first duty is justice, after retributive justice has been effected, and after the deterring and preventive side of punishment has had its part, the 

State should seek to reform the offender. When once an offender comes before the court, the problem is that of preventing that indi-

vidual from repeating the same offense, while punishing him in a fitting manner. Sometimes reformation is effected more readily without imprisonment, or by a more lenient sentence. Modern "socialized courts" examine the past environment of the offender, try to discover the particular causes of his delinquency, and then consider the best method of treating him.

Sometimes a crime is so grave that the government can legitimately administer the death penalty upon an offender whose guilt is clearly established: this is called capital punishment. The fear of death is universal. Where it is the practice of a State to have judgment swiftly following upon the apprehension of an offender, if the judgment is that of death, the sentence should be speedily carried out without any emotional "glory" attendant upon the exploitation of "news" in the press. The death sentence is a fitting punishment for a heinous crime, and punishment meted out in the manner just described is very effective in preventing others from committing a similar offense and may be a necessary safeguard for the protection and welfare of the community. 

How delinquents are dealt with in the United States. 

When a delinquent is apprehended, he is either allowed to go on bail until he is tried, or he is sent to the local jail to await trial. 

If he is freed on bail, he must usually provide a sum of money to prove the good faith of his promise that he will return for the trial. 

Many local jails are far from ideal places of detention: the unconvicted and first offenders may be placed with habitual or incorrigible criminals, and those suffering from diseases may be placed in close contact with the others.

When a delinquent is tried in the court, he may be acquitted of the charge against him, in which case he leaves the court a free man. If he is convicted, the punishment may be fixed by law, in which case the court is bound to administer it. If, however, the offense is not of the type with fixed punishment upon convictions, then except in cases of minor misdemeanors, most modern courts

examine the social history of each offender before the trial. The causes of the delinquency are thus ascertained so far as is possible, and fitting punishment or remedies can be applied. Often physical, psychiatric, and psychological examinations are given before trial to determine causes other than social. In this way mentally defective and insane persons are separated from offenders of other categories, and are given special treatment. 

Where no fixed punishment is made by law, a convicted delinquent's punishment may be of several kinds. Some delinquents are merely fined. Fines have the advantage of costing the State nothing and being, instead, a source of revenue. They can be adjusted to the offense, and are a punishment for all but the very rich. They are, however, unduly hard upon the poor, for usually the alternative to paying the fine is a prison sentence. 

Those sentenced for short periods of imprisonment, up to nine months or so, usually remain in the city or county jail, whereas others either pass to a state penitentiary or, if they have broken a federal law (a law binding on all the citizens of the United States), to a federal prison. We shall shortly describe the different methods of dealing with prisoners. 

Sometimes convicted offenders are placed on probation. 

Probation is a system which, in general, has been very successful. 

The Federal Government, and most of the states in the Union have adopted what is known as the probation system in dealing with certain types of adult prisoners, and all except one state provide for juvenile probation. A delinquent who is placed on probation is allowed to remain free, but is under the supervision of a court official called a probation officer. The probationer reports to this officer at regular intervals. This forms part of his punishment. 

Usually restrictions are placed upon him as to his recreation, companions, and the hour at which he must retire from the streets at night. If he fails to carry out the probation regulations, he is taken before the court again, and usually committed.

The probation officer tries to help the offender to reform by taking a friendly interest in him, finding him work where necessary, and looking after his material and mental welfare to the best of his ability. Probation is successful only with certain types of delinquents, and only when the probation officer is conscientious in the performance of his obligation to the court, to society, and to the delinquent. In many cities, the St. Vincent de Paul Society, the 

Knights of Columbus, and other organizations provide Catholic probation officers for Catholic delinquents.

Modern prisons aim at building up the health and morale of delinquents.

Best modern penal methods aim at reforming the delinquent, not merely hardening him, by building up his morale, instead of breaking it down. In the best type of prisons, attention is usually given to the physical training of prisoners, so that they are put into good physical condition; to their education and industrial training, so that they can earn an honest livelihood when freed; and to their moral and religious instruction. This modern idea of reformation has led not only to the establishment of the probation system, but also to prison camps and farms, and to the indeterminate sentence with parole.

Prison camps originated as a result of overcrowding in prisons and the necessity of finding additional accommodation. In these camps offenders live in huts and work on the construction of new roads, or on the reclamation of land, or afforestation. This system costs the community much less than does the upkeep of prisons, and is usually much more helpful than ordinary prison conditions for building up morale and physical strength. A similar plan is that of the farms which are sometimes attached to prisons, where prisoners live in houses after the cottage or colony plan detailed in Chapter Sixteen. Most women prisoners are now looked after in colonies of this kind, particularly when they come under the federal administration. Here they are taught to care for a home

redorg sit sh sdond ing moboM insupnilob bom 1298 bed visiom wob ti gai

Black Star and Acme 

Scenes of prison life. An effort is made to rehabilitate prisoners through trade training, education, and wholesome recreation. Prisoners are thus given an opportunity to prepare for their return to normal social life. evda ads of and to make home life attractive, and the method has proved very successful where it has been carried out. 

Of course, not all prisons are conducted on the best modern lines, but the tendency is for ever greater improvement. 

Parole and the indeterminate sentence are also features of modern penology which may be successful with certain types of prisoners.

The indeterminate sentence is one which states the minimum and maximum number of years the delinquent is to be imprisoned, instead of giving a definite term of imprisonment. After the expiration of the minimum term, the prison administration board or the judge has the option of releasing the prisoner on parole at any time up to the fixed maximum term of imprisonment, when, of

course, he must be released and when parole must cease. In this way prisoners can be kept in custody long enough to make scientific treatment of individual needs possible and useful, and when the delinquent shows signs of reform, provided the minimum term of imprisonment is accomplished, he may be released. This method thus gives a delinquent a powerful motive for a sincere attempt at reformation. His behavior is generally measured by the merit or progressive-stage system, which allows a prisoner to obtain certain privileges or to lose them, depending on his conduct. 

Parole is not pardon. A prisoner on parole is subjected to the same supervision as a person on probation, and he receives the same assistance. If he does not behave properly, the parole may be withdrawn at any time before the maximum sentence has expired, and he is again put into prison to serve his full term of imprisonment. 

Parole has several disadvantages. Where prison authorities or court officials are corrupt, it makes the way easy for bribery and inadequate punishment. The worst difficulty, however, is that of finding suitable employment for the parolee. Many people fear to employ an ex-prisoner, lest he again become delinquent. 

Prison labor is usually paid for in modern prison systems. 

Although unconvicted prisoners awaiting trial cannot be made to work, convicted prisoners are usually given some form of work to keep them occupied during the greater part of the day. This work is now often allotted as part of a scientific program of reformation, and not with the idea of putting the prison on a profitmaking basis. Where a man is not a skilled craftsman, under best prison conditions he is trained in work which will fit him to take a decent place in the economic world when he is released. If a prisoner is paid a wage for the work which he does, this will give him an incentive to work well, and any honest labor has a great therapeutic value and can assist in reform. A small amount of the pay is usually given to the prisoner to spend on whatever comforts the prison offers to him for sale, such as tobacco. The balance is

Courtesy National Probation Assoc., Inc. 

Scene in a juvenile court. The judge questions the delinquent, who sits between his parents. Others present are the court reporter and the probation officer on the case. The probation system has proved to be the best for handling youthful delinquents. placed to the prisoner's account, either to be saved for him until he is released from prison, or, if he has dependents, to be sent to these so that he does not lose sight of his obligations of providing for them, and so that they do not become public charges. 

The economic problem involved in prison labor concerns the disposal of the products made. Of course, prisoners usually do all the needed work of the prison, but the disposal of other goods has

Courtesy The Boys Town Times 

An aerial view of Boys Town. This is the best known of all the institutions caring for delinquent and homeless boys. Boys Town is a true town with its own mayor and post office. caused much controversy. In the past, prison-made goods have been sold to outside agencies, but as the rates of prison pay are less than those of ordinary workers in the community, the cost of prison goods is less than that of goods privately made, and as a result private manufacturers sometimes lose business. Federal laws have been passed in recent years, which permit states to restrict the sale of prison goods. It is a pity if work done by prisoners as part of a reformation process cannot be sold and so allow wage payments to prisoners to be continued, and permit the prison to be run on a self-supporting basis. The problem has not yet been satisfactorily solved, although much useful prison work can be done for sale to public institutions within each state. 

Juvenile delinquents are given special care and attention by the courts.

Since most adult criminals begin their career of delinquency and crime before they reach maturity, if it is possible to reform young delinquents, a greater number of potential crimes will be obviated. 

Final habits are not formed until adult life, and when young people

Courtesy The Boys Town Times 

A group of Boys Town citizens planning their activities. The boys are allowed a great measure of self-government under the guidance of adults. acquire bad habits, they can be helped to change them more easily than if they were older.

Juvenile delinquent is the term usually given to a delinquent under the age of sixteen or eighteen. Special courts, judges, and probation officers are usually provided for these young people. If possible, an attempt is made to adjust the child to normal behavior by placing him on probation. Special probation officers then make it their duty to be real friends of the child, to visit him regularly, and to do everything possible to help him. When the delinquent will not respond to this treatment, or when home conditions are not satisfactory, he is placed in a special reformatory school, usually run on the colony plan. The best known of these reform institutions is "Boys Town," because its work has been dramatized in motion pictures and exhibited throughout the country. 

Delinquents older than juveniles, but under twenty-one years of

age, are often treated separately as "young persons" and are sent to reformatories or placed under the guidance of special probation officers. 

The St. Vincent de Paul Society, the Knights of Columbus, the 

Catholic Women's League, and similar societies help Catholic juvenile delinquents in most cities, by providing "Big Brothers" and "Big Sisters" who act as probation officers, visit Catholic inmates of prisons and reformatories, and generally do all that a real elder brother or sister would do for an erring younger member of their family.

Unit Five NATIONAL ORGANIZATION 

The people of the United States of America form a nation which is different from other nations. Within one hundred and fifty years it developed from an insignificant colony to the stature of a great Power among the nations of the world. This tremendous development is unparalleled in history, and it is evident that problems have arisen which no other country has experienced in quite the same way. In this unit, therefore, we shall discuss some of the problems of our population development: immigration, and the racial minority groups who live among us, as an integral part of our nation. We shall also discuss.some of the general features of our American national way of life, so that we shall have a better appreciation of what it means to be an American citizen. 345

Drink mor Mill 

No other country is composed of people of so many different nationalities as our own. These little children-some of them of Chinese blood-salute their flag.

Chapter Nineteen IMMIGRATION AND ITS PROBLEMS The purpose of this chapter is: 

1.

To know the main facts of the history of immigration to the 

United States. 

2. To realize the important place of immigrants in our national culture and for our economic well-being, both in past years, and at the present time. 

3. To realize the social problems which are in immigration, and the need of so organizing our national life that immigrants become Americanized without depriving us of the benefits of the good features of their varied former culture. 

Many nationalities compose the American nation. 

No other country in the world is composed of people of so many different nationalities as our own. We all know that North American 

Indians were the first inhabitants of our continent, probably coming here from China by way of the Bering Straits. There are certain indications that Norwegians were the second people to arrive and that they even penetrated as far inland as the present state of Minnesota. We know little about them, however. Other people are sure that Negroes came here from Africa long before Columbus' time, and settled down as free people.¹ Then Christopher Columbus rediscovered the continent in 1492. People came from Europe to various parts of what is now the United States, to colonize the country. The East was settled chiefly by the Dutch, the French, and especially the English. The French were the chief colonizers 

¹Cf. Weiner, L., Africa and the Discovery of America; also The Negro Year 

Book, 1931-32 edition, p. 305. 347

NATIONAL ORGANIZATION 

of the Southeast, and the Spaniards of the South and West. A certain number of free Negroes also came to our shores in these early times (not all the Negroes in the United States are descended from slaves). Negroes were brought as slaves to the West Indies as early as 1511, but the slave traffic in the United States did not begin until the seventeenth century. In the later history of the United States, it was the Eastern colonizers who spread westward, and whose culture came to dominate this country. Since English people, with their customs and institutions, were in the majority in the East, English culture forms the basis of our own, although, of course, it has been considerably changed in the course of time, especially because of the varied cultural backgrounds of later immigrants. As the country opened up, immigrants from all nations in the world. came here.

Immigration began in the eighteenth century. 

The first peoples who came to the United States from Europe were colonists rather than immigrants. Some of them came because they were dissatisfied with their own country and had to leave it on account of religious or political persecution. Nevertheless, they retained their nationality, and were chiefly concerned with developing the colonies for their mother countries. Later, immigrants arrived.

In the eighteenth century large numbers of people of Scottish- Irish descent migrated from Northern Ireland because of dissatisfaction with the rule in their own country. As most of the territory east of the Alleghanies was in possession of the English at the time of their migration, they settled slightly farther west. 

In the eighteenth century also, large numbers of Germans migrated from Germany to our shores, and settled in what are now the states of the Carolinas, Maryland, and Pennsylvania. These were the ancestors of the people now erroneously known as the "Pennsylvania Dutch," and of certain German stock in other states. 

At the time of the establishment of the Republic of the United

IMMIGRATION 

349 States, 80.7 per cent of the population was white, and of these 82 per cent were of English or Welsh descent. 

Many Negroes were then in the country. Numbers of slaves were brought from the Barbadoes to South Carolina at the end of the seventeenth century, to cultivate such crops as sugar, tobacco, rice, indigo, and cotton, work for which they were better fitted physically than the white man. The slave traffic continued all through the eighteenth century, but the biggest impetus to slave importation took place after Eli Whitney had invented his cotton-ginning machine in 1793, which permitted the spinning of American shortfibered cotton. Cultivation of cotton then began on an enormous scale and with it the demand for slaves. In 1790 there were 575,171 

Negroes in the United States (of whom 59,557 were free) out of a total population of 3,929,214. In 1860, the number of Negroes here was 4,441,830, of whom 448,070 were free.²

The great influx of immigrants into the United States took place in the nineteenth century.

The number of immigrants who came here since the early nineteenth century is as follows:

NATIONAL ORGANIZATION 

Irish people first came to the United States in large numbers at the beginning of the nineteenth century. The largest Irish migrations, however, were at the time of the potato famines of 1845 and 1882. They chiefly settled along the Atlantic seaboard. 

In the nineteenth century, too, a further influx of Germans came to our shores. Most immigrants came because of their poor economic status, but these Germans were chiefly people of education and were skilled in commerce or in one or other of the trades. They settled chiefly in the Middle States. 

The gold rush of 1849 brought large numbers of Chinese to the Pacific Coast about that time. These belonged to the poorest classes in China, and so had a very low standard of living. 

In the 1880's a number of people from Scandinavia (Norway, Sweden, and Denmark) migrated here, settling chiefly as farmers in Minnesota and the Dakotas, or going into the lumber industry. 

After 1880 there came a change in the composition of immigrant groups in America. Thereafter, immigration from the northwestern countries of Europe declined, and large numbers of Italians, Slavic peoples, Greeks, and Jews came from southern and central Europe. 

These peoples have been classed as belonging to the "new" immigration. In 1913 alone there were more than 800,000 immigrants from Russia, Austria-Hungary, Italy, and Greece. Most of these later immigrants were unskilled, and settled in the centers of dense population in the East, intensifying our problems of urban life. 

Some of the Slavic peoples took up farming, however, and a fairly large number of Italians went to California to engage in fruitgrowing there.

Since the depression years of the 1930's, immigration declined so greatly that during the four years 1932-36 many more people emigrated from the United States than came here to stay. From 

1933 onwards, however, the National-Socialist government in Germany began its campaigns against Jews, and it also affected a number of Christians either because of their religious beliefs, or because they did not belong to the Nazi political party. From 1933 onwards,

IMMIGRATION 

351 therefore, an increasing number of Jews and Christians left Germany and came to the United States as refugees, followed later by other peoples who came from German-occupied territory. In 1940 somewhat under 2000 British children also came as refugees. Most of them stayed with relatives or friends who wished to save them from the effects of a possible German invasion of Great Britain.
