Chapter Twenty-Two INTERNATIONAL SOCIETY The purpose of this chapter is: 

1. To show the essential need of an international society of nations. 

2. To show that such a society is a fundamentally natural society, and to discuss ways and means whereby we can help to bring such a society into existence. 

3. To point out the evils of war, but to learn when a war may be just and necessary. 

4. To learn the causes of war, so that we may discuss how they can be avoided in the future.

It is natural for man to form separate States, but excessive nationalistic feeling within these States is undesirable, for States with their citizens, are interdependent. 

We showed in Chapter Five that the State is a natural society, and is a harmonious cooperation of individuals and families within definite territorial divisions, fixed by natural boundaries, by custom, or by long-established conquest. Whenever men have congregated in groups, they have had their own local interests and needs, they have formed their distinct culture, and in the course of time have come to acquire a certain uniformity of racial characteristics and of language. Because of this homogeneity of race and culture, the men who form State groups have distinct feelings of "belonging" to the group, and as we said in Chapter Five, patriotism, the true and genuine love of man for the country of his birth, is a highly desirable and admirable trait. This implies, of course, that the government of his State does not impose conditions which are intolerable and against the natural moral law. As 407

INTERNATIONAL ORGANIZATION 

Pope Leo XIII said: “The natural law enjoins us to love devoutedly and to defend the country in which we had birth, and in which we were brought up, so that every good citizen hesitates not to face death for his native land." 1

The Catholic International Union of Social Studies has given us a worth-while statement on this subject: "The national sphere is for a man a natural one, which in its complete form, offers him at the same time an extension of the domestic sphere on a spiritual, cultural, ethnical, and territorial basis. It exercises on him an educative and stabilizing influence which is of great value in the development of the individual. The natural rights enjoyed by nationals and national groups are founded on this service rendered to the individual.” "12 Yet Pope Pius XII has shown us clearly the true nature of patriotism in connection with our relations with other nations. Peoples who harbor feelings of hatred or of resentment to other nations are not true followers of Christ, for he says: "Legitimate and wellordered love of our native country should not make us close our eyes to the all-embracing nature of Christian charity, which calls for consideration of others and of their interests in the pacifying light of love."

Moreover, we must not forget the fact that just as individuals and families need the cooperation of others, and unite into varied social groups and into the State group, so also no State can exist as an isolated unit in the world. Each State is but one of many, and none is self-sufficing. The higher the civilization of the peoples of a State, the greater the number of their needs, and the more do they depend upon the products of other States. Even if one excludes the economic phase, each nation has its own individual quota to contribute to the common needs of man, in the arts, in the sciences, 2 

¹Pope Leo XIII, Sapientiae Christianae. 

"Nationality: Its Place in the Law of Nations," Catholic Mind, February 

22, 1932, p. 86.

Pope Pius XII, The Function of the State in the Modern World, p. 21.

INTERNATIONAL SOCIETY 

409 and in general culture. Any nation which refuses this cooperation, hinders the development of mankind in general, and is surely blameworthy. Imagine the world's loss if no one could take advantage of the benefits of travel and intercourse with those of other nations. 

Imagine conditions, too, if each nation maintained a strict monopoly on the inventions of its citizens. The people of the United States would not enjoy such a rich culture as at present if France had kept the work of Pasteur as a closed secret from us; if Canada had retained exclusive use of insulin; if Italy had denied us the benefits of her art, her music, and of Marconi's invention of the radio; if England had monopolized the many engineering and electrical contributions of her citizens, among her other inventions and contributions to world culture; if Germany had kept strict guard over the products of the brains of her great musicians, chemists, and writers of the past.

International society is a natural society because of man's aptitude and propensity for it and his need of it. 

International society means an amicable federation of the nations of the world, each maintaining its entity, but each working not only for its own good but for the good of all the others. It means a society of States, not a fusion of existing States into one world State as would exist in a cosmopolitan society. Just as man's family life should not suffer by the fact that he belongs to civil society or the national State, so neither should the national State suffer if it belongs to a federation of States. The State exists for the good of the families and the individuals which compose it, as we have many times stated. So a society of States is needed for the good of the member States.

That nations are interdependent, and are intended by God to belong to international society, may be proved in many ways. In other words, international society, or a family of nations, is a fundamentally natural social institution, even though it does not as yet exist.

INTERNATIONAL ORGANIZATION 

a) From the viewpoint of religion, from the supernatural outlook, man was clearly intended to live in world harmony. 

It is a fundamental concept of Christianity that God intended all men to be united in brotherhood, in the enjoyment of the common Fatherhood of God, as part of the Mystical Body of Christ. 

We are all redeemed by Christ, and we are all intended for the same eternal destiny. It is because of this human brotherhood that there should be a human solidarity in the world, not disunity and conflict. St. Augustine said of the Catholic Church: "Thou joinest together, not in society only, but in a sort of brotherhood, citizen with citizen, nation with nation, and the whole race of men, by reminding them of their common parentage." 194 b) The oneness of the human race, and the obligation of nations as well as individuals to abide by the one moral law, shows man's aptitude for, and need of, international society. 

We have already discussed the "oneness of the human race" in the preceding chapter. We all form part of the one human race, no matter what be the differences of our color or our culture. As individuals, we are all bound by the natural law of morality. Obviously, people do not lose their obligation to be moral merely because they are grouped together collectively in a State. So States, which are merely groups of people organized under a government, must adhere to the natural law when they deal with other groups of people organized under different governments. In other words, when nations deal with one another, they must observe the moral law, they must be just, and they must be charitable. If justice and charity in national dealings were strictly observed, truly harmonious international relations would exist, and almost inevitably would result in an organization of the different nations, for the common good of all, within an international society. 4 

Quoted by Pope Leo XIII, encyclical Immortale Dei.

A chart of the population and resources of the leading nations of the world. No one nation is entirely self-sufficient; each depends to some extent on the others. c) Man has a social, cultural, scientific, and economic need of mutual helpfulness among nations. 

We can easily see that man's need of social life extends beyond the narrow circle of his family, his work group, and the other local groups to which he belongs; and that it extends, too, beyond the State of which he is a member. As we mentioned in an earlier paragraph, no State is self-sufficing, but gains many cultural and other advantages, as well as economic help, by sharing its resources with others. With improved methods of transportation each State is enabled to share its economic advantages with others, and to participate in the pre-eminence of other States. Despite the barriers which have been placed against international trade within recent years, it is clear that international trade has much value. We in the 

United States would not have coffee, rubber, silk, tin, nickel, and

INTERNATIONAL ORGANIZATION 

many other things if other nations did not send them to us in trade;5 and these other nations would be poorer if we did not send them our goods too. We owe much to the other nations which buy our goods (for they thereby give work to our people) and we also owe much to the nations which sell us the goods which we need. World improvement in health and sanitation also requires cooperation between nations by international agreement as to laws regarding health, the sale and use of narcotics, and other measures to stamp out disease and vice. True development in a spiritual way also requires the help of others. d) Man has an aptitude for international society, and despite the wars which have arisen, an inclination for peace among the nations of the world has been evident for several hundreds of years.

Until the growth of travel facilities, people did not realize to the fullest extent their need of peaceful intercourse with the citizens of other nations. For centuries, however, the right and need of States to make treaties with one another, and hence their essential equality and sovereignty, have been recognized. The efforts of the League of Nations, and of the Pan-American Union prove that this desire has been inherent in most nations. Since God intends all men to live in charity and justice, with His grace we can accomplish this.

The effects of war are always disastrous and far reaching. Future wars must be prevented.

When a war is just, then it is the duty of the citizens of the country waging the just war to do what they can to bring it to a successful issue. Individuals who perform deeds of heroism when waging a just war on behalf of their country, or to vindicate wrongs "For an account of the dependency of the United States upon trade with other countries, see "Defence Economy of the United States: An Inventory of 

Raw Materials," Foreign Policy Reports, November 15, 1940. A short list of products needed from abroad is to be found in Ross, E. J., Fundamental 

Sociology, p. 273.

INTERNATIONAL SOCIETY 

413 

Official Navy Photograph 

A scene of destruction in World War II. War, even when just, is wasteful of men and property, and is a builder of hatred and revenge. done to other countries, are deserving of being regarded as heroes and should be accorded the highest honor and praise. Yet there is no romance about war. One must remember the numbers of men who are killed in war; and those who are maimed or crippled for life, or diseased through poison gas and are rendered incapable of earning a livelihood for the rest of their days. One must remember, too, those men who may return in apparent good health, but who are deprived of the physical and mental endurance they once had. 

Added to this, one must take into account the mental anguish of those whose relatives and friends are fighting or in danger of death and injury by attacks from the air. Children in countries which are at war are frequently weakened by lack of adequate food, and by

INTERNATIONAL ORGANIZATION 

the mental strain; their education is often necessarily neglected during wartime. War brings with it, too, the irreparable loss of many of the world's choicest buildings, libraries, and art treasures. 

It gives reign to man's passions and fosters emotionalism and national hatreds. The financial cost of war is so great that a nation may be permanently impoverished in modern warfare. Indeed, the bad effects of war are so great that for self-protection, if for no loftier motive, the nations of the world ought to devise an organized means whereby it may be avoided in the future. 

Despite the bad effects of war however, when discord has taken place, it may be a nation's duty to intervene in a just conflict. 

Lack of world federation leads to wars, the direct causes of which may be many and varied. 

Where organized relations between nations are lacking, war seems to be the inevitable result. So many factors enter into the cause of wars that it is impossible to summarize them except in a general way. However, we find that most wars of modern times have been attributable to one or several of the following reasons, and Pope Pius XII has pointed out to us the two root causes of most of the world's international troubles, and these we shall give 

1. "Forgetfulness of that law of human solidarity and charity which is dictated and imposed by our common origin and by the equality of the rational nature in all men, to whatever people they belong, and by the redeeming Sacrifice offered by Jesus Christ on the Altar of the Cross, to His Heavenly Father on behalf of sinful mankind." Having thus given us the first cause of the present international friction, Pope Pius XII shows how this forgetfulness of Christian charity has led to ideas of excessive nationalism, so that some nations have exalted themselves above other nations, despite the fact that all races are equal in the Church. 

The pride of excessive nationalism, such as was fostered by 

"Pope Pius XII, The Function of the State in the Modern World, p. 16.

INTERNATIONAL SOCIETY 

415 Nazism in Germany and Fascism in Italy during the 1930's, sometimes makes a nation intolerant of others, regarding them as inferior and breeding a hatred of other States and an excessive sensibility to supposed hurt to national honor. Excessive nationalism is a fault which all peoples need to avoid if future wars are to be prevented. 

2. The error of the totalitarian State. The second basic error pointed out by Pope Pius XII as the cause of the difficulties of the late 1930's and after, is the error of the totalitarian State idea as expounded by German Nazism, Italian Fascism, Russian Bolshevism. As the Pope shows: "It is the error contained in those ideas which do not hesitate to divorce civil authority from every kind of dependence upon the Supreme Being - First Source and absolute Master of man and society and from every restraint of a Higher Law derived from God as its First Source. Thus they accord the civil authority an unrestricted field of action that is at the mercy of the changeful tide of human will, or of the dictates of casual historical claims, and of the interests of a few." In other words, wars are caused by belief in a mistaken philosophy that the State is an end in itself and is not bound by the moral law: that it can commit acts (for example, the seizure of property of other nations, or the invasion of a nation's territory without any just cause) which would be wrong if done by an individual but which, by reason of the State's supposed sovereignty, is permissible to the government of a people. Obviously, since a State is merely a group of people organized to secure the benefits of civic society, no ruler who governs in the name of the people can do in their name acts which would be immoral if these were performed by the individuals themselves.

Because the various nations should conduct their relations with other nations in an honorable way, they should respect the international law which has been developed, and they should also rigidly 'Ibid., p. 22.

INTERNATIONAL ORGANIZATION 

respect the solemn pacts they have made by treaties. Pope Pius XII teaches us clearly what must be done in this connection. Of international law, he says: "It is indispensable for the existence of harmonious and lasting contacts and of fruitful relations, that the peoples recognize and observe these principles of international natural law which regulate their normal development and activity. Such principles demand respect for corresponding rights to independence, to life and to the possibility of continuous development in the paths of civilization; they demand, further, fidelity to compacts agreed upon and sanctioned in conformity with the principles of the law of nations." 118 The Pope gives a clear exposition of how treaties are to be regarded: "It is true that with the passage of time and the substantial change of circumstances, which were not and perhaps could not have been foreseen in the making of a treaty, such a treaty or some of its clauses can in fact become, or at least seem to become, unjust, impracticable, or too burdensome for one of the parties. It is obvious that should such be the case, recourse should be had in good time to a frank discussion with a view to modifying the treaty or making another in its stead. But to consider treaties on principle as ephemeral and tacitly to assume the authority of rescinding them unilaterally when they are no longer to one's advantage, would be to abolish all mutual trust among States. In this way, natural order would be destroyed and there would be seen dug between different peoples and nations trenches of division impossible to refill." 

3. Economic instability and need is also a cause of some wars. 

Again, Pope Pius XII shows us a correct light on the matter, when he says: "For true though it is that the evils from which mankind suffers today come in part from economic instability and from the struggle of interests regarding a more equal distribution of the 

Ibid., p. 30. 'Ibid., p. 31.

INTERNATIONAL SOCIETY 

417 goods which God has given man as a means of sustenance and progress, it is not less true that their root is deeper and more intrinsic, belonging to the sphere of religious belief and moral convictions which have been perverted by the progressive alienation of the peoples from that unity of doctrine, faith, customs, and morals which once was promoted by the tireless and beneficent work of the Church."10

The economic struggle offers several reasons for war: a) The desire for territorial expansion, when the population has increased considerably, and the man-land ratio has become acute. 

Such an economic difficulty should be settled by international conferences, not by war. b) The struggle for the control of raw materials, since the more developed a nation becomes the more raw materials it finds necessary for its economic well-being, and no nation possesses all the varieties sufficient for its needs. This desire for goods brings about friction and antagonisms. c) The struggle for markets as an outlet for excessive production consequent upon the lack of agreement among nations regarding foreign trade. This desire for a market for goods leads not only to a desire to possess other territory but to the erection of unreasonable trade and tariff barriers. d) Excessive trade and tariff barriers, erected by nations to foster their home industries. Import tariffs are taxes imposed upon goods brought into the country from abroad. Occasionally they may be necessary because of a need to foster some particular industry. 

Where they are established merely to keep out goods produced more cheaply abroad, citizens have to pay higher prices for goods made in their own country and thus their purchasing power is lessened. This means that they buy less other goods, which creates unemployment in other trades or industries. It also means that people in other nations will not buy the goods of the country which 

10 Ibid., pp. 33-34.

INTERNATIONAL ORGANIZATION 

first imposed a tariff against their goods,¹¹ and so in the long run the tariff-imposing country gets less trade and the tariffs lead to unemployment and economic distress. Of course, where high import tariff already exists, it may be necessary to reorganize industry somewhat before they are taken away. For example, new industries might be established in the neighborhood and financed by government loans or gifts, or persons temporarily unemployed might be paid a "dismissal wage." e) The importance to a country of controlling international means of communication, shipping, railroad and cable facilities, and the like sometimes lead to wars. f) Financial investment of one country in another, whether the loans be made directly by the government of that country, or to manufacturers, transportation companies, public utilities, and other enterprises. This gives the lending nation an excessive interest in the affairs of the other. It likewise affords the latter an undue incentive to declare war in the hope of the cancellation of its debts in the event of victory. 

4. The possession of excessive armaments also leads to wars. 

Each nation has the right and the duty to protect its citizens against unjust aggression from the outside and from internal disorders, and may even conscript men for military service. Nevertheless, international rivalry in providing armaments and an army usually leads to each nation fearing lest one of them become stronger, and hence leads again to further investment of the country's resources in arms. 

Thus a vicious circle is formed, for which there would appear to be no remedy but universal and mutual consent among the nations for proportionate reduction of arms. This will be impossible, however, so long as totalitarianism of any sort exists in the world. In 

1940 and 1941, the semidefenseless plight of the United States in "If a nation does not itself buy goods from the countries with which it deals, the other countries must pay in gold. As there is a limit to the possibility of this one-sided trade, sooner or later it will cease if reciprocal trade cannot be established.

INTERNATIONAL SOCIETY 

419 the face of world upheaval, for example, was due to the sincere desire of our people for peace, forgetful of the fact that ruthless aggression cannot always be prevented merely by the desire that it should not be made. 

5. The lack of a positive conception of the obligations which the solidarity of the human race imposes upon all nations. 

A sincere desire for peace has led many of our citizens to be Isolationists. Generally speaking, however, their ideas have been based upon false premises, and we ought to consider the facts. First of all, the United States is not self-sufficient, as some people wrongly imagine. A glance at a list of goods which we need from abroad will confirm this fact, and also a consideration of the need which we have of foreign trade, as discussed above. More important still, however, is the fact that if we realize the meaning of human solidarity, we shall discover that we cannot consider ourselves as isolated. We are not isolated. We are members of the family of nations, having duties to uphold right and to punish wrong, in so far as it is within our power to do so, and when this is necessary for the common good of the other peoples of the world. 

The Catholic International Union of Social Studies made a state ment which merits serious examination, viz.: "Neutrality, which considers war as other people's business, is the very negation of that solidarity which must unite nations in the common defence of justice and international order. Whether it be an admission of failure, prudent abstention, or selfish move, neutrality is always an inglorious shift; it should not find place in a well-organized society of nations which denounces an unjust war as a crime against humanity and mobilizes against it the repressive power of all States."12

Against this view of neutrality, some people argue that one cannot nowadays decide whether or not a war is just, because of propaganda and mixed motives. So far as propaganda is concerned, 12 

A Code of International Ethics, p. 105.

INTERNATIONAL ORGANIZATION 

POPES ENCYCLICALS 

Her

The New World 

Blueprints for tomorrow. By showing how Christian social principles must motivate all our social relations, and our planning for a better organized social life, the popes laid down the basis for a decent world social order and for lasting peace. intelligent people ought to be able to form their own opinion about reported facts which they know to be substantially correct, and to distinguish between facts and the mere expression of opinion. Moreover, if the peoples of a State choose their rulers wisely, they ought

INTERNATIONAL SOCIETY 

421 to trust the leadership of those who have proved themselves to be honest and trustworthy. As for mixed causes of war, it should not be too difficult to discover which side is, on the whole, right, and which is wrong as in the second World War. Of course, just as extreme isolationism is wrong, so too is war mongering or interventionism of an unnecessary type. No nation should enter into a war of intervention without weighing the evidence of facts as regards justice, and without seeing clearly the moral and practical need of such a serious move.

A war may sometimes be just, but only in certain restricted circumstances.

There will always be a danger of war until nations have come to a universal agreement for the reduction of armaments to a minimum, and until man has set aside his selfish pride and greed and indifference, and has come to a sane realization that peace and prosperity can be attained only by a true community of mind and spirit among all peoples: each individual citizen working for the good of his State; each State looking beyond its petty nationalistic aims to the well-being of the community of nations. It is well, therefore, to set down the conditions of a just war. 

1. There must be a just cause an actual and morally certain violation of right by another State. 

2. All peaceful means to avoid war must have failed or be impossible. 

3. There must be a reasonable hope of success that the people may not be made to suffer from the war without any prospect of good deriving from it, whether present or future. 

4. The evils involved in the war must not be greater than the evils sought to be averted. 

5. The sovereign authority must declare the war, and he must have a right intention. However just the cause, if a nation had subsidiary ideas of unjust aggrandizement, or other wrongful benefit accruing to the victor, the war would, in this aspect, be immoral.

INTERNATIONAL ORGANIZATION 

It may be mentioned, too, that at the termination of a war, the victor may exact compensation for losses he has unjustly suffered, but he should be moderate in the prosecution of his rights. He may not aim at destroying the opposing nation, and he may take punitive measures to subjugate the enemy only in so far as these are clearly necessary as a precaution against future assault-where, for instance, the enemy clearly shows an attitude of permanent hostility and a resolve to renew attack at a future time. 

Within recent years many efforts have been made to promote peace.

The League of Nations, founded on January 10, 1920, is the most direct attempt that has existed to bring international society into being. It aimed at avoiding warfare by bringing the nations of the world together, through their representatives at the headquarters of the League in Geneva, Switzerland; by drawing up a code of international law; and by the establishment of the World Court to the settlement of disputes by arbitration. The idea of the 

League was that of President Woodrow Wilson, but the question of the United States joining the League was made a major national issue. Most Americans opposed it because they did not see the need of cooperation, and they did not wish to give the League any jurisdiction over the affairs of the United States, even though they were favorable to peaceful settlements of international disputes. 

Germany was not allowed to enter the League until later, and Germany, Japan, Russia and other States eventually withdrew. Despite its weaknesses, the World Court has settled various disputes between nations in a satisfactory manner, and the International Labor Organization (the I.L.O.), referred to earlier in the book as affiliated with the League, though separate from it, has had notable success in improving working conditions in many countries of the world. 

Many other efforts have been made to promote peace since the first World War. The Nobel and the Bok peace prizes, the Carnegie

Association for International Peace, have, in general, helped to

INTERNATIONAL SOCIETY 

423 

Keystone View 

A session of the League of Nations at Geneva. The League was an attempt at international society. promote good will and to eliminate narrow provincialism or parochialism in those who had little opportunity to come into contact with peoples abroad. The Catholic Association for International 

Peace, affiliated with the N.C.W.C. in Washington, issues valuable pamphlets and conducts informative peace meetings. The Pax 

Romana, international Catholic student movement, has also an important place in promoting international fellowship. 

It seems obvious that a league of nations, an organized international society, is essential if true world harmony is to be attained. 

Surely, therefore, all enlightened Catholic citizens will strive to promote the foundation of such a league, one that will eliminate whatever undesirable features the League of Nations of 1920 was found to include. Yet there would seem to be little hope of world

INTERNATIONAL ORGANIZATION 

The statue of the Sacred Heart which overlooks the harbor of Rio de Janeiro. The outstretched arms seem to invite the whole world. peace until the two major roots of international disorder, pointed out by Pope Pius XII, are generally eliminated. First, then, we must uproot ideas of State totalitarianism, wherever they exist. 

Secondly, we must promote international harmony and charity among nations, negatively, by forming true notions of charity toward other nations, and positively, by taking our part in actively promoting the human solidarity of all nations. Pope Pius XII gave

INTERNATIONAL SOCIETY 

425 us five fundamental conditions of a just peace, in a Christmas message to the world in 1939:13 

1. "To assure the right to life and independence of all nations, large and small, strong and weak." 

2. "Nations must be liberated from the heavy slavery of the race for armaments, and from the danger that material force, instead of serving to protect rights, become the tyrannical violator of them." 

3. "To examine the consequences of the gaps and deficiencies of the past," and "the establishment of juridical institutions, which serve to guarantee the loyal and faithful fulfillment of terms and, in case of recognized need, to revise and correct them." 

4. To observe "the real needs and just demands of nations and of peoples as well as of ethnical minorities." 

5. That rulers and peoples should be "penetrated always, more and more by that spirit from which alone can arise life": justice and charity.

If unjust wars arise, we must see that we play our part in putting them down. In the present situation (1941) we must surely hope to promote a just peace, and must be careful to avoid any negotiated peace which would leave the totalitarian States free and able to prosecute other unjust wars of self-aggrandizement in the future.

Appendix | THE POSTULATES OF SOCIOLOGY 

NOTE: In Chapter One we set down certain postulates which a study of man and his social relations from the Christian viewpoint must necessarily take for granted. Since it may be useful to the reader to realize the exact import of these postulates, and to know why these particular truths have been taken as necessary foundations to our study, and not facts of another kind, we give below a brief discussion: first, of the necessity of postulates in general, and then of each of the truths which we have, in particular, postulated for the science of sociology.

A postulate is a truth or fact which is a necessary basis for the study of a subject, but which is taken for granted because it is already proved by other sciences.

To make headway in the advancement of knowledge, most sciences have to take as postulates certain truths or facts which are either evident in themselves or which, having already been satisfactorily established in other fields, it would be superfluous to prove again.

A postulate, however, is not a mere hypothesis or theory. If we use such suppositions as a basis for reasoning or as a starting point for investigation, we must be careful that we verify them before drawing conclusions. Unverified theories in another branch of knowledge must never be taken as postulates, for they would certainly not lead to certitude, and they might easily result in error. 

As with other sciences, there are certain facts or truths which are necessary as a foundation for the study of sociology, and which, as stated in Chapter One, are either self-evident, or can be amply proved if we wish to study them in detail. That we should believe in 429

APPENDIX I 

the particular postulates set forth in this book makes a great difference to our comprehension of mankind, as will be easily understood. 

Many non-Catholic sociologists do not believe in them, and for this reason many of their ideas about man's social relations are false, and often dangerous for man's well-being.

We shall now take each of these postulates in order, and attempt to show why a belief in them is essential if we are to regard man and his social relations in a true light and a correct perspective. 

Proofs will be found in books of philosophy, psychology, ethics, history, and theology.¹ 

1. God exists and created all things, man included. 

To deny God's existence is to deny all purpose in life. We could not understand man and the universe did we not believe that God created all things. There would be no explanation of the world's wonders; no fundamental reason why we should be moral, why we should be self-sacrificing at times for the good of our fellow men; no hope in an eternal reward in heaven to compensate us for pain and sacrifice here below. To refuse to acknowledge God's existence, and that we are created for His honor and glory, would be well calculated to lead us to despair. 'For supplementary reading the following might well be consulted:

Catholic Encyclopædia; articles on Ethics, Evolution, Free Will, Immortality of the Soul, Law— natural, Rights.

Haas, Rt. Rev. F. J., Man and Society (New York: Century Co., 1930), Chaps. 

I, III, IV, V. 

Muntsch, Rev. A., S.J., and Spalding, Rev. H. S., S.J.

, Introductory Sociology 

(New York: Heath & Co., 1928), Pt. 2, Chaps. II, V. 

O'Brien, Sister M. C., O.P., Christian Social Principles (New York: Kenedy and Sons, 1941). 

Ross, E. J., A Survey of Sociology (Milwaukee: Bruce Publishing Co., 1932, now out of print), Chap. II and Appendix I. 

Chap. 7. 

Fundamental Sociology (Milwaukee: Bruce Publishing Co., 1939), Ward, M., and Sheed, F. J., Catholic Evidence Outlines (New York: Sheed & 

Ward, 1936).

POSTULATES OF SOCIOLOGY 

431 2. Man has a spiritual soul which is immortal, hence man has an eternal destiny.

Philosophy proves for us that man has a soul, and that his soul is immortal. The importance of taking these truths for granted in the study of man's social relations lies in the fact that if man had no eternal destiny, then the materialists would be justified in working for merely human standards of happiness, and in striving to better man's condition on earth, the question of his future happiness would be of no account. This false assumption would, of course, lead to many abuses and, in fact, does so at the present time. 

Then again, if man had not a soul, he would be no better than the brute creation; he would have no rights and no duties toward his fellow men. It is argued that he would undertake social obligations in order to preserve harmony in social life. It is inconceivable, however, that all men would follow their nobler yearnings and be moral in this life when, in so many cases, to do so would be against their natural inclinations and entail hardship on them. At least an implicit belief on the part of man in future reward for good done on earth is necessary for social cooperation. And without this implicit belief man would find the inequalities of this present life, with the pain and sufferings that some endure, and the great temporal happiness of others, wholly insupportable. 

3. Man is endowed with a free will. 

Our belief that man has a free will is very important. Many men refuse to consider the proofs furnished by philosophy, and deny man this freedom. They tell us that man has no control over his thoughts, and they take from him all responsibility for his acts, be they good or bad. They presuppose that, given a certain heredity and environment, a man is inevitably bound to become a criminal, or to be a good citizen. If a man does not fulfill his duties, they state that he is not to blame, for undoubtedly his heredity or environment is the cause and inevitably inclines him to disregard those duties. If these

APPENDIX I 

ideas were true, it would be useless to strive to better man's temporal condition, which is the main object of social service; and it would be useless to expect anyone to overcome the bad habits he may have acquired. Both morality and law are built upon the doctrine of free will. Without freedom of will there could be no reward for doing good, and no punishment for crime; there would be no heaven and no hell. We are certainly born with predispositions to good and evil, and our environment usually has some influence on us. As we showed in Chapter Three, however, our conduct, that is whether we are actually good or bad, depends to some extent on our ideals and attitudes, on the habits we have acquired and ultimately on our will; and this psychological influence is more important than heredity and environment. 

4. Man is subject to laws, both physical and moral. 

Every created thing, living and nonliving, irrational and rational, has its own finality; that is, it was created for a particular purpose. 

Everything that exists is willed by God to exist for a particular end. If a thing has no purpose to justify its existence, if God does not will that it should exist, then it would cease to exist. This "divine reason directing in advance the actions and movements of all existing things" (to use St. Thomas Aquinas' definition) is called the eternal law. The eternal law is, therefore, in God. That part of the eternal law in which creation participates, however, is called the natural law, and this natural law may be divided into the physical law and the moral law.

The physical law is the creature's natural inclination (instilled into it by God) to its proper end and act. All the things of nature, irrational creatures, and also the physical side of man, are subject to this physical law. Reason discovers these laws by observation, experiment, and induction; for example the laws of physics, chemistry, astronomy, biology, the law that we need food and sleep in order to live, that certain acts will inevitably lead to definite physical results. 

This physical law may be known by observation.

POSTULATES OF SOCIOLOGY 

433 1 Man, however, is subject to the physical law only on his physical side. He differs from the animal in that he is subject also to the moral law. His reason tells him that certain things are right and others inadvisable or wrong, but he is not forced by a physical law into the observance of that order: he is, owing to his free will, capable of accepting or rejecting it. The moral law, therefore, is the natural law in its application to rational beings; that is, to man. 

Conscience interprets the natural law for us. We are under its sovereign authority, and our mental integrity is impaired if we refuse to do what, after due reflection, is presented to our mind as right, and therefore necessary for us to do. 

God has deigned to reveal this natural moral law to us more explicitly through the divine positive laws of the Ten Commandments, the teaching of Christ, and the laws of the Catholic Church which Christ founded.

To promote good order in society, man has also made positive laws, such as traffic laws, and laws in regard to voting or the payment of taxes. These are human positive laws, and they must always be in accord with the natural law, physical or moral, else we are not bound to obey them.

If we did not believe that man was subject to moral law there would be chaos in the world: certainly no social harmony. 

5. Man has certain rights and duties and these are common to all mankind.

If we are subject to law, we have a duty to observe it; and if we have a duty to observe a law, we have a right to expect that others will not hamper us in the exercise of this duty. We have likewise a duty to respect the rights of others.

A right is an inviolable moral or legal title and claim to have, to do, to demand, to omit, or to acquire something. A duty is a moral obligation to do whatever the law commands and to omit what it forbids. Man's principal rights are to life, liberty, justice, and the ownership of private property; also to live socially, with directive

APPENDIX I 

authority (since groups of men cannot come together in harmony and sustained unity of purpose without a leader and a guide); and the paramount right of man is to perform his duties to his Creator, which means the right to follow his conscience. 

Many subsidiary rights are included under these brief headings, and are discussed in ethics. They are too comprehensive to detail here, but to give a few examples we might state that under man's right to life comes his right to a living wage, and to rest and recreation, in order to maintain life at a reasonable standard of living. 

Under the same heading comes also man's duty not to commit suicide and not to kill another. Under his right to liberty comes his right to fulfill all his duties, his right to his own opinion, if this opinion is not opposed to truth or to the moral law, and his right to marry or remain single, as he pleases. His right to justice demands that his lawful rights be not taken away from him; that he be not unfairly discriminated against on account of color, race, or creed; that he be given a living wage for work honestly accomplished, and that his good name be preserved. His right to truth includes a right to receive a Catholic education in order to have his mind nourished with truth. His right to property denies the tenets of socialism. (See appendix II.) 

In accordance with these rights, ethics teaches us that we have several duties, which may be classed under these headings: a) Toward God we owe the duty of worship, the acceptance of His Will, and the fulfillment of all our religious obligations. b) Toward ourselves we owe the preservation of our life and our health, both physically and morally. We also owe to ourselves the proper utilization of our spiritual and material powers and possessions. We must be jealous of our honor and reputation. And before all else we owe ourselves the duty of self-respect. Self-reverence demands the recognition of the dignity of oneself as a creature of God with an eternal destiny, and of our body and powers as the instruments for the attainment of that destiny. It demands the performance of all our actions as perfectly as possible.

POSTULATES OF SOCIOLOGY 

435 c) Toward our fellow men we owe first, justice, that we do not encroach on their rights; second, we owe charity, according to the divine precept: "Thou shalt love thy neighbor as thyself."2

The importance of recognizing these rights and duties in a study of man's social relations must be obvious. 

6. Christ, the Son of God, established the Church, to which He gave divine authority to guide men in matters related to their supernatural destiny.

These facts are proved primarily by the historical records we have of Christ's life, by His revelation, and by miracles. If a man believes in the divinity of Christ and His Church, he follows their teachings. If he follows their teachings he will recognize the rights and duties of others and be the best type of citizen. 2 'Mark 12:31; cf. also Matt. 22:39.

Appendix || PROPERTY 

NOTE: In Chapter Four we stated that the right of property ownership is one of the most important social institutions, and that it is, indeed, a natural institution. Reasons for this statement are given below.¹

Definitions. 

Property is an object or "good" which one has the stable right to use, retain, or dispose of, subject only to certain prior rights to be discussed later. Ownership is the right to use, hold, or dispose of such a good. Consumptive property (consumption goods) consists in goods such as food and clothing which are used to satisfy some direct want of the user (called the "consumer" in economic language). Productive property (production goods) consists in goods which are only used in connection with producing other goods in other words, they are of indirect, rather than direct, use. Factories and their machinery, or even a simple spade, are examples of production goods.

There are many proofs that man has a natural right both to productive and consumptive property. 

1. There is God's sanction for private property ownership in the two commandments which presuppose its existence: "Thou shalt not steal"; "Thou shalt not covet thy neighbor's house nor his field . . . nor anything that is his." 

2. Since life is man's dearest possession, he must procure the goods necessary for his sustenance, and this may involve storing up goods in the form of productive property to make other goods. 

¹For supplementary reading see, Ross, E. J., Fundamental Sociology, Chap. 11. 436

PROPERTY 

437 3. Acquisitiveness is a human trait, which seems almost universal, and to put forth his best efforts in labor, a man needs more than a bare daily living. By nature he needs an incentive to work, and a motive power to give him courage, and for the majority of men this incentive is most surely provided by the possibility of amassing property. 

4. Man is social by nature, and in exercising his sociability he must live in harmony with his fellow men. To avoid quarrels, to maintain peace among men, and to assure a wise administration of goods and production, stable rights of private ownership seem very necessary. 

5. Man has a right to marry and to found a family, and the family is the most basic of all the social institutions. He therefore has a duty to provide for his family which, on account of the vicissitudes of life, can often be done only by amassing some property for them. 

6. Man has many contingencies to meet, such as accidents, illhealth, old age, the leaving of unprovided dependents at death, and these cannot be well provided for unless a man saves up property for the future. 

7. Man has an intelligence and the right to use his intelligence to foresee the contingencies just mentioned and to provide for them. 

He also has a right to perfect his intelligence and his other gifts by study, travel, and in other ways. This is impossible without some property, unless scholarships or other gifts were procured; but in a socialistic or communistic society only those who appeared gifted at the start would procure the means to continue their self-improvement, and many people develop late, and only after they have acquired much experience. 

8. There is clear evidence that all peoples prefer private property ownership. Primitive tribes recognize private property ownership in many goods, including such intangible goods as property in songs, special medicines, and names; in communistic Russia the system of wage payments had to be changed, so that the more gifted and hard working received higher wages than the rest.

APPENDIX II 

Man's right to the ownership of property is a general right only. The possession of property imposes duties on the owner which, if universally recognized, would largely solve the world's economic problems.

Although man has a natural right to own property, one must not confuse this fact with the idea that he must therefore actually possess goods or suffer an injustice. Man's right to own property is undeniable, but it is a general right and must be made definite by purchase, inheritance, or other just acquisition, or by what is called "first occupancy"; that is, by settling on land which belongs to no definite person, and thus taking possession of it. This latter method of acquiring property is still possible in some parts of the United 

States. 

Even though a man has exclusive right to his own property, this does not mean that his possession is unlimited and without control. 

The state has the right to purchase property, even though the owner may not wish to part with it, provided a just price is paid and the purchase is necessary for the general good of the citizens. Similarly, the State has the right to tax property to provide itself with the necessary funds to carry out its duties, and it may grade taxation so that larger property owners pay a proportionately larger tax. 

Again, not only must property be justly acquired by the owner, but it must be put to proper use. This obligation imposes two duties on the rich the duty of justice and the duty of charity. Man has to account to God for the right use of the property which he possesses, and charity suggests that at least some of the surplus over and above that necessary to maintain himself and his family according to his state of life should be devoted to the benefit of those less fortunate than he. Property may be shared with the needy either by outright donation or by investment, provided in the latter case it serves the common good through adequate wage payments and suitable labor conditions. A wise administration of goods is the chief duty of the property owner.

Many of the world's economic problems have been caused by

PROPERTY 

439 men having lost sight of the fact that property ownership is really a stewardship of goods. They have come to regard property ownership as an end in itself, instead of as a means to an end: attaining their eternal destiny by the right use of the goods allowed to them by God.
